Hindu Law. Adoption—Non-obseryance of formalities among hill vil- 
lagers—Leyirate— Rights of children begotten on brothers 
widow.] 

Where a Khasia villager Bhawan Singh took to live with him a widow 
with her son Jasa and treated the latter as his adopted son, 
though without any of the formalities of adoption prescribed 
by Hindu Law, it was held that Jasa was practically adopted 
by Bhawan Singh ; such adoption is all that takes place in the 
hills and the formalities required by the Hindu Law are never 
gone through, except among the inhabitants of largo towns and 
rich people. Bhawan Singh had been the son of his father by 
the latter’s deceased brother’s widow and it was furthor held 
that it is the custom for brothers to take up with the widows 
of deeeased brothers and the children by such a connection are 
treated as other children of the family so far as outsiders’ 
[rights] are concerned. 

Padua v. Bhawan Singh and others ., So 
Daughters’ inheritance—Gift by widow to daughter and 
son-in-law—Suit by reversioners—Form of decree.] - 

Where a Hindu widéw gifted certain of her late husband’s lands to 
her daughter and son-in-law jointly and the collateral revyer- 
slonors sued to get aside tho gift, it was held by the Commis- 
sioner that the parties being pure Rajputs were subject to the 
ordinary Mitakshara Law and the daughter would succeed in 
preferenco to Collaterals and the suit was dismissed. On refer- 
ence to the High Court by the Local Government, it was held 
that in view of the fact that the gift was partly to the daughter 
and partly to the daughter’s husband, the suit was maintainable 
and a decree should be granted declaring that the gift would 
not bind the plaintifis beyond the donor’s lifetime, but that the 
daughter, if still alive, would havo her rights of inheritance as a 
daughter on her mother’s death. 

Dulab Singh and another v, Musammat Ganga and others .. 

Inheritance by daughter—Claim by alleged distant rela- 


tive.] 
A person claiming as a distant ralative to inherit in preferenes to a 
daughter must prove that he is hoir by Hindu Law, 
Lalu v. Musammat Kura me . 
» Inheritancs ~Daugbter’s rights among Rajputs.] 
Held that among persons of the Gorla Rawat caste, being pure Raj- 
puts, daughters inherit in preference to collaterals .

Hindu Law, Inheritance—Co-sharer dying without natural heirs— 
Succession by coparcenary body.] 

Where the widow of a co-sharer in a village died leaving no aera 
heirs, the body of co-sharers of the village (the panch hissadars) 
were given a decree for the land against defendant, who had 
managed it on behalf of the last male owner. 

Tila v. Bhagat Singh and others eS + $ 19 

Inhoritance—Illegitimate son and daughters.] 

When a panda died leaving an illegitimate son by one wife and two 
legitimate daughters by another wife, the son following the 
ordinary Hindu Law was held entitled to half a legitimate son's 
share and the daughters were held entitled to a house sgaid to 
have been allotted them by their father.“ 

Jharu v. Durga and others ., ve 20 
Inheritance—Illegitimato sons of Brahmans Olaim to 
inherit to collaterals.) 

Held that the alleged local custom under which an illegitimate son 
of a Brahman claimed to succeed to the estate of a collateral 
of his father was not established. 

Radhapati v. Har Kishan .. 5 i il 
__.____—. Illegitimate son—Inheritance by such sons’ among 
Khasia castes—Claim by such son to control alienation by 
father during the latter’s lifetime.] 

Held that though the illegitimate sons of men of Khasia castes in the 
hills inherit a share of thoir father’s estates on the death of 
their fathers, yet their rights in the estate only accrue on the 
death of the father, they cannot claim to control their father in 
his disposition of the property during his lifetime. 

: Karam Singh v, Sher Singh and another : 40 

Inheritance by illegitimate sons of a Brahman Admis- 

sion of right by other heirs.) 

A yillage Brahman died leaving ono legitimate and threo illegitimato 
sons. The legitimate son, Badri Datt, after contesting in muta- 
tion proceedings the joint succossion of his half-brothers to the 
fathor’s landed property, withdrew his objection, On tho death 

- of Badri Datt without issue his four first cousins got the whole 
land recorded in thoir names ; his half-brothers filed this suit for 
declaration of title. Two of the first cousins had admitted thé 
joint ownership of the illegitimate sons in the mutation pro- 
ceedings. It was keld on an appoal by the other two cousins 
that the illegitimate sons wore entitled to their father’s and 
brother’s property. 

Bachi Ram and another v, Tarapati and others

Held that among families of the inferior bisht caste, sons by a

Bachi v. Mahenar Singh 2T 


Hindu Law. Inheritance—Illegitimate son—Admission - Estoppel.] 
Where the son by a lawfully married wife had admitted the right of a 
son by a danti wife to half the father’s estate, and the proprie- 
tors had admitted that the latter son was entitled to half the 
khalkari holding, it was held that the latter were estopped from 
contesting the right of the latter son in a subsequentsuit. The 
court was not prepared, without further inquiry, to accept the 
general principle that in Garhwal the issue by a dhanti wife ' 
inherit equally with the issue of a lawful wife. 
Umrao Singh and another v, Jhagru .. a 23 
Inheritance—Levirate~—Rights of widow—Daughter’s 
inheritance.] > 
Where the widow of an eldor brother lived with the younger brother, it 
was held that after the latter’s decease without male issue ehe 
was not entitled to a widow’s estate in his property but must 
look to her sons (by the elder brother) for maintenance. Held, 
further, that the daughter of the younger brother (by his lawful 
wife) was not entitled to inherit his property, which was decreed 
to the collateral heirs, third brother and nephews. 
Jamnu v. Musammat Manuli and others Fi 24 
Lovirate—Conditions restricting inheritance by ofispring 
of a brother’s widow.] e 
Held that the son of a man by his deceased brother’s widow does not 
inherit his father’s estate unless the father has taken the widow 
to live in his own house and treated her as of his own family. 
Kirpal Singh v. Purtab Singh and others “5 12 
-. Inheritancos—Claim of Sautia Bant (inheritance per 
stirpes) among sons— Evidence to prove such custom.] 
Where a Hindu died leaying sons by different wives and the sons by 
one wife claimed to inherit half the estate by Sautia Bant, it 
was held that the usageand common custom in Garhwal follows 
the usual Hindu Law in favour of B4ai Bant (inheritance per 
capita), and that a valid authority must be proved to vary the 
law in any partcular case. 
Rabi Datt v. Abdai Ram 
: . Inheritance —Sautia Bant]

per stirpes) cannot bè made by the courts.]

in second appeal disagreed with the lower courts’ findings on

Hindu Law, Inheritance~Sautia Bant among sons -- -Tllegitimate 
sons.] 

In a family of Khas-Rajputs consisting of one legitimate son by a law- 
fully married wife and four illegitimate sons by a dhanti wife, 
the legitimate son claimed half the father’s estate by Sautia 
Bant (division per stirpes). Held that the custom of the caste 
was Bhai Bant (division per capita) and the legitimate son was 
only entitled to one-fifth of the estate. 

Nar Singh v. Ram Singh and others 
Saulia Bant—Division made by father during his Me 
time.) 

Whero a Saulia Bant (division per stirpes) among sons was made by a 
father during his lifetime and found to be oorroborated by 
existing possession, it was upheld by the courts. 

Padmu v. Shib Datt and others = 

—————., Claim of Sautia Bant (inheritance per stirpes) among 
sons by different wives—Evidence necessary to prove the alleged 
custom.] 


Held that a written agreement or will or the strongest evidence is _ 


necessary to support a claim to Sautia Bant. 
Ratan Singh v. Sher Singh .. aS a3 
- dethon (the extra portion of the oldest son)—Arbitra- 
tion.] $ “ 

Held that the claim of Jethon (tho extra portion of the eldest son) 
must bo incontestably proved to be tho custom of the individual 
family concerned.

Joint holding—Mortgage by one co-sharer—Partition in 
execution.] 

Where a co-sharer ina joint holding mortgaged his share and the 
mortgagee obtained a decroo for foreclosure, and the lower 
court in execution caused the judgement-debtor’s share to be 
partitioned off and tho docrce-holder placed in possession of it. 
On a suit by anovher co-sharer in the land, this procedure was 
upheld by the Commissioner,

Hindu Law. Joint properby—Sal: of portion by one co-sharer without 
the consent of the other.] 

Where one of two joint Hindu brothers sold his share of the ancestral 
joint property without the consent of the other, the sale was 
declared void on the suit of the lattor. 


Sukhdeo Prasad v. Gauri Datt 


——_——.. Local customs regarding marriage among Rajputs—In- 
heritance--Proper framing of issues—Evidence necessary to 
prove local custom.] 

Held that among the Bhandari caste of Rajputs a local form of marriage 
wherein the Ganeshpuja ceremony is performed, but the Sapta- 
padi and Phera Bhaura are not performed is not a lawful 
marriage, and the son of a woman so married does not inherit; 
in such cases & clear issue must be framed on the specific point 
in issue and the party alleging a local custom in variation of the 
normal Hindu Law regarding marriage must prove clearly that 
the specific custom set up is followed in the caste to which the 
parties belong. 

Fateh Singh v. Gabar Singh.. 


Naik caste—Custom of inheritance -Right of sisters to 
inherit.] 

Held that among the members of the Naik caste sisters inherit their 
brothers’ estates, when the latter die without male issue, in 
preference to collaterals. 

Lal Singh and another v. Musammat Sundar 
Naik caste —Legitimacy.] 

Held that in a community of Naiks it would be superfluous to entor too 
closely into questions of legitimacy; sons of a man by the same 
mother should bo regarded as heirs to each other. 

Diwanu v, Lungi zR oie ae 
Rajwar of Askot’s estate an impartible taluka—Main- 
tenance.} 

Held that the ostate of the Rajwar of Askot is an impartible taluka, 
relatives of the Rajwar are only entitled to maintenance.

suit by a reversioner.

Kedarnath Temple, Right of pandas to enter temple—Right to sue— 
Distinction between gifts and offerings—Right of pandas to 
retain gifts.] : : 

Whore tho rawal of Kelarnwth hvd obbvined an ordor of the District 
Magistrate prohibiting the pan las (tho personal priests of the 
pilgrims) from entering the temple with their clients, and 
further claims tha right to all gifts mido within the temple 
to the pindas, it was held that tha rawil had no right to deny 
access to the temple to the pandas, anil that tho latter were 
entitled to receive within the templo ani retain all gifts made 
to them by their clients and intanied as gifts to them and not 
as offerings for the temple. The exercise of these rights must 
bo subject to the power! ths rawal to miintiin order and 
decency in the temple.

Pre-emption. Ast (principal) village and laga (hamlet)—Co-sharer in 
one but not in the other] : 

Held that for the purposes of pre-emption the ast (principal) village 
and laga (hamlet or offshoot) are one, Where a purchaser of a 
share in the laga was already a co-sharer in the as? village, it 
was held that a co-sharer in the laga had no right of pre-emp- 
tion against him. q

Buildings.] 
Held that, so far as the evidence in this case went,-there is no right 


of pre-emption in Kumaun in respect of houses (as distinct 
from land).

Held thata near relative of the vendor, who was also a co-sharer in the

Rabi Datt and others v, Parja Pati 3 

Pre-emption, Claim by near relative, who was a non-resident co- 
sharer, against resident co-sharer purchaser—Non-ancestral 
land. 

Held that the vendor's brother, who was also a co-sharer in the village 
but was non-resident, was entitled to pre-emption against a 
purchaser who was a resident co-sharer even in respect of non- 
ancestral land. 

Narain Datt v, Bhola Datt and others as 


. Claim by non-resident co-sharer—Record of village 
customs. ] 


Held that a non-resident co sharer, who was related to the vendor 
within the third degree, was entitled to pre-emption against 
a purchaser who was a resident co-sharer of the village but was 
not related to the vendor ; to keep tho family intact is a more 
important object of the custom of pre-emption.than to keep 
undesirable outsiders out of the village community. 
Lachi Negi v. Joga and another e. a

and co-sharer in the village but non-resident—Proof of custom. ]

Before Sir Henry Ramsay, Commissioner. 

NAND RAM or MAUZA OHANDOLRAI, PATTI NADALSYUN, GARHWAL 
DISTRIOT (APPELLANT) v. BHAJAN DATT or DITTO 
(RESPONDENT). 

[Revenue appeal no. 90 of 1861.] 

Hindu Law—Inieritance—Sautia Bant, 

Held that in the absence of a will a partition by Sautia Bant (division per 
stirpes) cannot be made by the courts, 

Tus is one of the various rulings on the subject of Sautia 
Bant (inheritance or partition per stirpes. between families by 
different wives). Sir Henry Ramsay, it appears from the various 
rulings, only allowed this method of division where a written 
agreement or settlement or will was proved, or where such 


division had actually been carried out by the father in his life- . 


time. 

Itis unnecessary to detail the facts of the case. The defend- 
ants-respondents claimed in a partition proceeding that the 
partition should be carried out on the basis of Sautia Bant 
alleging this to be the family custom. 


ORDER By SIR Henry Ramsay, COMMISSIONER. 
In the absence of a will Sawtia Bant cannot be made by the 
courts. The division must be Bhat Bant. I cancel the deputy 
collector’s order aud order a Bhat Bant. 


Before Sir Henry Ramsay, Commissioner.

RATAN SINGH RAWAT or MAUZA KIRSAL TAILI, BADALPUR, GARHWAL 
DISTRICT (ÅPPELLANT-DEFENDANT) V. SHER SINGH or DITTO 
(ResPoNDENT-PLAINTIFE), 
[Revenue appeal no, 91 of 1876.] 
Hindu Law —Inheritance—Claim of Sautia Bant (inheritance par stirpes) among 
- sons by different wives— Evidence necessary to prove the alleged custom. 
Held thata written agreoment or will or the strongest evidence is neces- 
sary to support a claim to Saulia Bant . 


In this case (1) Diwan Singh, father of Sher Singh, plaint- 
iff, was the only son of his father by one wife and (2) Ratan 
Singh and his three brothers were the sons by a second wife. 

Sher Singh sued, claiming through his deceased father for half 
the family estate, alleging that the custom of Sautia Bant 
(inheritance per stirpes) obtained in the family. It appeared 
thatat settlement in 1864-65 Diwan Singh had allowed the 
cultivators of the village Maletha to be recorded as proprietors 
but Ratan Singh had sued and recovered the proprietary title to 
the village. Inthe course of these proceedings Ratan Singh 
had stated that he (with his brothers) and Diwan Singh were 
each entitled to half the village, On the strength of this admis- 

sion, the lower court decreed Sher Singh’s claim to half the 
family property by Sautia Bant, Sir Henry Ramsay reversed 
this decision. 

ORDER By Sir Henry Ramsay, COMMISSIONER. 

w appears that with respondent’s father’s assistance the 

asamis of this village were recorded hissadars at the settlement, 


i 
Í 
f 
i 
: 
; 


Digitization by Afya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


CS) 
On appellant’s filing a regular suit he got a decree for hissadari. 
Plaintiff now sues for half, I sce no reason why he should get 


half when Ratan Singh rescued the hissadari from the asamis, 


and in the absence of a written agreement or will or the strongest 
evidence a Sautia Bant ought not to be givex. I therefore 
modify the order of the lower court and give plaintiff a decree 
for Bhai Bant or fifth share. Each party will pay their own 
expenses.

PADMU MAHAR or Gauan, PATTI PAIDULSYUN (APPELLANT) V. 
SHIB DATT AND OTHERS OF DITTO ( RESPONDENTS). 


[Revenue appeal no. 108 of 1877.] 


Appeal against an order of P. Bhawani Datt Joshi, Extra Assistant Oommis- 
sioner, Garhwal, of the 1st June, 1877. 
Hindu Law~Inher itance—Sautia Bant—Division made by father during his 
© lifetime. 


Where à Sau tia „Bant (division per stirpes) among sons was made bya 
father during his lifotime and found to be corroborated by oxisting possession 
it was upheld by the courts. 


THs case represents a variation of the custom of Sautia 


` Bani. al Deo had three wives and during his lifetime he divided 


up his land, not giving one-third to the offspring of each wife, 
as is usual in Sautia Bant, but allotting half to the oneson of his 
first wife, one-fourth to the two sons of his second wife and one- 
fourth to tho one son of his third wife. 


Padmu, ono of the sons by the second wifo, claimed to inherit 
one-fourth of the whole estate by Bhai Bant, the normal rule, 
but his suit failed. 


Tho facts further appear from the order of the lower court 
which isro produced, 


December 5, 
1877. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(ea) 
ORDER By P. Buawani DATT JOSHI, EXTRA ASSISTANT 
COMMISSIONER, GARBWAL, DATED JUNE |, 1877. 


Issue.—Has there been any division as alleged by Shib Datt 
and ought it, if it was ever made, to stand or ought there to be 
equal partitions into four shares ? 


Defendant.—Shib Datt in support of [record torn] that bis 
father made a parititioa, files a deed, dated the 29th April, 1863, 
purporting to be executed by Lal Deo, the father of the parties: 
According to this instrument he gives to Shib Datt half share of 
all the land in his name and out of the remaining half he gives 
half or one-fourth of the whole to his son Ram Datt and the last 
half or one-fourth of the whole to Padmu (plaintiff) and 
Jitaroo, The family debts and rents to'be liquidated in the same 
proportion, half by Shib Datt and the remaining half by the other 
three brothers between them. On testing the existing possession 
of the parties the court finds them to be as follows :— 


ShibDatt ... ... 105% nalis 

Ram Datt 660 bi 49 » Nalis a 
Total 213 0. 

Padmu and Jitaroo aa BT = 


This will show that Shib Datt’s possession extends to double 
the land occupied by Ram Datt, Padmu and Jitu. Both Padmu 
and Jitu admit that their father, Lal Deo, in order to prevent his 
three wives quarrelling, divided land amongst them and the 
nature of the division thus made proves indirectly the provisions 
of the deed mentioned above ‘The deed itself has been attested 
by three witnesses whose veracity there is no reason to question. 
The division made by Lal Deo, father of the parties, will therefore 
be maintained and plaintiff is at liberty to have the land giver 
_ him and his brother Jitu recorded in their names in the phant. 
This suit is dismissed with costs. 


ORDER sy Sin Henry Ramsay, COMMISSIONER, 
I dismiss the appeal.

Before Mr. C. A. Daniell, ©. S., Senior Memter, Board of Revenue. 
RABI DATT or MAUZA KEORALI, GARHWAL DISTRICT (PETITIONER) v. ABHAI 
RAM OF DITTO (OPPOSITE PARTY). 
[B. O. no. 1144/11 —382, on petition no, 205 of 1885-86.] 


Hindu Law—Inheritance—Claim of Sautia Bhant (inheritance per stirpes) 
among sons—Evidencs required to prove such custom. 


Where a Hindu died leaving sons by difforent wives and the sons by one 
wife claimed to inherit half, the estate by Saulia Bant, it was held that 
the usage and common custom in Garhwal follows the usual Hindu Liw 
in favour of Bhai Bant (inhoritance per capita), and that a valid authority 
must be proved to vary the law in any particular case. 

One Daya Ram, a Brahman, left eight sons: Abhai Ram 
and tivo Others by one wife and Rabi Datt and four others by a 
second wife. AbhaiRam and his brothers claimed to inherit 
half of Daya Ram’s estate by Sautiw Bant, claiming that their 
father had during his lifetime carried out a settlement on these 
lines by a deed of 1873, and that the shares of the sons had 
been entered accordingly by mutation in the revenue records 
in 1875. Thedeed of 1873 however was insufficiently stamped 
and was unregistered. 

The Commissioner decided in favour of the Sautea Bant, 
but the Board in the order reproduced below reversed _ that 


decision. 


a 
ORDER BY Mr. C. A. DANIELL, SENIOR MEMBER. 


The appeal was heard to-day before the parties. Two of 
the appellant’s own brothers, Kashi Ram and Narain, and two 
of the respondent’s party, Abhai Ram and Baij Ram, were 
present, The appellant was also represeated by a pleader. ne 
is urged on behalf of the appellant that the Commissioner 8 
decision is against law and usuge among Hindus, and Brahmans 
in particular, andis against former decisions of the courts 1n 


' this division. “Also that the father could only dispose of property 


in the manner stated by respondent if the property were solf- 
acquired, and not ancestral. 

Also that the partition deed of 1873 is valueless as it is 
not stamped with the proper stamp aud is not registered. Also 
that the Commissioner admits the evidence for tho Bhat Bant 150 


July 6, 
1886. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 6) 
little stronger than that for the Sautia Bant. Ihave had the 
record read and heard the pleader and parties present. 

There seems to me no doubt that the usage and common 
custom in Garhwal is in accord with the usual law, Le. that the 
property would be equally divided between all the sons. prom 
regard to whether any of them are sons of one mother or of differ- 
ent mothers (i.e. Bhai Ban). The arrangement of Sautta Bant 
setup by Abhai Ram and his two own brothers and apparent- 
ly consented to by Hari Ram, son of the other wife, appears to 
be known to [but ? ] is made allowable only when there is some 
special agreement which can legally dispose of the inheritance 
in that manner. Now. in this case there is no legal authority 
for a departure from the usual law of inheritance. Neither 
of the lower courts admit the partition paper as evidence as it is 
palpably inadmissible. The first court decided for the Bhat 
Bant in favour of the present appellant. The Commissioner 
records a judgement as follows: “ The fact of Hari Ram ad- 
mitting that the Sawtia Bant was correct is sufficient to turn 
the scale. ‘The evidence in favour of Bhat Bant is very little 
etronger than the evidence in favour of Sautia Bant; so when 
there it the admission of one interested party that Suutia Bant 
is correct, is must gain the day.” 

If the evidence of Hari Ram had been taken as proof that 
there was a special agreement or disposal of property in legal 


form [his] statement might perhaps be of value, but Hari Ram’s — 


admission in the mutation case in 1875-76 isnot evidence of any | 
such fact. ‘This admission could be only binding against any 

claim he himself might make thereafter. It cannot have any 

effect as to the disposal of the property among his four brothers 

oras regards their individual rights. 

So faras I am able to judge, I do not find that the scale 
is so closely balanced as Commissioner would consider. I judge 
that the usual and common custom follows the law and the law 
must be upheld, unless auy valid authority is proved to exist 
which alters the law in a particular case. 

In the present instance, I find no such authority to exist. 
I would therefore maintain the usual law of inheritance, I 


grant the appeal and I order that the Commissioner’s decree be. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(atlas) 


reversed ; and the decree of the court of first instance be rein- 
stated. 

The respondent here will have to pay the present appellant’s 
costs in all courts. 


p 


Before Mr. H. Ross, Commissioner. 


PADUA (APPELLANT) v. BHAWAN SINGH AND OTHERS OF MAOHOR 
MALLA, LAKBANPUR (RESPONDENTS). 


[First appeal no. 10.] 


Hindu Law—Adoption— Non-observance of formalities among hill villagers— 
Levirato—Rights of children begotten on brothers widow, 


Where a Khasia villager, Bhawan Singh, took to live with him a widow 
with her son Jasa and treated the lattor as his adopted son, though without any 
of the formalities of adoption prescribed by Hindu Law, it was held that Jasa 
was practically adopted by Bhawan Singh ; such adoption is all that takes place 
in the hills and the formalities required by the Hindu Law are} never gone 
through except among the inhabitants of lerge towns and'rich people, 
Bhawan Singh had beon the son of his father by the lattor’s deceased 
brother’s widow, and it was further held that it is the custom for brothers to 
take up with the widows of deceased brothers and the children by such a 
connection are treated as other children of the family so far as outsiders’ 
(rights) are concerned, 

Tars case is interesting as exemplifying the laxity of family 
customs in the hills among the Khasia castes, a point which, it is 
sometimes urged, is not sufficiently proved by specific instances. 
Kalu, the plaintifs great-great-grandfather, had two wives (one 
of them, in all probability, though this is not clear on the record, 
a dhantt, not lawfully married), By the second wife he had two 
sons, Jaman Singh and Madhan Singh, who inherited half his 
property. On Jaman Singh’s death, leaving male issue, his 
widow was taken to wife following the usual custom by Madhan 
Singh and bore him two sons, An Singh and Bhawan Singh, 
Bhawan Singh took to live with him a widow, who had a son 
Jasa by her former husband; Jasa was treated as his own son by 
Bhawan Singh, though none of the formalities of adoption were 
observed. In the present suit Padua, Jasa’s son, claimed to 
inherit the property of Jogia, Jaman Singh’s grandson, who died 
without issue, as against Kalu’s-descondants by his first wifo. 

The Commissioner held that Jasa was validly adopted by hill 
custom and that offspring by a brother’s widow were entitled to 
inherit againsh more remote collaterals, ‘The family tree and

1886.

Law regarding the sons by a brother’s widow and was reversed 
on the basis of local custom,

buton the ground that his father was supposed to be illegi- 
timate, his rights to inherit Jogia’s property ure objected to. 
From the evidence recorded in court and the enquiries made in 
the village, it appeared that after Jaman Singh’s death Madhan 
Singh kept bis widow by whom he got two sons—An Singh and 
Bhawan Singh. Bhawan Singh also kept as his wife a woman 
who came to him with ason named Jasa, whose father was not 
known. But Bhawan Singh adopted Jasa ashisson. Jasa was 
though not formally yet practically Bhawan Singh’s opted 
son. The plaintiff is the son of this Jasa who died prior to 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 9 ) 
the death of Bhawan Singh. On the death of Bhawan Singh 
Padua inherited from him, 

Padua claims to be legitimate, and in support of this he 
states that he became heir of _ his grandfather, Bhawan Singh. 
In defence the defendants urge that he inherited Bhawan Singh's 
property on sufferance. 

First of all it has to be considered whether Padua is entitled 
to get a share in Jogia’s property. In reply to this I hold that 
he is not. According to the evidence, I think, no one denies 
that Padua is not legitimate. But the illegitimate sons can 
inherit only their father’s property under paragraph 467, page 
533 of Macnaughten’s Hindu Law, volume III, and they cannot 
claim any right to any other relation’s property in the family. 
According to this law Padua can never be Jogia’s heir by-reason 
of his illegitimacy. 

I therefore dismiss the plaintiff’s suit and direcb that the 
plaintiff will bear defendant’s costs. 


— 


ORDER By Mr. Ross, COMMISSIONER. 


In this case Jasa was practically adopted by Bhawan Singh. 
Such adoption is all that takes place in these hills; except 
amongst the inhabitants of large towns and rich people the 
formalities required by Hindu Law are never gone through. 

It is also quite the custom for brothers to take up with the 
widows of deceased brothers and the children are treated tho 
same as the other children of the family so far as outsiders are 
concerned. Bhawan Singh would therefore inherit before distant 
relations, his adopted son takes his place, and as appellant is the 
son of the adopted son he should ‘inherit. I therefore 
accept appeal and direct that Padua, Ginua, and Jitua succeed 
to Jogia’s property. Respondents to bear costs. 


Before Mr. H. Ross, Oommissioner.

ISHWARI SINGH or mauza Korgar LOnBA (RESPONDENT-DEFENDANT)

ake, Held that the claim of jethon (the extra portion of the oldest son) must be

In this case Jhagar Singh as the elder son claimed an extra 
share consisting of land paying Rs, 9-11-0 revenue, as his jethon 
(birthright), Ishwari Singh, defendant, denied that it was

by way of jethon. The claim was dismissed by the original

On appeal the Commissioner reversed this decision, 


ae ee ed ae es 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


Geiss) 
ORDER By Mr. J. R. Rein, COMMISSIONER. 

The following curious peints occur in this case :— 

(1) The Commissioner, without consulting the parties, 
ordered the case to be referred to arbitrators, He 
had no legal right to do so, and the order was illegal. 

(2) No provision was made in the order for arbitration fora 
difference of opinion between the arbitrators and for 
the disposal of the case ‘should there be a difference 
of opinion. 

(3) The Extra Assistant Commissioner decided that in 
consequence of the difference of opinion between the 
arbitrators, the case could uot be decided by arbitra- 
tion. But the award of the majority of the arbitrators 
(two) seemed fair, and so he accepted it. It is to be 
noted that both the two arbitrators on one side and 
the single arbitrator on the other, gave good reasons 
for the belief that was in them. 

The claim of jethon must be incontestably proved to be a 
custom of the individual family concerned ; and going back 
to the original decision of the Extra Assistant Commissioner 

` there seem very good reasons for distrusting the justice of this 
claim, I therefore cancel the intermediate proceedings’ begin- 
ning with the Commissioner’s illegal reference to arbitration, 
and uphold the Deputy Commissioner’s decision, dated the 13th 
August, 1887, All costs on respondent,

legitimate son of Tarapati and Radhapati, defendant, son

KIRPAL SINGH DHARKOTI, MAUZA PARKANDI (APPELLANT-PLAINTIFF) 
v. PARTAB SINGH, MOSAMMAT PARBATI, mornek or UMRAO 
SINGH AND MUSAMMAT GAURA, moruer or JITU 
(Responpents-DrrENDANTS). 

[Revenue regular appeal no. 69 of 1890.] 

Appeal against an order of Pandit Dharmanand Joshi, Deputy Collector, 
dated the 6th June, 1890. 

Hindu Law—Levirate—Conditions restricting inheritance by off spring of 
a brother's widow. 

Held that the son of a man by his deceased brother’s widow does not 
inherit his father’s estate, unless the father has taken the widow to livo in his 
own house and treated her as of his own family. 

Tue facts of this case illustrate in a curious way the pre- 


valence of the leyirate custom in the hills. 


LO C—O 


noou 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


CR) 

There were three brothers, Parju, Tejua and Chawanu. Parju 
died without issue and the second brother, Tejua, took Parju’s 
widow to live with him, Tejua had one son, Mangal Singh, 
by his own wife and one son, Bagolia, by his brother Parju’s 
widow. ‘Tejua then died and the third brother, Chawanu, formed 


asimilar connection with Tejua’s widow. Chawanu had one son, 


Partab Singh, defendant, by his own wife, and one son, Kirpal 
Singh, plaintiff, by Tejua’s widow. Tne other parties to the suit 
were the widows of the two sons of Tejua representing their 
minor sons. ‘Ihe plaintiff alleged that one-half of the family 
estate belonged to himself and his half brother, Partab Singh, 
through their father, Chawanu, aad claimed title and possession 
in a half share of this half. 


The lower court found that plaintiff was the son of Cha- 
wanu; that by local custom, if the elder brother’s widow went 
to live in the younger brother’s house as his wife, her children 
by him were considered legitimate -and inherited as such, but 
that if the widow continued to live in her former husband’s house, 
she was regarded as only a concubine of the younger brother and 
her issue by him as illegitimate, and that in this case the 
plaintilf’s mother had never gone to live with Chavanu as his 
wife, but had continued to live in Tejua’s house looking after 
Mangal Singh, her son by Tejua. Plaintiff was accordingly 
held to bo illegitimate and not entitled to ashare in his father 
Chawanu’s estate. 

From this order the plaintiff appealed to the Commissioner, 
The parties were Khasias. 

ORDER By MR. F. GILES, COMMISSIONER. 

Tho firsb plea of appeal breaks entirely now ground— 
throughout the proceedings no mention has bcen made by appel 
lant (plaintiff) of any partition by his father. Ho has claimed 
throughout as heir. 

He does not dispute the existence of the custom mentioned 
by both the deputy collectors that a son of a man by his brother’s 
widow does not become his heir unless he has taken the mother 
into his house and treated her as of his family. 


4ugust 11, 
1891, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 4 ) 


The simple question is then, was appellant’s mother so treated 
by his father ? 

After careful perusal of the record I see no reason to doubt 
the justness of the lower court’s conclusion that she was not so 
treated. The appeal must therefore be dismissed with costs.

LALU BURHAKOTI, mauza TALLI KOLRI (APPELLANT-DEFENDANT) v. 
MUSAMMAT KURA or Gavram (REBPONDENT-PLAINTIFF). 
[Revenue regular appeal no. 44 of 1891-92.) 


Appeal against an order of Pandit Manik Lal Joshi, Deputy Collector, 
Garhwal, dated the 17th February, 1892. 


May 3 Hindu Law—Inheritance by daughter—Claim by alleged distant relative. 
i S . e . . 
1892, A person claiming as a distant relative to inherit in preference toa daughter 
must proye that he is heir by Hindu Law. 


THE facts of the case appear sufficiently from tne judgement. 
It may appear superfluous to reproduce this ruling, but in view of 
the alleged local custom, which is constantly asserted in such cases, 
that daughters and their descendants are completely excluded 
from inheritance in the hills, the judgement is not without 
significance. Mr. Pauw remarks that the right of the daughter 
to succeed even in preference to unrelated co-sharers in the 
village is not generally recognized by the people themselves ; 
and at a meeting of Kumaun pandits held at Almora in connec- 
tion with the compilation of this digest and commentary, no 
less than 13 out of 16 present urged the retention of the 
old principle of the complete exclusion of females and their 
descendants from the line of succession, alleging that the ordi- 
nary Hindu Law on this point Was entirely unknown in the 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(17 ) 


hills, and that rulings introducing the principles of Hindi Law 
were still never followed by the higher classes. The courts 
must therefore expect to find instances where it is contended 
that the daughter is not an heir at all by local custom, In this 
case Lalu was a co-sharer in the village and of the same caste as 
the deceased co-sharer and probably a distant relative. 


od 


ORDER By Mr. D. T. ROBERTS, COMMISSIONER, 
Parties present. 


This is a suit to cancel a mutation of names which the defend- 
ant Lalu had gob made in his favour in succession of Lalmani 
and is brought by Kura, the daughter of Lalmani. 

It appears that after Lalmani’s death a dispute arose in 
1870 A.D. between his half-brother, Jeura, and his widow, Musam- 
mat Lalde, and this was settled by Colonel Garstinin accordance 
with a deed made by the parties of which the effect was that 
Jeura and Musammat Lalde should hold the property on behalf 
of the two daughters of Lalmani. One of these duughters is dead 
and the other is Musammat Kura, the plaintiff in the present 
case, 

In that case Lalu did not intervene or put forward any claim, 


and he says he-did not do this until the death of Musammat. 


Lalde last year. Jeura had died long previously. 

It_is thus clear that the property is in possession of Musam- 
mat Kura who can only be ousted by some one with a better 
claim and by a regular civilsuit, ‘The mutation of names was 
incorrectly made and was not made on the basis of possession. 
Considered as a revenue suit this settles the matter, but the 
lower court has also taken up the civil claim, and on this also 
has decided against; Lalu. 

Obviously it is for Lalu to prove thathe is heir by Hindu 
Law to Lalmani and this he has failed to do. His pedigree is 
not proved by any evidence and on his own showing he is at 
least seven degrees removed in relationship from Lalmani. 

As Lalu’s claim has broken down, because he has proved 
nothing except that he is of the same caste as tho deceased Lal- 
mani, it is unncoessary to go further and decide the general

question as to under what circumstances and with what limitations

MATHURA DATT GURANI, Aumors (APPELLANT-PLAINTIFF) V. (1) MUSAM- 
MAT DHARAM SUNDARI, vanvor, anv (2) SHEIKH KULLEN, VENDEE 
(RESPONDENTS-DEFENDANTS). 
(Civil appeal no. 5 of 1892]. 
Appeal against an order of Colonel G, E, Reade, Assistant Commissioner, 
Almora, dated the 7th June, 1891. 
Hindu Law—Sale by widow—Local custom of applying for permission to sell. 
Held that the local custom under which widows apply to the district officer 
for permission to sell is only a precautionary measure to prevent litigation and 
does not debar a subsequent suit by a reversioner. 
THE facts are sufficiently clear from the court’s order. 
The custom is now obsolete, or practically so, but it is possible 
that instances of it aud pleadings based on it may come before 


the courts still, 


ORDER BY Mr. D, T. ROBERTS, COMMISSIONER. 


This is a suit to cancel a sale by a widow who under Hindu 
Law has only a life interest in the property sold. Jn accordance 
with a custom apparently peculiar to Kumaun, application was 
made for permission te the Senior Assistant Commissioner to sell, 
and after a summary enquiry by the tahsildar permission was 
given, and the coutention is that this permission disposes of the 
case, but the evidence filed relating to this custom shows that 
it is only a precautionary measure to prevent litigation, and the 
permission given only extends to sale of property to pay. debts 
already incurred and for payment of which there is no other 
resource. The custom doos not invalidate ordinary Hindu Law 
but is a matter of executivo usage, It romains open to parties 
interested to raise, by regular suit, the question whether that 
permission was properly given. Now in the present case the 
application for permission only represented debts of Rs. 100 and 
the sale is for Rs. 600, that is to say, far in excess of the actual 
necessities of the case and it is only in case of necessity that por- 
mission is given up to the extent of thab necessity, 


a a 


SOE 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 19°) 


In the second place the widow on the summary enquiry was 
represented as la-waris (having no heir) but it is now admitted 
that the appellant is her heir and will succeed her in ordinary 
course. I therefore find that the widow had no right to sell 
more than her life interest in the property, and I grant the 
appeal declaring the sale null and void as far as the appellant is 
concerned, that is to say, the sale is only a valid conveyance 
for the lifetime of the widow (vendor) and after her death the 
property reverts to her heir. Respondent to pay costs. 


Before Mr. D. T. Roberts, C.S., Commissioner. 


TILA BAGURI or MAUZA MoHANARI, ALMORA DISTRICT (APPELLANT) V. 
BHAGAT SINGH, CHATURA AND otmers (RESPONDENTS). 
[Revenue regular 2ppeal no. 18 of 1891-92.] 
Appeal against order of Mr, H. E, Holme, Assistant Commissioner, Almora, 
dated the 25th November, 1891. 
Hindu Taw —Tnheritance—Co-sharer dying without natural heirs—S uccession 
of village co-parcenary body. 


Where the widow of a co-sharer in a village died leaving no natural heirs, 
the body of co-sharers of the village (the panch hissedars) were given a decree 
for the land against defendant, who had managed it on behalf of the widow of 


the last male owner. 
THE facts of the case appear sufficiently from the order of 


the lower court, which is reproduced below. The case exem- 
plifies the custom whereby in Kumaun the co-sharers of a 
village are reversionary heirs to the share of a co-sharer, who 
dies leaving no natural heirs. The village as a unit is settled by 
Government with the whole body of co-sharers, who are jointly 
and severally responsible for the whole revenue assessment, and 
this reversionary right follows as a corollary from this arrangement. 

The order of the lower court (Mr. M. E. Holme, Assistant 
Commissioner) was as follows :— 

Plaintiffs, the sharers in mauza Mohanari, sue defendant for 
wrongfully having certain land in that village entered in his 
own name, ‘The land in question belonged to one Ramauti, who 
died leaving no heirs, Plaintiffs claim that it reverts to thom 
jointly as the co-sharers of tho village. Dofendant rests his 
title to the land on possession and a decision of the late sadr 
amin, This decision however on being inspected turns out 
to be merely a permission to defendant’s brother to manage 


May 11, 
1892. 


February 11, 
1893. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 20 ) 


Ramauti’s estates during her lifetime, provided her heir, a 
certain sepoy, gave his consent thereto: this sepoy was however 
apparently never heard of again. With regard to the question 
of possession, on the present date to which the suit has been 
adjourned after the framing of issues, plaintiffs produce three 
witnesses to their possession while defendant makes default, 
Judgement for plaintiffs with costs. 


ORDER BY Mr. D. T. ROBERTS, COMMISSIONER. 
Appellant present. 


I concur with the lower court that the appellant has no 
right to this land. Appeal dismissed with costs.

JHARU PANDA or mauza Dion, GARAWAO (APPELLANT-PLAINTIFF) V. 
DURGA anD NATHU PANDAS anp MUSAMMAT BASANTI AND 
MUSAMMAT RAJI or DITTO (RESPONDENTS-DEFENDANTS), 

[Special civil appeal no. 1 of 1893.7 3 
Hindu Law—Inheritance —Illegitimate son and daughters, 

Where a Panda died leaving an illegitimvte son by ona wife and two 
legitimate daughters by another wife, the soa following the ordiniry Hindu 
Law was held entitled to half a legitimate son’s share and the daughters were 
held entitled to a house said to haye been allotted them by their father. 


THE parties to this case were pandas, who are the personal 
priests of pilgrims to Kedarnath aud are not of genuine Brah- 
man caste. The facts appear from the lower court’s report on 
remand The case is only worth noting as applying the 
ordinary rule of Hindu Law to the inheritance of an illegitimate 
son and to that of daughters, who are commonly excluded from 
inheritance by the local custom of the hills. 

Tt appears however from the record that no plea was raised 
or enquiry made regarding local custom in this case, and the 
Commissioner’s order, it will be seen, laid down no definite 
ruling. 

The deputy collector’s remand report was as follows :— 
“This case was remanded for further enquiry and the issues 
were— 

(i) in whose possession is the house in dispute at the 
present time ? 


— 


SL woe 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 21 ) 


(il) if itis nob in plaintiff-appellant’s possession, is he 
entitled or nob to be given possession at once? 


(iii) to whom does the house in question belong at the 
present time ? 


Ist isswe.—To arrive at a satisfactory finding on the issue I 
went to mauza Diuli where the disputed house is situated, in 
person, and there out of a good number of persons collected at 
a distance from court, selected five men, aged and respectable, at 
random, with a view to examine them on the subject in-hand. 
These were accepted by the parties. They unanimously state 
that the house in question, since the girls’ mother, Musammat 
Kali, was married to Pate Ram, had been in her possession 
without interruption, fand that after her demise the girls have 
been occupying it. 


2nd isswe.—The house is not in appellant Jharu’s possession, 
His illegitimacy has not only been established by the evidence 
accepted by the parties, but Jharu himself has in his sworn state- 
ment of the 11th February last admitted that his mother was 
illegitimate, Where there is no legitimate male issue, but only 
daughters, as in this case, the illegitimate son is entitled to half 
of the share of a son, and Jharu cannot therefore obtain posses- 
sion of the house in question, because he has more than his share 
in mauza Paturi in his possession (Dattaka Chandrika, sections 
31 and 32 and Mr. Macnaughten’s work, page 18). Further, 
being on account of his illegitimacy not in a position to offer 
spiritual benefits to his father, he cannot inherit the whole of 
Pate Ram’s estate, so long as his legitimate daughters, Basanti 
and Raji, who exclude him from it, are alive (Dattaka Chandrika, 
sections 31 and 32, and Mr. Macnaughten’s work, page 18). 
Moreover, the evidence acceptel by the parties tacitly goes to 
show that Pate Ram had, while living, made a division of his 
property, by which the house in dispute fell-to the share of 
Mueammat Kali—the mother of the girls—Basanti and Raji, 
and Paturi where he had a house and Rs. 4 revenue paying land, 
went to his remaining two wives. The house in Diuli being 
thus the property of Musammat Kali, cannot go to Jharu’s 


mother, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 22 ) 


8rd issue—-The foregoing shows that the house at present 
belongs to Pate Ram’s legitimate daughters, Basanti and Raji.” 
ORDER BY Mr. D. T. ROBERTS, COMMISSIONER. 
Isee no sufficient reason for admitting a special appeal. 
Rejected,

OHANDRI OF MAUZA MUCHYALGAON, PATTI PAINON, GARHWAL DISTRICT

[Revenue miscellaneous appeal no. 27 of 1892-93.] 
Appeal against an order of Pandit Manik Lal Joshi, Deputy Collector, 
Garhwal, dated the 6th January, 1893. 

April 28, Joint holding — Mortgage by one co-sharer — Partition in execution, 

1893. Where a co-sharer in a joint holding mortgaged his share and the mort. 
gages obtained a decree for foreclosure, and the lower court in execution 
caused the judgement-debtor’s share to be partitioned off and the decree-holder 
Placed in possession of it, on a suit by another co-sharer in the land, this 
procedure was upheld by the Commissioner. 


IN this case Shib Datt mortgaged his -share in an undivided 
family holding to Kalmu. On the latter’s obtaining a decree 
for foreclosure and applying for execution in default of payment, 
the lower court ascertained that Shib Datt’s share amounted to 
280, nalis and deputei an amin to draw up a partition list con- 
taining this area with proper proportions of the various qualities 
of land : the list made was approved by the court. 

Chandri, cousin of Shib Datt, objected and then appealed, 
alleging that this land really belonged to him, ‘The case is 
reported with reference to the custom of a co-sharer’s ability to 
alienate his joint share in a family estate without reference to 
his joint co-sharers, aud also as illustrating the informal proce- 
dure, which used to be customary in such cases in Kumaun. 
The joint holders in this case however were evidently not a joint 
Hindu family. :

Hindu Law—Inheritance — Illegitimate son— Admission —Lstoppel. 
Where the son by a lawfully married wife had admitted the right of a son

that the latter son was entitled to half the khaikari holding, it was held that 


tho latter were estopped from contesting tho right of the latter son in a sub- 
sequent suit. à 


The court was not preparod without further onquiry to accept the general 
principle that in Garhwal the issue by a dhanti wife inherits equally with the 
issue of a lawful wife. % 

In this case the lower court (Pandit Manik Lal Joshi had 
laid down the general rule that in Garhwal the sons of a dhanta 

wife inherit equally with sons by a lawful wife. The case is 
recorded as containing a brief discussion of this local custom 
and another instance of the admitted succession of the son of 
such a connection. ‘The parties were Khasias (Khas-Rajputs) by 
caste. The facts appear sufficiently from the court's order. 
Jamuu, the legitimate brother, since deceased, had admitted 
Jhagru’s right to a share during mutation proceedings, in which 
the proprietors, the plaintiffs, had also admitted his righb toa 
share in the khaikari holding.

1 am not prepared, without further very full and careful 
enquiry, to assent to bho finding of the deputy collector that in

Garhwal the issue of a dhanti wife is entitled to a share equally 
with the issue of a lawful wife. A case has recently been before 
me in which the officer (Pandit Jai Datt Joshi) presiding in 
another revenue court in Garhwal, ruled precisely the reverse * 
and that ruling is in accordance with the ordinary Hindu Law. 

But in this case I find that the deceased Jamnu admitted that 
the present respondent Jhagru was his half-brother—the son of 
his father by a dhanti wife—and that he was entitled to half the 
property of their common father in the khaikari holding on

prietors at the same time admitted that Jhagru was a co-sharer 
in the khaikari. 

The proprietors are, by their admission, estopped from now 
challenging the right of respondent Jhagru. As to their object- 
tion that Jhagru had not paid the sharc of his father’s debts, for, 
which Jamnu stipulated, that is a matter which does not concern 
the appellants, but rests entirely between Jamnu’s heirs and 
Jhagru. 


l see no reason to interfere, Appeal is dismissed with costs,

DEWANU, «Auza MAKU MALLA (APPELLANT-DEFENDANT) V, LUNGI, 
NAUZA PHEGU (RESPONDENT-PLAINTIFF). 
[Rovenue regular appeal no. 19 of 1893-94] 
Appen] against an order of Pandit Manik Lal Joshi, Deputy Oollector, 
Garhwal, dated tho 19th Decembor, 1893. 
Hindu Law—Naik caste—Legitimacy. 
Held thatiin a community of Naiks it would ba suporfluous to entor too 


closely into questions of legitimacy: sons of a man by the same mother should 
be regarded as hoirs to cach othor. 


Tur women of the Naik caste are practically invariably 
brought up to tho profession of prostitution. In this case Lungi 
claimed to succeed to the estate of Rama, deceased, alleging that 


‘they were both sons of Harku by his wife Kali, Dofendant 


alleged that Lungi was the son of Kali by another man and born 
aftor Harku’s death, and that Harku was the son of his (defend- 
ant’s) aunt, a prostitute, hence he claimed the estate. 


ORDER BY COLONEL G. E, ERSKINE, COMMISSIONER. 

I sce no reason to doubt that Rama was in possession until 
his death in 1889. I also concur with the lower court that 
Lungi was tho brother of Rama. Ina community of Naike it 
would be superfluous to enter too closely into questions af legiti- 
macy. By the usual custom of that community both Rama and 
Lungi should be rogarded as heirs of Harku by the same 
woman (Kali), They should be regarded as heirs to each other. 
Lungi has a far better title to succeed Rama (his own brother) 
than Diwanu who can only set up relationship some generations 
back in a left-handed fashion, 

‘Tho decision of the lower court seems to me to be in accord- 
ance with the equity of the case, whethor regard be had to 
possession or relationship, Appeal dismissed with costs,

BAOHI or mivza Basyun (ÅPPELLANT-DEFENDANT) V. MAHENAR SINGH 
(Res eoNDENT-PLAINTIFP). 
[Revonue regular appeal no, 61 of 1892-93,] 
Hinds Law —Inheritance—Lltegitimate sois—Claim to suoceed to collaterals. 
Held that among families of the inferior Bisht caste, sons by a dhanti wife, 
though they may succeed to a share in their fathor’s estate, do not inherié to 
collaterals, 

Tue parties in this case were Bisht Rajputs of tho inferior 
cultivating type; it appears that the Bisht caste, a widespread 
one, includes various grades, some of whom are evidently of the 
Khasia class, Among the lower grades of Bishts it appears 
from this case that sons by dhanti wives inherit a share of 
their father’s property equally with the sons by lawful wives, 
but do not inherit to collaterals, except in the absence of logiti- 
mate descendants. 

The facts of the case appear from the enquiry into local 
custom made on remand by the lower court, which reported as 
follows :— r 

This case has been remanded by the appellate court for 
further enquiry as to whether the common father’s property 
having been divided between the offspring of the legal and 
dhanti wife, aud separate possession having been given, the 
plaintiff descended from the dhanti wife’s son, can claim to 
inherit a share in the property of one of the sons of tho legal 
wife, 

As desired by the appellate court, the parties were given 
ample opportunity of proving the custom aud I have also 
examined an indepondent witness of respectability and standing. 
There would seem to be some doubt as to the exact status of a 
woman known as a dhanti wife. ‘ho partios in the present case 
are Bishts, and I examined Trilok Singh, thokdar of Naugaon, 
who is also a Bisht, and a man of good standing, To my surprise, 
he repudiated tho notion of. being a Bisht like the parties to the 
present suit. He is something highor, and he says in his commu- 
nity adhanti wifois one who takes up her abode with a man, and 
no marriage ceremony is performed with her ; and that 1t does 
not matter if money is paid for her or not, Butin the case of 
Bishts of zamindari class, liko the partios to tho suit, if monoy 


April 28, 
1894. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(28) 


\ 


is paid for a danti woman, she is treatod as something superior 
to one for whom no money is paid. Then, tho witness adds, the 
custom is difforent for different villages and for different classes 
of Bishts. Thus, then, it is clear from this man’s statement that 
no gònerál recognized custom prevails on the subject, aud if is 
very much what each village community may choose to determine 
upon for its own special guidance. I am afraid it must be ad- 
mitted that the tone of morality here is not of a very high stand- 
ard amongst the village classes, and the custom’ regulating 
inheritanco is not clearly defined. To accept oral evidence of 
interested witnesses will not be of much help in arriving at the 
truth, Trilok Singh’s evidence is disinterested and may be relied 
on as representing something approximating tho actual state of 
_affairs at the present timo. So that, according to him, if there 
are two sons, ons by a hanti wifo and the other by a legitimate 
wife, and if the latter’s son dies without issue, then his share is 
inherited by the dhanti’s son, Thisis opposed to Hindu Law, 
as will be seen from paragraph 457 of Mayns. Itis there laid 
down that illegitimate sons can only take to their father’s estate. 
They have uo claim to inherit to collaterals. If, on the other 
hand, Trilok Singh adds, thera is issue by a legitimate wife 
alive, ora grandchild, then he would succeed to the share of the 
deceased son of another legitimate wife, to the exclusion of tha 
dhanit wite’s son. Legitimate wives cannot partake of food with 
the dhanti wife, who is, thus, a social outcast, 


Applying the custom—as far as it has been possible for mo 
to ascertain what it is—to the present case, it would appear the 
pleintiff-respondent, Mahenar Singh, who is the grandson of 
Sagat Singh, has no claim to inherit any portion of his deceased 
uncle’s (Joga’s) share. Sagat Singh enjoyed the dubious privi- 
lege of having no less than three wives—two are called legitimate. 
and one dhanti. ‘The sons of the two former were Madho Singh 
and Joga, and that of the dkanti woman, Mohan Singh. During 
his lifetime Sagat Singh divided off his property, giving each of his 
three sons a one-third portion, Madho Singh and Joga were re- 
corded together on two-thirds—Joga being shown as Madho Singh’s 
#hikmi; and Mohan Singh, by the dhanti woman, was recorded 
separately, Joga died without issue, Madho Singh is succee led 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 29 ) 


by his son Bachua, the present defendant, and Mohan Singh by 
his son Mahenar Singh, the present plaintiff. The plaintiff sues 
to succeed to half of Joga’s share, and I gave him a decree inthe 
first instance, as my limited experience in the hills has shown me 
the marriage tie sits very lightly on the village classes, and it 
is by no means an easy matbor tu.say who comes within the 
term “legitimate wife.” I have not seen any great distinction 
laid down between children of legitimate women and those of 
thanti ones. It must be borne in mind that courts of justice 
io Kumaun have no written law in such cases to guide them, 
and, as appears from the present onquiry, it is far from an easy 
matter to ascertain, wilh any degree of certainty, the actual 
custom prevailing in these parts in such an important point as 
inheritance, I have failed to trace any precedent and I should 
hail with delight an authoritative ruling on tho subject by the 
appellate court, With these remarks L re-submit the proceedings 
to the appellate court for final orders.

By the ordinary Hinda Law as laid down in the Mitakshara, 
there is no doubt that a sister is excluded by any male heir, even 
though much more remote than plaintiff’s are. Defendant how- 
ever being the sister of deceased, bases her title on the custom of 
her caste—that of Naiks. ‘The female members of this casto are 
generally, perhaps universally, brought up to the trade of prosii- 
tution, which they practise with very lucrative results in the 
plains. The customs claimed by defendant are that, on the death 
of a male without issuo, his sisters and brothers inherit, and that 
the male and female members of a family have equal rights, and 
therefore share equally, 

In support of this there are adduced the following unreyersed 

decisions of Kumaun eourts:— j 

Suit no. 90 of 1891 in the court of H, Shaw, Esq., 
Collector, Almora, Chunni and Aouli v. Narwa, Madhia 
Himatua and Mohan Lal (exhibit I). In this it was held that 


the sisters had an equal share with the brothers, defendants 
nos. 1,2 and 3, These defendants did nob however resist the 


Deputy

claim, it being against their interest to do 80, as the property. 
in which plaintiffs claimed a share had been attached by defend- 
ant no. 4 in execution of-a decree, The case however filed ay 
exhibit Q, is not open to this objection, Chunni v. Sri Ram, 
which was a claim by a sister on her brother for half share in pro- 
perty. This was decided on the 24th September, 1890, the sadar 
amin of Garhwal, an officer of great knowledge and experience 
in the hills, finding for plaintiff -on the ground that the sister 
had equal rights with the brother. This decision was upheld 
on appeal by W. R. Partridge, Esq., Deputy Commissioner of 
Garhwal, dated the 25th November, 1890, 

All the witnesses called by defendant and examined on 
commission in Bareilly agreo that this is a recognized custom 
of the caste. They are, most of them, respectable men with 
opportunities for knowing the custom of this class, as either they 
or their relations have kept women of the caste as mistresses, 
Thoy adduce numerous instances of women succeeling by inherit- 
ance. 

All the witnesses from Ramgarh in Naikana, however, the 
native village of the parties, speak against the existence of any 
such custom. 

The strongest evidence however is afforded by the phant and 
dakhilkharij files of Naikana village. These show that proparty 
has again and again passed to women, and that, roughly speaking, 
half the shares in the village are held by women, many of the 
transfers being from brother to sister. By ordinary Hindu Law 
a widow is almost the only woman who could hold a share, as 
almost invariably there is some male heir, though remote, who ` 
excludes the women. 

A large number of these dakhilkhary cases, including many 
of the transfers from a brother’s name to a sister’s, were decided 
at one time on the spot by Mr. Garstin, Senior Assistant Com- 
missioner, on January 22, 1883. 

The order for mutation mentions tho facts that tho sharers of 
the village were present and ayreed to these mutations. 

Tho witnesses from Ramgarh give as a reason, against defend. 
ant inheriting, the rule that sisters cannot inherit their 


“brother’s property except in default of male heirs, and except if 


5 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 34 ) 


they ‘reside in the family: but that by entering another man’s 
house as a kept mistress, they lose all rights in their family. 

That a woman should become a kept mistress is no reason 
why she should Jose her family rights. The relationship is nob 
that of marriage and carries with ib no rights of inheritance to 
the property of the patron, Thore is moreover much evidence 
to prove that this relationship is purely veluntary and termin- 
able, that during its existence the women are not cut off from 
family intercourse with their caste, and that on its cessation, as 
in the case of defendant, Sundar, by the death of her patron 
Mulchand, they actually go back and live with other members of 
the caste. The weight of evidence supported by numerous 
instances, and in particular the entries in the phant prove that 
far from sisters being excluded by male heirs, they are on the 
same footing as brothers. The probable reason for this differ- 
ence from ordinary Hindu Law is that the women of this caste, 
instead of being merely passive, non-productive members of 
the family, are actually the chief earners of ib, their gains being 
far more than those of the men. : Moreover, the history of this 
litigation indicates that the claim is not really on behalf of Lal 
- Singh and Gusain, the nominal plaintiffs, at all. It originated 
in a crimival petition by Munia (guardian of plaintiffs in this 
suit) against Lalu, filed July 22, 1893, charging defendant 
undor section 330, Indian Penal Cude, with stealing the jewellery 
and other immovable property—the subject of suit no. 277 now 
under trial. In her petition, and in her conduct of the criminal 
case, which was dismissed on August 21, she nowhere claims on 
behalf of Lal Singh and Gusain, but throughout makes mention 
of the property asher own. Musammat Sundar, the defendant, 
admittedly took no share of the property sued for on two pre- 
vious occasions. First whon Chunni her sis‘er died ; second when 
Chet Ram and his brother died. But she gives a sufficient 
reason for this. She was the member ofthe family who of all 
was making the largest earnings, The family was joint. There 
was therefore no need for her to have ashare; it was rather the 
other members of the family who were getting a share out of ; 
her earnings. 

The court therefore finds on this issue that the custom is 
established that the women and men of the caste, Patars and 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(85 ) 

Naiks, are on equal footing, and in particular that Musammat 
Sundar, the defendant, has not lost her rights of inher ritance, and 
that she is a nearer heir and therefore inherits to the exclusion 
of the plaintiffs Lal Singh and Gusain, 

It is unnecessary to consider other issues, as the finding on 
this issue involes the dismissal of the suits with costs, 

Decree for defendant with costs, those on the first suit being 
Rs, 442-4-5 (including Rs, 30i-8-9 pleader’s foo and Rs. 40 cost 
of stamp on security bond) and those on the second suit being 
Rs, 7-12-0 (including Rs. 6-12-0 pleader’s fee). Total costs 
Rs. 450-0-5, for which the decree is against Munia only, not 


‘against the minors Lal Singh and Gusain. 


ORDER BY COLONEL E, E, Griag, COMMISSIONER. 


For the appellant Babu Din Dayal has in a very able manner 
argued against the admission of custom, unless that custom has 
been invariably adhered to and is very ancient, quoting from 
Fields Law of Evidence in support of his arguments. He also 
points out that the evidence of Balesi Lal, Musammats Durga, 
Keshari and Maldi is that of interested parties, and as regards 
the witness who was to state to the Shastvas that he was ignorant 
of their contents. 

He considers the lower court was wrong to afford the 


respondent three months wherein to prepare evidence in support 


of his contentions, that it was nob right to have had evidence of 
the witnesses taken by commission and as to the mutations 
which had been effected in the names of females that it was 
not clear whether the cases were on all fours with the present. 
He objects to the application of the Garhwal courts finding 
in respect to a similar claim’on tho ground that the custom 
of the Naik castes in the two districts differ. On the other hand, 
the learned counsel for the defence urges most strongly that 
the entire evidence supports the respondent’s contention that 
she is entitled to inherits 

The lower court has mado a very careful and patient enquiry 
regarding the subjecb in dispute, and I think he has shown very 
clearly that in the past the right of sisters to succeed to the 
ancestral property has been the custom in the village. The 


ae, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 36 ) 


caste of the parties is that of Naiks and Patar or prostitutes, 
The females, so soon as they arrive at the age of puberty, are 
sent forth in the world to earn their living. The respondent 
had to go forth and was taken into the keeping of one Mulchand 
with whom she resided as his concubine for upwards of 20 
years and until his death. Her wages were two hundred rupees 
a month and the court is undoubtedly sound when it urges that 
such an income gave the recipiont a ‘status suporior if anything 


to those of her kinsmen who lived on the proceeds of the landed 
property alone, 


Moreover, their appears not the slightest reason for doubting 
that in the past mutations have been regularly effected in the ` 
names of sisters. In the plains Patriyas do, so far as my 
recollection goes, succeed to their brother’s property in default 
of male heirs, and it would appear a similar custom prevails in 
the hills. I decline to interfere and dismiss the appellant’s 


appeal with costs and as regards respondent’s objection as to 
adjudication of costs there is no appeal.

Hindu Law—Joint property—Sale of portion by one co-sharar without the 
consent of the other. 


Where one of two joint?Hindu brothers sold his share of the 
joint property without the consent of the other, 
the suit of the latter. 


Tuts decision applying the ordinary rule of Hindu Law on 
the point is reported, since it conflicts with the local custom 
prevailing throughout the hills. The lower appellate court 
decided the case by a referonce to Mayne and tho ordinary 
Hindu Law, and the question of local custom does not appear to 
have been raised at all. The custom of tho hills as accepted by 
the Indian members of the 1915 committee and by a public 


ancestral 
the sala was deolared void on 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 89 ) 
meet ng at Almora recently, was embodied in the following 
draft clause of the Bill prepared in 1915 :— 


“ A malo adult coparcener in joint family property, other 
than a son or grandson during the lifetime of his father or 
grandfather, shall be deemed to be entitled at any time to 
alienate his ascertainable share of the joint estate without the 
consent of his coparceners, subject to any liability of his share 
for.any common debt owed by the coparceners,” 

In the present case Gauri Datt, plaintiff, was the elder 
brother of Gobardhan, the vendor. Gobardhan sold his half of 
the ancestral property without the consent of Gauri Datt to one 
Sukhdeo Parsad. It was found that the two brothers were a 
joint family and no partition had been made, 

Babu Din Dayal, vakil, for appellant. 


ORDER BY Mr. J. S. CAMPBELL, COMMISSIONER. 

T see no reason to interfere. The lower courts have found— 
and I think rightly—that there is no evidence on the record to 
justify the presumption that respondent and his younger brother 
had separated and were no longer members ofa joint family, and 
that therefore the latter hal no power to sell part of the joint 
property without respondent’s authority ; and the sale-deed has 
been declared void. 

Appellant failed to produce all his witnesses and} refused to 
pay the costs of an adjournment, so has no grievance on thab 
point. He can certainly not claim a refund of the sale consi- 
deration from respondent, 

I uphold the Deputy Co mmissioner’s order and reject this 


appeal.

BUDH SINGH oF MAUZA DUMAILA, PATTI KHATLI, GABHWAL DISTRICT 
MUSAMMAT RANGI OF THE SAME VILLAGE (RESPONDENT).

of khaikhari land. 
Hindu Law—Inheritance—Daughter’s rights among Rajputs. 


Held that among persons of the Gorla Rawat caste, boing puro Rajputs, 
daughters inherit in proference to collaterals. 


February 7, 
1913. 


+ 


June 25, 
1913. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(40 ) 


THis is a simple case of the question of whether the local 
custom excluding daughters from inheritance was established or 
not in the case of Rajputs of the Gorla Rawat caste. 

The plaintiff endeavoured to prove the custom, but was held 
not to have succeeded, and the Commissioner applied the rules 
of the Mitakshara Law and held that the daughter’s claim was 
superior to that of near collaterals. 

Babu Din Dayal and Pandit Brij Mohan, vakils, for appel- 
lant ; and Netra Mani, mukhtar for the respondent. 


ORDER BY Mr, J. S. CAMPBELL, COMMISSIONER. 


I see no reason to interfere with the Deputy Commissionor’s 
order. 

The parties to this dispute are Gorla Rawats and pure Raj- 
pats ; and succession in their case is guided by the ordinary 
Mitakshara Law, unless there is strong evidence of peculiar local 
custom. ‘This has not been established. I therefore uphold the 
Deputy Commissioner’s finding that the daughter’s claim to her 
father’s land is superior to that of the collaterals—appellants— 
and dismiss this appeal with costs. l

KARAM SiNGH, minor, OF MAUZA TALLAGAON, PATTI UNCHAKOT,
