oF MAUZA Danma, (2) PREM SINGH BORA or THe E 
BAME VILLAGE (RESPONDENTS). 
[Special civil appeal no. 15 of 1913.] 
_ Appeal against an order of Mr. W. S. Cassels, I.0.S., Deputy Commis- 
sioner, Naini Tal, dated the 18th November, 1912, 
Hindu Law-—-Illegitimate son—Inheritance by such sons among Khasia castes— 
Claim by such son to control alienation by father during the latter's lifetime. 
Held that though tho illegitimate sons of men of Khasia castes in the 
hills inherit a share of their father’s estates on the death of their fathers, yòt 
their rights in tho estate only acerue on tho death of tho father ; they cannot 


claim to control their father in his disposition of tho property during his life- 


time, 
a 


In this case a distinction is drawn between the right of the 
illegitimate son (by a dhanti wife) of a man of tho Khasia class 
to inherit a share of his father’s estate, which right is admitted 


| 
| 


! matter of fact, Sudras and ofj mixed origin.” 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 41 ) j 
and is illustrated in the history of the case, and tho claim by 
such a son to control his father’s alienation of his landed pro- 
perty, which claim is ruled to bo inadmissible. Prem Singh, 
defendaut, had himself inherited landed property from his father, 
Bhawan Singh, though he (Prem Singh) was an illegitimate son 
by a dhanti. 

An this suit Prem Singh’s illegitimate son, Karam Singh, by a 
dhantt mother claimed to contest an alienation of certain landed 
property made by his father. Ib was held that such rights as 
may belong to illegitimate sons in Khasia castes are confined to 
inheritance on their fathers’ death; they do not ucquire a joint 
interest in the.family estate on birth. ‘Phe parties were hill 
Khasias or Khas-Rajputs of the Bora caste. 

The facts of the case atid a full discussion of the position 
a pear in tho judgement of the first appellate court (Mr. W. S. 
Cassels, Deputy Commissioner of Naini Tal) which is as fol- 
lows :— 

I have heard the learned counsel and have perused the 
record. 

The facts of the case are simple. 

Prem Singh, tho} illegitimate son of his father, sold in 1904 
landed property which he had inherited from his father to Sher 
Singh. Karam Singh, the minor and illegitimate son of Prem 
Singh, sues for the cancellation of the salo on the ground that 
it was’ a sale of ancestral property, and that tho sale was not on 
account of any prior debt nor was it one binding upon 
the joint family. 

The parties to the suit are hillmen and villagers and ihe first 
thing to see is what their local status is, Mr. Stowell in his 
book on land tenures of the Kumaun division, page 52, observes : 
“ Every Hindu villagor in Kumaun calls himsolf a Rajput or a 
Brahman, Butin reality . . + the most numerous class of 
proprietors in the hills`are really of Sudra origin or of castes s 
dubious standing. Some of them havo assumed the sacre 


threac '3 Ar dually doing so, but they are, asa 
thread and others aro gra y g 80, ale 


x° 7 JEG 7 ry 0 
case the parties call thomselves Singh, but this 18 no evidenc 


that thoy arc of Rajput origin. 
G 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 42 ) 


Padam Singh, witness for the plaintiff-respondent, says: 
“Prem Singh is my cousin. We plough our own lands. We 
are Khasia zamindars. We work as coolies and dandy bearers 

. . I amnota Sudra or Rajput.” 


This statement is clear, but the whole evidence tends to show 
that tho parties are ordinary hill hissadars, and therefore 
Khasias and not pure Rajputs. 

The next point is the nature of the debt on account of which 
the sale took place. Itis proved that the money was appliod to 
pay for builocks, goats and jewellery purchased by Prem Singh. 
There can be no question that, if a Hindu died leaving a debt 
behind him incurred for tho above purpose, his heirs would be 
bound to pay the debt, as the debt was in no way immoral. 


The third point to notice is thab this suit is brought by a 
minor, aged 13 or 14, under the gnardianship of his mother—the 
‘unmarried or dhanti wife of Prem Singh—against his father 
Prem Singh. It looks very much as if Prem Singh haa really 
instituted this suit himself, in order to get the sale-deed set. 
aside, 

The point now reached is this. The parties are Khasias and 
the debt for which the sale took place was not of an immoral 
nature. 


Mr. Stowell in his book, page 52, observes: “ It may safely 
be asserted however from a considerable experience of incidental 
and undisputed instances that among the ordinary villagers of 
somewhat dubious casto as distinct from the undoubted Brahman 
and Rujput castes, an illogitimate son inherits equally with 
legitimate sons as a matter of courso,” 

The question at issue here is not howover whether an illegi-~ 
timate son inherits from his father. Admittedly in the present 
easo Prem Singh, an illegitimate son, did inherit property from 
his father. The question is whether an illegitimate son has any 
right to control his father in the alienation of the family 
property. 

On the subject of the right of sons to control their father 


in the alienation of tho family property, Mayne (Hindu Law, 
section 322, page 382) , observes :— 


j 
| 
į 
| 
f 
| 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 3) 


“Yt must he owned that the principle of the Mitakshara that 
sons have a right to control their father iu the alienation of the 
family property is almost nullified by the other principle that 
they are bound after his death to pay his debts even though 
contracted without necessity ; aud by the logical extension of 
that principle, recently laid down by the Privy Council, that the 
father is entitled to sell the family property in order to pay off 
his own debts, which were not contracted for tho benefit of the 
family, but which the sons would bo under a moral obligation 
to discharge.” 

(Girdharee Lal v. Kantoo Lal 1 I.A. 321: S.C. 14, B.L.R, 187, — 
S.C. 22 Suth. 56.) 

It has been seen that Prem Singh’s debts, in the present caso, 
were not of an immoral nature, bub were such as his heirs would 
be bound to discharge. On the principle laid down above by the 
Privy Council, Prem Singh was entitled to sell the family pro- 
perty in order to pay his debts. If the above principle 
governs the cases of all legitimate sons, much moro does it 
govern the case ofan illegitimate son. For the question arises, 
does an illegitimate son have a joint intorest with the father in 
the property? Are they members of a joint family ?, 

As to this, Mayne (page 374, section 316) says: “ Disposition 
of property by a father can of course be objacted to by those 
who have a joint interest with him in the property, either by 
joint acquisition or by birth. Whero the objection is based on 
the latter ground, ib is necessary to show that such an interest 
vested in the objector at his birth or by his birth.” 

On the subject of illegitimate sons, Mayne (page 629, sec- 
tion 503) says: “Illegitimate sons in the three higher classes 
never take as heirs, but ar® only entitled to maintenance . 
The illegitimate son of a Sudra may however under certain 
circumstances inherit either jointly or solely.” 

And again (section 506, page 633) Mayne says: “ Supposing 
an illegitimate Sudra to be entitled, tho next question would be 
as to his rights. Upon this the Mitakshara says in oxplanation 
of the texts of Manu and Yajnayalkya: ‘Ihe son begotten 
by a Sudra on a female slave, obtains a share by the father’s 


choice, or at his pleasure, But after the demise of the father, if 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


Cas) 


there be sons of a wedded wife, let these brothers allow the son 
of the femalo slave to participate for half a share, ete? ” 

The point brought out here is that the illegitimate son of a 
Sudra inherits “ by the father’s choice or at his pleasure.” The 
inference to be drawn is that he has no rights at birth or by his 
birth, His inheritance depends upon his father’s choice or 
pleasure. 

It may be said that this is all very far from Kumaun, where 
Mr. Stowell says that in the case of ordinary Khasia families an 
illegitimate son inherits equally with legitimate sons as a matter 
of course. * But the point ia that in this case the suit is based on 
the principle of Hindu Law that a son has a right to control his 
father in the alienation of the family;property. 

If therefore the plaintiff in this case bases his claim upon the 
ordinary Hindu Law, it is necessary to go to the Hindu Law to 
see that he cannot claim to have such aright, as ke is illegiti- 
mate and did not inherit such aright, at his birth or by his birth. 

Another poirt is whether property inherited by Prem Singh, 
an illegitimate son,j7from his father, can be properly styled 
ancestral property. I do not think it can bo so called. 

Lastly, Prem Singh himself says: “I got the share on the 
death of my father... While the father is alive, he stays 
in possession, but on his death the property is divided.” ‘This 
statement seoms to show that while an illegitimate son among 
hill Khasias may inherit from his father, he does not havea 
share in the property till his father’s death. He cannot there- 
fore claim to control his father as regards tho alienation of the 
property belonging to the father. For all these reasons I hold 
that the sale cannot be cancelled, and I allow tho” appeal. ‘The 
sale will stand and the claim is dismygsed with costs. 


os 


ORDER BY Mr. J. S. CAMPBELL, COMMISSIONER. 

T see no reason to differ from the learned Deputy Commis- 
sioner’s finding in this case. There is no doubt that among the 
Khasias of Kumaun an illegitimate son by a dhanti wife is 
entitled to inherit a portion of his father’s ancestral estate. But 
thistright of inheritance accrues after the father’s death, and the 
Deputy Commissioner has given substantial reasons for holding 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 45 ) 


that such an illegitimate son has no right of control over the 
ancestral property during the fathor’s lifetime. 

In the present case the father parted with the property for 
purposes which were rot immoral ; and even a legitimate son 
would have found difficulty in disputing the necessity for the sale, 

For the above reasons I uphold the Deputy Commissioner’s 
order and dismiss this appeal with costs.

DULAB SINGH AND ANOTHER OF MAUZA PANCHALI LOHABA, GARHWAL 
(PETITIONER) 0. MUSAMM AT GANGA AND ANOTHER (OPPOSITE PARTIES). 
[Miscellaneous case no. 45 of 1914.] 


Hindu Law—Daughter's inheritance —Gift by widow to daughter and son-in-law — 
Suil by reversioners—Form of decree. 


Whore a Hindu widow gifted certain of her lato husband's lands to hor 
daughter and son-in-law jointly, and the collateral revorsioners sued to sot 
aside the gift, it was held by the Commissioner that the parties being pure , 
Rajputs wore subject to tho ordinary Mitakshara Law and the daughter would 
succeed in preforence to collaterals, and the suit was dismissed. Qn roferenca 
to the High Court by tho Local Governmont, it was held that in view of tho 
fact that the gift was partly_to the daughter and partly to the daughtor’s 
husbind, the suit was mvintainable and a decree should bo granted deolaring 
that the gift would not bind the plaintiffs boyond tho donor’s lifetime, but 
that the daughtor, if still alive, would have hor rights of inhoritancaas a 
daughter on her mother’s death. 


Tur facts of the case are interesting as illustrating local 
customs. Musammat Rupa, the donor, was originally the widow 
of one Shib Singh. On his death withoub issue she was taken 
to wife by his younger brother, Umrao Singh, by the local 
levirate custom and bore him five daughters including Musammat 
Ganga; Umrao. Singh also left a lawfully married widow, 
Musammat Gaura. 

Musammat Rupa gifted part of Umrao Singh’s landed 
property to her daughter Musammat Ganga and son-in-law 
Darmian Singh, reciting that the latter had been adopted as 
a ghar-jawain (resident son-in-law) by Umrao Singh according 
to local custom. ‘The court of first instance found that Darmian 
Singh had not in fact been so adopted. 

Lhe plaintiffs, nophows of Umrao Singh, sued to set aside the 
gift undor the local custom, whereby daughters are oxcluded 
from inheritance by collateral heirs, 


June 


1914, 


18, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 46 ) 

The Commissioner held that the parties being pure Rajputs 
must follow the normal Mitakshara rule, whereby daughters 
inherit in preference to collaterals, in the absence of proof of 
peculiar local custom, and the other daughters and widows were 
also nearer heirs than the plaintiffs, who thus had no status 
to sue. 

The High Court on a reference returned the opinion quoted 
above, which was embodied in a decree by the Local Govern- 
ment (G. O. no. 930/VII—532, dated July 9, 1914). 

It will be noticed that tho legitimacy and right of inheritance 
‘of the daughters of Umrao Singh by his brother’s ‘widow were 
accepted by the courts, 

There appears to be some “inconsistency here ; this peculiar 
custom of the levirate and legitimacy of the children by such 
a connection was accepted, although the parties were pure 
Rajputs in whose case it might be doubted, while the exclusion 
of daughters from intieritance, which is a more univorsal local 
custom irrespective of caste, was rejocted on the ground that 
the parties were Rajputs (and not Khasias). 

Pandit Mathura Datt Pande, vakil, for appellant. 


ORDER By Mr. J. ©. CAMPBELL, COMMISSIONER, 
DATED THE 29TH SEPTEMBER, 1918. 

I sce no reason to interfere with the Deputy Commissioner’s 
order. These people are pure Rajputs and as such subject to 
the ordinary Mitakshara Law unless there is clear proof of any 
peculiar local custom in opposition to the normal law. Thoro 
is uo proof of such local custom in this case. Consequently the 
daughters would succeed to the parental proparty in preference to 
appellants. ‘There are another widow and four more daughiers, 
all of whom must be considered revorsioners in preference to 
appellants, who thus havo no status to apply for cancellation 
of the deed executed by Musammat Rupa in favour of Musam-

dismiss this appeal with costs,

FATEH SINGH or MAUZA KOTHGI, PATTI DASJULA, GARHWAL DISTRICT 
(APPELLANT) V. GABAR SINGH OF TIE SAME VILLAGE (RESPONDENT). 


[Specia] civil appeal no. 49 of 1915.] 


Appeal against an order of Mr, J. M. Clay, LO.S., Doputy Commissioner, 
Garhwal, dated the 18th September, 1915. 


Held Law—Local customs regarding marriage among Rajputs—Inheritance ~ 
Proper framing of issues—Evidence necessary to prove local custom, 

Held that among tho Bhandari casto of Rajputs a local form of marriage 
wherein the Ganeshpuja ceremony is performed, but the Saptapadi and Phera 
Bhaura arə not performed is not a lawful marriage, and the son of a woman go 
married does not inherit; in such cases a clear issuo must be framed on tho 
spocific point in issue and the party elleging a local custom in variation of the 
normal Hindu Law rogarding marriago must provo olearly that tho specific 
custom set up is followod in the caste to which tho parties bolong. 

The parties in this case woro Bhandari Rajputs of Garhwal, 
Fateh Singh, the son of Daulat Singh, sued to sot aside a sale- 
deed and a deed-of-gift both executed by his step-mother, 
Musammat Purba, in favour of one Gabar Singh. 

It was contended by the defendant that Fateh Singh’s mother, 
Musammat Maina, the second wife of Daulat Singh, was nob 
lawfully married to the latter, and thorefore Fateh Singh had no 
title to inherit or to contest the alienations, Fateh Singh was 
successful in all the Kumaun courts, but the case was then 
referred by the Local Government to the High Court for 
Opinion ; the reply of the High Court is reproduced below, and in 


April 


1916 


15, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 48 ) 


accordance with its opinion the Local Government remanded the 
case for further enquiry and decision by the Kumaun courts, 
The Commissioner’s final order in second appeal from the 
remand decision is given below. 

The above sketch and the orders reproduced give the facts of 

the case sufficiently. 
‘ If I may be permitted to expand the Commissioner’s remarks 
in his final order, I would point out that to any one acquainted 
with the general trond of Kumaun custom it is apparent 
that the Hon’ble High Court (not to mention the Local Govern- 
ment in their reference) have fallen into the very error which 
they reprehend and on the basis of which the case was remand- 
ed, 

The specific issue on which the caso really turned was not 
whether Musammat Maina was lawfully married to Daulat Singh 
but whether by tho local custom of the Bhandari caste sons by 
such a marriage (whether lawful or not) inherit as of right (a) 
equally with sons by a lawfully married wife or (b) as in this 
case in the absence of such other sons. 

The local custom is not a question of the legality of the 
mother’s marriage—there is often no question whatever that 
there was no legal marriage—but whether the particular caste ` 
docs or does not follow the general custom of Khas-Rojputs 
(and other Khasias), whereby the sons of women not lawfully 
married bub permanently living with the father as secondary 
wives, do inherit equally with legitimate offspring. As the 
Commissioner puts ib “it is no doubt locally held that the son 
of Daulat Singh is quite legitimate enough to succeed to his 
father’s farm.” 

_ Without going so far as to lay down that this ¿s the custom 
“of the Bhandari casto, I merely point out that the case was 
decided under the remand instructions on the wrong issue. 


Opinion of the High Court (dated the 8th February, 1914) 
on reference by the Local Government ; Chamier 
‘and Piggott, JJ. 
[Miscellaneous caso no, 449 of 1914,] 
This is a reference by the Local Government under rule 
17 of the rules and orders relating to the Kumaun division. 


Raita intimin et to 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 49 ) 


The question in issue ont of which this reference arises is 
whether Fateh Singh was the legitimate son of one Daulat Singh, 
It is prove! that Daulat Singh was in the first instance lawfully 
married to one Musammat Purwa or Purba, While this woman 
was still alive Daulat Singh brought to his house one Musammat 
Maina, who is described as belonging to a lower sub-division 
of the caste of which Daulat Singh wasa member. The facts 
found to be proved are that a bride price of Rs. 200 was’ paid to 
Maina’s father ; that she was brought to Daulat Singh’s house in 
a dooly; that the biradari was feasted, and that a ceremony 
known as Ganeshpuja was performed. It is expressly found 
that the Saptapadi and Phera Bhawra ceremonies, which are 
ordinarily considered as necessary for the validity of a marriage 
among the higher castes, were not performed. The Kamaun 
courts have however come to the conclusion that the marriage 
was valid and Fateh Singh, the offspring of this marriage, was a 
legitimate son. The Local Government has referred this case 
to this court, because it is dissatisfied with the way in which 
this point has been dealt with by the local courts. We think 
there is reason for this dissatisfaction. We find that the issue 
as framed by the first court on this point was in the vaguest and 
broadest terms possible, namely, “Is the plaintiff entitled to 
sue?” The court of first appeal, which has come nearest to 
discussing the matter from a right point of view, after setting 
forth the facts above noted, proceeds to remark that it was the 
case for tho plaintiff-respondent, that is to say, for Fateh Singh, 
that the Ganeshpuja isa form considered by the custom of 
Rajputs in the district of Garhwal as constituting & valid mar- 
riage, particularly in the case of a second marriage. It was thore 
remarked that there was no evidence to this effect on the file, 
but a finding in favour of the validity of the marriage was arrived 
at on the ground that it is a matter of common knowledge that 
the ordinary Hindu Law and usage is considerably modified by 
local custom, especially in reference to marriage and succession 
among the Rajputs in the hills. This does not seem to be suffi- 
cient, because it does not find what particular modifications of 
the ordinary Hindu Law are recognized as sufficient to constitute 
a valid marriage, and whether the performance of Ganeshpuja, 
7 


ae GR ad 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 60 ) 


as alleged by the plaintiff in the present case, is such a modifi. 
cation. The court of second appeal has virtually adopted the 
decision of the Deputy Commissionor without discussing it. In 
the order of reference by the Local Government it is suggested 
that in order to prove a valid marriage it was incumbent on the 
plaintiff in this case, the burden of proof lying on him to establish 
his own legitimacy, to show that by the custom of the caste 
or district the ceremonies found to have been performed in 
the presont case are considered as constituting a valid marriage, 
and that these ceremonies were performed with the object of 
constituting such valid marriage between Daulat Singh and 
Musammnt Maina, We desire to remark that in spite of the 
unsatisfactory state of the evidence on this point, we might have 
been prepared to accept as sufficient to cover any other deficiency 
in the plaintiffs case clear and overwhelming evidence that, from 
the date of her arrival in Daulat Singh’s house, Musammat 
Maina was treated in every respect, by those persons who are 
likely to be well informed, as Daulat Singh’s lawful wife. So 
far however from the evidence being clear and unambiguous, 
there is definite evidence to the contrary. The courts below 
have found that rice cooked by Musammat Maina was not eaten 
by the members of Daulat Singh’s family or brotherhood. 
Under the circumstances we are of opinion that the point raised 
by the Local Government’s order of reference should have been 
made the subject of a specified issue and determined as stated in 
the order of reference. We return the record with this answer, 
and we think all costs, including costs in this court, should abide 
the event of the suit.

FINAL ORDER BY Mr. P. WYNDHAM, COMMISSIONER. 


P. Brijmohan Chandola, vakil, for GABAR SINGH, and FATEH 
SINGH, in person. 

This is a second appeal and the only point for this court to 
decide is “whether by virtue of any custom existing among the 
Bhandari sect Musammat Maina was legally married to Daulat 
Singh.” ‘The issue asso framed is on the son, Fateh Singh, 
and to be successful in the proof of a custom the learned lower 
appellate court shows many essentials are necessary, It is clear 


Taia a 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(ae) 


that the plaintiff Fateh Singh has come nowhere near the middle 
rung of his ladder in attempting to scale the formidable obstacle 


. which the law casts up against him. 


The persons are residents of northern Garhwal and probably 
have never heard of the Mitakshara Law. However, calling 
themselves Hindus they have to abide by it when they come to 
court, While in the simplicity of their life they have never 
assimilated the mass of detail laid down in this formidable 
volume,—yet the law strictly requires them to bring down from 
these hills a mass of evidence to prove they have never done so. 

To speak plainly,the law imposes an impossibility. ` Were 
this the ultimate court of appeal I would not hesitate to hold, 
what is no doubt held locally, that the son of Daulat Singh is 
quite legitimate enough to succeed to his father’s farm, As 
however the directions given me are so drawn up, it is impossible 
for the courts of Kumaun to do so. I regret that I have to 
concur with the learned lower court and dismiss this appeal 
and feel that in doing so we are acting quite contrary to public 
opinion in the valleys from which this case comes, We are 
however accomplishing a task which could not be performed 
ovon by the most zealous of Hindu missionaries, 

This appeal is dismissed with costs.

Before Colonel G. E. Ersking, Commissioner. 

PAR3ISAH JAGATI (SPECIAL APPELLANT-PLAINTIEF) V. BUE RAM, 

BADRI AnD GANGISAH (Spica, RESPONDENTS: -DESENDANTS). 

[Special appeal against an ordər of Mr. F. Giles, Sonior Assistant Commis- 
sioner, Kumaun, dated the 20th August, 189), dis slowing piain i s claim to 
enforce right of pro-emption against defendant. ] 

Pre-emption —Claim by naar re ‘alive nat being a co- shaven in the villaye —Wistory 
of the custom in Kumaun. 

Held thata near relative of the vendor of a share in a village has no right 
of pre-emption at all, when sush relative is not himself a co- sharer in the 
village. 

THE facts of the case appear sufficiently from the orders of 
the Commissioner and the remand finding of the Senior Assistant 
Commissioner, which is repro luced in full, a3 co ntaining a full 
and valuable discussion regarling the nature and history of the 

custom of pre-emption in Kumaun and reference to various 
decisions on the snbject. 


Pundit Hari Ram Pande for respondents. 


PRELIMINARY ORDER BY Cou. G. E. ERSKINE, COMNISSTONER. 

The share in mauza Bhatgaon of Gangi was sold to Bijai Ram 
and Badri who were not previously sharers in that village. 

Parsi sued to be allowed to exercise the right of pre-emption. 
He did not distinctly state the grounds upon which he based his 
claim, and ib was dismissed in both the lower courts, cn the 
ground that at the time when the sale took place Parsi had no 
share in the village, and consequently no right of pre-emption 
under the usual clause in the ikrarnama. 

But Parsi objects that, irrespective of the’ ikrarnama, he has 
aright by the custom of the country. He asserts hat he is 


first cousin of Gangi, the seller, thus: — 


PEN 


“all 
Shib lial. Hijai Ram. 


Parsi Gangi (soller) 
(plaintiff). 5 (respondent). 


December 16, 
1890. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 56 ) 


The issue is, whether, by the custom of the country, a first 
cousin, who is not a co-sharer of the seller, has a right of pre- 
emption as against an outsider. Thisissue has not been dealt 
with by the lower courts in the present case. The decisions 
put before me are conflicting. Sir Henry Ramsay in the case 
of Dharmanand (appellant) v. Ganga Ram dezided on the ord 
August, 1866, that a relation of a seller, not being a co-sharer in 
the village in which the property sold was situated, had no right 
of pre-emption as against a co-sharor who was no! a ralation of 
the seller. 

Mr. Ross, on the 9th December, 1887, in the case of Kamla- 
pati and Ganga Datt v. Purnanand and others decided “the 
universal custom is that relatives up to the third generation have 
the right of pre-emption as against an outsider. Hundreds of 
cases have been decided according to this rule.” 

Then there is a decision by Mr. Giles, Senior Assistant Com- 
missioner, in the case of Lal Singh v. Kallian and others, dated 
the 6th July, 1888, in which the following passaga occu rs :— 
“Subsequently no doubt. . . propinquity of relationship to 
the seller was grafted on as a fourth qnalification for the pre- 
emptive right. How far relationship is a qualification has been 
authoritatively settled.” The Sonior Assistant Commissioner 
then quotes the case of Kamlapati v. Purnanand. He cons 
claded by deciding as between Lal Singh and Kallian Bisht, who | 
were both sharers in the village, that Lal Singh being related to 
the seller in only the fifth degree had no right of pre-emption 
superior to that of Kallian Bisht who was not related to, though 
a co-sharer of, the seller. He concluded by remarking that the 
appellant (Lal Singh) might have hala good case against the 
stranger Jaideb (who had effected an intermediate purchase). 

There is also another case decided by Mr. Ross on the, 
23rd December, 1887, Amba Datt v. Badri Sah in which the 
Commissioner seems to have held that relationship within the 
third degree did not establish a right of pre-emption where the 
relation was not also a sharer in the village. Buthe also gives 
as areason for disallowing the claim of the relative of the seller, 
that the sellers had disposed of their entire village to tho pur- 
chaser (an outsider) and that tho purchaser had a garden near 
the place. 


nS SNE Saat Sa 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(wots) 


In this conflict of decisions it is difficult to say what is the 
customary law among the Hindus in Kumaun. 

I therefore remand this case to the lower appellate court— 

(1) for explanation as to why it did not in this case follow 
the authoritative settlement to which it referred in 
the case Lal Singh v. Kallian Singh, 

(2) for further enquiry into, and finding on, tho issue 
Whether, by the custom of the country, the first 
cousin (aot being a co-sharer) of a seller has or has 
not aright of pre-emption as against an outsider. 

The proceedings will be returned to this court for final dispo- 
sal of the appeal. 

I would here note that I regard the question as purely one 
of the existence or non-existence of such a customary right as is 
claimed. As to whether it is expedient that such a right should 
be acknowledged in favour of a relative who has no interest in 
the village of which the property sold forms a part, I express no 
opinion. 

REMAND FINDING By MR. F. GILES, SENIOR Assistant COM- 

MISSIONER, KUMAUN, DATED THE 6TH SEPTEMBER, 1890. 

. With reference to paragraph 1 of the Commissioner’s order 
of remand, I would explain that I did not follow the authorita- 
tive settlement to which I referredin the case of Lal Singh v. 
Kallian Singh, because [ understand the purport of that settle- 
ment, pronouncedin Kamlapativ. Purnanand to be that as 
between sharers in a village only, propinquity of relationship to 
the vendor within the third degree determines right of pre-emption 
to land sold within that village. Kawmlapati and Purnanand 
were both sharers in the village in which the land sold was 
situated. In the present case the claimant of the right of pre- 
emption is a relation of the vendor, but he was not at the time of 
the sale a sharer in the village in which the land sold is situated. 

Paragraph 2 of Commissioner's order.—The claimant has 
called several witnesses, whose statements 1 have recorded, to 
show that mere relationship to a vendor confers right of pro- 
emption, The evidence of these witnesses is of no practical 
value, Parsi Sah has filed copy of a decision in the caso of 

8 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 58 ) 


Guman Singh v. Narain Singh. In that case the claimant for 
pre-emption obtained a decree because he was related to the 
vendor in the eighth or ninth degree and was alsoa sharer in the 
village while the vendor was of a different caste to the vendee 
and was not asharer in the village. This decision is not germane 
to the present enquiry, since the pre-emptor’s claim was good 
quite apart from relationship, because he was a co-sharer in the 
vendor’s village and the vendee was not. 

Parsi Sah also cited the case of Biji Sunar v. Durga Datt, 
etc., in which the value of certain house property in the town 
of Almora wasin question, A right of pre-emption had been 
decreed and was not disputed on appeal, ‘The property consisted 
of half a house and the successful claimant for pre-emption was 
the heir of the vendor and also owner of the other half of the 
house. The property in dispute was not village land, but here 
too the pre-emptor already owned a share in the house sold, so 
that the proceedings throw no light on the rights of a claimant 
for pre-emption who is not a co-sharer, The third case mention- , 
ed by Parsi Sah is that of himself versus Daya Nand, etc. 

In this case Pandit Ganga Datt, deputy collector, decreed 
the pre-emptor’s right to Parsi Sah, because the purchasers 
had no share in the village in which the land sold was situated 
and were not related to the vendor, while Parsi Sah was his 
nephew. 

Pandit Ganga Datt found that under these circumstances the 
land was Parsi Sah’s ancestral (mawrust) property. 

This decision was not appealed and with great respect for 
Pandit Ganga Datt’s legal attainments, I should hesitate to 
assume, solely on the authority of this case, the existence of a 
right of pre-emption as between Hindus to ancestral property 
for which I can find no warrant in treatises on Hindu Law, 
such as those of Mayne and Macnaughten, which is not recognized 
by the Kumaunis generally and of which I have been unable to 
find mention in other judicial records filed in this office. 

‘he above constitutes the evidence offered by Parsi Sah in 
support of his view that a first cousin of a vendor of village land, 
who isnota co-sharer in the village, enjoys a right of pre- 
emption against a stranger. 


at 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 59 ) 


I have made some independent enquiries in respect of the 
issue indicated by the Commissioner. 

In Govind Dayal v. Inayat-ullah (I. L. R. AU, Vol. VII, 
page 775) the nature of pre-emption was discussed at great longth 
by the local High Court. It was laid down that the doctrine of 
pre-emption belongs strictly to the Muhammadan Law, but that 
Hindus who deal with Muhammadans in respect of property, 
knowing the conditions and obligations under which such pro- 
perty is held, cannot be permitted to evade those conditions and 
obligations. 

Mr. Justice Mahmud held (page 789) that there has never 
been such a right as that of pre-emption recognized by Hindu 
Law, though the object of the rule of Hindu Law that prohibits 
a member of a joint undivided family from selling his share in 
the joint property is probably similar to that of the law of pre- 
emption. At page 790 he referred to exceptional local customs 
of pre-emptive right vesting in Hindu shareholders of a village 
in which land is sold as against a purchaser who is nob a 
co-sharer. 

At page 801 he pointed out that in the Muhammadan Law 
from which the custom of pre-emption has, where it obtains 
among Hindus, been borrowed, the cause of pre-emption is the 
contiguousness of the purchased property whether by co-patner- 
ship or vicinage. 

In Jai Ram v. Mahabir Rai, ete. (I. L. Rẹ All, Vol. VII, 
page 720) it was held in respect of. a right of pre-emption 
recorded in a villoge wajib-wl-arz, as between Hindu co-sharers, 
that partition extinguished the right in the new mahal thereby 
formed. 

At page 726 the dictum pronounced in a former oase was 
quoted and assented to that “ an essential condition of a right of 
pre-emption is, that the parties claiming such a right shall be 
coparceners in the same estate as those against whom the claim 
is made, a relation between parties which is extinguished by the 
very operation of partition and the separate proprietorship there- 
by established.” : 

In Dharmanand v. Ganga Ram the Commissioner (Sir H. 
Ramsay) laid down on appeal that “ tho object of pre-emption is 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 60 ) 


to secure village proprietors against injurious intrusion of out- 
siders—not to secure the rights of distant relatives.” In this 
case the vendee was not a relation of the vendor, and indeed was 
of a different caste, but he was a sharer in the village in which 
the land sold was situated. The claimant for pre-emption on the 
other hand was arelation of the vendor, but he was not a sharer 
in the village and he lost his case. 

In Amba Datt v. Biji Ram the Commissioner (Mr. Ross) 
found that the appellant was within “ the degree of relationship 
for which pre-emptiom is allowed ” but he was not a shareholder 
in the village and therefore could not claim pre-emption, 

This claim Very clearly illustrates the explanation afforded 
above as to the first paragraph of the order of;remand now under 
reply. 

In Joga Singh v. Chanar Singh the Commissioner (Mr; Ross) 
held that Joga, though he was qualified for pre-emption}; qua 
relationship, had no connection with the village, and therefore 
could not claim pre-emption, I have consulted the sadar amin 
and his opinion is submitted herewith. It is to the effect 
that heretofore there has been no fixed rule as to pre-emption 
but every hakim has decided as seemed best in his own eyes, 
In the sadar amin’s view cousins should enjoy a right of pre- 
emption as heirs, whether or not they are co-sharers, and 
he says that formerly the-most distant relations to the vendor 
could claim pre-emption. As I have indicated I can find no 
judicial precedent for this extreme view and the suit now under 
consideration was, at the original hearing, dismissed by the sadar 
amin himself, because the claimant for pre-emption was not a 
sharer in the vendor’s village. 

There seems to be some confusion in the minds both of the 
sadar amin and Pandit Ganga Datt between pre-emption 
which is a right in respect of property conveyed to another by 
a living person, an heirship which concerns title to the property 
left behind him by a person deceased. 

j Further enquiry then has only served to. strengthen the 
opinions I have already expressed as to the nature of the 

pre-emptive right in regard to village landin Kumaun, I under- 
Atanasia ownership of a share in the village in which the land 


sói 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


C 


sold is situated is the first requisite for pre-emption; but that as 
between co-sharers who are claimants to the right superior 
propinquity of relationship to the vendor within the third degree 
confers preference, This qualification has, I believe, been grafted 
on to the original custom which was itself unknown under the 
Hindu administrations of the Chand Rajas and the Gurkhas and 


was borrowed from the Muhammadan Law and introduced into — 


Kumaun by British officials for political purposes. 

I can find no justification for the view that relationship to 
the vendor, however near, can in itsclf and apart from ownership 
of a share in the village, support a claim for pre-emption to 
village land in Kumaun. 

The papers are again submitted to the Commissioner, together 
with the complete records of the cases herein referred to. 

FINAL ORDER BY COLONEL G. E. ERSKINE, COMMISSIONER, 
DATED THE 1TH DECEMBER, 1890, 

Read further proceedings of the lower appellate court, 
dated the 6th September, 1890. 

I have now no hesitation in ruling that ueither under settle- 
ment arrangements, nor under Hindu Law, nor under the custom 
in Kumaun, has the relative of the vendor of property in village 
land any right of pre-omption against a purchaser who is not 
related to the vendor, whether that purchaser is, or is not, a 
co-sharer in the village of the vendor. At the time} when; Badri 
purchased the share of Gaagi, tho plaintiff (special appellant) 
had no share in mauza Bhatgaon. Consequently he had no right 
of pre-emption. 

Special appeal dismissed. 


Before|Mr. F. Giles, C.S., Officiating Commissioner. 
DAT RAM PUJARI, MAUZA ANETHA (APPELLANT-PoarntisF) V RAGHUNATH 
AND JAYA NAND (PURCHASERS) AND GAURI DATT (SEDLER) 
(RESPONDENTS-DEFENDANTS). 
i [Special civil appeal no. 7.] 
Appeal against an ordor of Mr. W. R. Partridgo, O.B., Senior Assistant 
Commissionor, Garhwal, dated the 28th April, 1891, 
Pre-emption—Asl (principal) village and laga (hamlet)—Co-sharer in one but 
not in the other. 
Held that for the purposes of pre-omption tho asl (principal) villago and 
laga (harnlet or offshoot) are ono. Whore a purohasor of a share in a laga 


July 11, 
1891. 


A 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


Ger) 


was already a co-sharer in the asl village, it was held that a co-sharer in the 
laga had no right of pre-emption against him. 

In the hills lagas or subsidiary villages are often attached 
to asl or principal villages: they possess separate maps and 
records of proprietary rights but are held under_one revenue 
engagement with the principal village. In this case Jwalpa- 
sera is a laga of Anetha. The purchasers, Raghunath and 
Jaya Nand, were already co-sharers in Anetha, but not in Jwalpa- 


sera. 
Gauri Datt sold them his share in Jwalpasera and Dat 


Ram, another co-sharer in Jwalpasera (as well as in the principal 
village) claimed the right of pre-emption. 

The claim was rejected in all the courts. 

ORDER BY Mr. F. GILES, COMMISSIONER. 

I have no doubt that the decisions of the lower courls are 
correct. The object of the peculiar custom of pre-emption of 
village land in Kumaun is to exclude strangers and the pre- 
emption right obtains only against outsiders, It does not take 
effect against a purchaser who has an interest in the village in 
which land is sold whether his share is recorded in the asli 
village or laga. For the purpose of pre-emption as for other 
purposes the asli village and the laga are one. 

Appeal is dismissed with costs.

ROSHAN DIN anp ALI BAKHSH, ALMORA (APPELLANTS-PLAINTIPFS) V. 


(1) MUKIM (venvor) anp (2) LACHMI BALLABH 
(RESPONDENTS-DEFENDANTS). 


[Regular civil appeal no, 1 of 1893. } 


Appeal against an order of Colonel G, E. Reade, Assistant Commissioner, 
Almora, dated the 1st November, 1893. 


Pre-emplion—Town lots in Almora—Muhammadans—Shara Muhammadi not 
followed by Almora Muhammadans. 


Held that the custom of pre-emption extends to town lots in Almora, that 
pre-emption can be claimed by a joint co-sharer with the vendor, and that 
Almora Muhammadans do not observe the legal forms of the Shara Muham- 
madi but follow the local rules applicable to Hindus. The court also expressed 
the opinion that among local Muhammadans a claim of pre-emption by a near 
relative as such (not a co-sharor) might also be entertained. 


In this case one Mukim sold a plot of land in Almora town 
to Lachmi Ballabh. Ali Bakhsh and Roshan Din, plaintiffs, 
were the brothers of the vendor and claimed pre-emption, 
alleging that the plot was part of the ancestral land-owned 
jointly by themselves and Mukim. 

The case was remanded for further enquiry as to local custom 
by Mr. D. T. Roberts, Commissioner, and finally decided by 
Colonel E. E. Grigg, Commissioner. 

The remand report by Mr. Sturt, Deputy Commissioner, 
stated that the custom of pre-emption obtained in Almora town, 
that relatives of vendors were entitled to pre-emption, and that 
the rights of co-sharers were not recognized; there was no 
memorandum of customs for the urban area. 

It will be seen that the Commissioner, though his order and 
findings are not clearly pub, did not accept the above report as 


April 27, 
1894,

OR 


regards custom, but decreed the claim on the ground of plaintiffs 
being co-sharers with the vendor. 
Babu Din Dayal for respondents.

They don’t prove this by any means but they give indications 
where proof may be had and he should, under the circumstances, 
be allowed to give proof, if possible, that he was at Ranikhet 
on the 28th November, 1891. There are also some preliminary 
points to be proved. It is not clear whether a right of pre- 
emption exists in the case or not, The claim apparently is 
under local custom not under Muhammadan Law, because the 
special requirements of the Muhammadan Law had not been 
complied with. s 

It is first therefore to be desided whether a custom holds in 
Almora or not, and if it does, does the right of pre-emption 
extend to both Ali Bakhsh and Roshan Din or to Ali Bakhsh 
only, and if it extends to Roshan Din also, what offect that has

to the sale.

FINAL ogpER BY COLONEL E. E. GRIGG, COMMISSIONER. 
No notice has been served on Mukim’s heirs. But inasmuch 
as this appeal is in reality between appellants and the purchaser, 


-Lachmi Ballabh, this omission may be passed over, Roshan 


Din asserts he has a share inasmuch as ho obtained a share in 
his deceased brother (Bakhshi’s) share of the property. A notice 
of court, dated the 29th October, 1888, for mutation of names on 
the death of ahim was served at the instance of Ali Bakhsh, and 
on this Ali Bakhsh states mutation of names was effected in the 
names of Ali Bakhsh, Mukim, Mula, Mujnuand Bakhshi’s widow. 
It may be remarked that in the report of the 4th January, 
1894, Fahim’s name was found entered in the revenue register 
up to the 23rd April, 1892, when Lachmi Bullabh’s namo was 
recorded in respect to 4/16 out of 18/16 of no, 545 whilst Fahim’s 
Name remained in respect of 9/16 of this plob, From this, and 
the fact that Fahim has been dead for the last six years, it is 
evident the revenue registers are nob posted up-to-date. 

= But Roshan Din states that he is a revorsioner of Bakhshi’s 
share. That he has since Bakhshi’s death supported the widow, 
and his claim to succeed to Bakhshi’s share on the death of the 
latter’s widow is admitted by Mujnu and Ali Bakhsh, both of 
Whom are present. From this it would appear that Roshan Din 
is @ presumptive sharer in the property left by Fahim, although 
in respect to his original share he was componsated, 


9 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(66) 

This disposes of the question of Roshan Din’s claiming as z 
prospective sharer in the ancestral property. I do not thus 
ecnsider it is necessary to discuss the question of a relative 
claiming. the right of pre-emption. Though I may be permitted 
to say that cases may arise whon such a claim advanced even by 
Musalmans should be entertained. 


The origin of these three cases appears to have been the 
desire of Lachmi Ballabh to acquire a piece of land which is 
situated near his shop. In Kidri’s case (special appeal no. 11 of 
1893) the document executed was first found to be a deed of gift. 
But after a long enquiry it was held to be a deed of sale, The 
plea set up by Lachmi Ballabh was ovidently with the object of. 
defeating a claim for pre-emption. ‘There are two suits regarding’ 
the share sold by Mukim. The first was instituted by Roshan 
Din and Ali Bakhsh on the 29th March, 1892, and the second by 
Mujnu on the 19th November, 1892. They are the subjects. 
of appeals nos. 1 and 12 of 1893. In the former Roshan Din 
and Ali Bakhsh are appellants and in the latter Lachmi Balla!yh. 
There is also a third case (appeal no. 11) in respect to the sale 
effected by Kidri. In this case Roshan Din and Ali Bakhsh are 
the plaintiffs and Lachmi Ballabh and Kidri the defendant. 
Lachmi Ballabh is the appellant in this last case. 


The questions I am called upon to decide are (1) whether 
Roshan Din as a relative only can claim? and (2) whether 
the custom in Almora town admits of claims for pre-emption by 
Musalmans? There is also a third point which is whether Musal- 
mans in the same manner as Hindus are by the lew loci entitled 
to claim pre-emption. With regard to the first point, I have: 
already found that Roshan Din comes forward, in reality, as a 
sharer. As to the second point, I find, as far back as the Gth 
August, 1855, Sir Henry Ramsay in re Musammat Gulabi, 
widow of Rahmat-wllak v. Hargobind admitted the right of pre- 
emption in the town of Almora, 


There is also the case of Asgar Ali v. Haidar Ali decided on 
the 21st November, 1833, by the sadar amin, Again Madar 
Bakhsh v. Khuda Bakhsh, son of Kirdi, decided on the 5th Jar- 
uary, 1887, by the sadar amin, 


. fore dismiss tho appeal, bub under 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 67 ) 


‘These decisions show very clearly that the right of pre-emp- 
tion in the town of Almora has been admitted to exist, and that 
legal forms enjoined by the Shara Muhammadi have not in any 
of the cases been considered as applicable to the Musalmans 
residing in Almora, who are apparently in this respect guided 
by the rules applicable to Hindus. This disposes of the second 
and third points, I therefore hold that Roshan Din, Ali Bakhsh 
and Mujnu are entitled to the relief sought. 

In this appeal I decree to Roshan Din and Ali Bakhsh the 
right of pre-emption in repect to a 9 pie share on payment of 
Rs. 15 to Lachmi Bellabh within one month, In default ‘the 
sale to Lachmi Ballabh will hold good, Respondent will pay all 
the costs of the suib.

JOGA NAND anp AMBA DATT, Josuts, or MAUZA BERON, ALMORA 
(APPELLANTS-PLAINTIFFS) v. AMBA DATT AND 
SHIB DATT or DITTO 
(REBPONDENTS-DEFENDANTS). 
[Special civil appeal no. 4 of 1901..] 


Second appeal against an order of Mr. A. W. McNair, C.S., Deputy Com- 
missioner, Garhwal, dated the 15th Novembor, 1900. 


Pré-emption— Previous sale under condition reserving right of re-purchase —Claim 
of pre-emption by near relative of vendor. 

Certain land was sold in 1859 under a condition that in the event of a 
re-sale the thon vendor or his representatives should have the first refusal of 
the property Part of the land was sold in 1900 in accordance with this condi- 
tion toa descendant of the original vendor. The brother of the prosent vendor 
claimed pre-emption as a noar rolative and co-sharer. 


It was held that the contractual stipulation overrode and took priority of 
the ordinary rights of pre-emption. 


THE facts appear sufliciently from the jadgement’ of the 
court, 

Pandit Hari Ram for appellants ; and Pandit Badri Datt for 
respondents, 


ORDER BY Mr. R., E. HAMBLIN, COMMISSIONER, 

Both parties are prosent and appellant. is heard. This, 
is a second civil appeal. Plaintiff-appellant’s brother has sold 
his property to Amba Datt, defendant, and plaintiff-appellant 
has claimed pre-emption. The basis of the sale to defendant is 
that by virtue of a deed of sale of 1859 the property, part o f 
which is now in suit, wassold by defendant’s ancestor to plaintiff’s 
brother’s ancestor with the condition that in case the sale took 
place the defendant’s ancestor should have the first offer of the 
property, Part of the property was sold subject to this con- 
dition in 1882 and the sale was upheld in court, Plaintiff's 
brother has now sold another part to defendants, 

Plaintiff objocts to this purchase and claims pre-emption on 
the grounds (1) that Amba Datt is not a legitimate descendant 
of the original seller in 1859, and is not therefore entitled to 
the benefit of the condition in that contract regarding re-pur- 
chase, (2) that such condition regarding re-purchase does not 
override plaintiff's ordinary rights of pre-emption as & near 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 69 ) 


relation arising from Kumaun custom anl settlement wajrb-ul- 

arz (3) that admitting even that defendant has rights of re- 
purchase, those of pre-emption of the plainti Ps are paramount 
as he is a brother of the present seller anda cc-sħarer, whilst 
Amba Datt is neither a co-sharer nor a relation. 

The first ground of legitimacy is a question of fact which 
cannot be raised in second appeal. As regards the second and 
third grounds I am clearly of opinion that the rights of re- 
purchase given by the sale of 1859 are not restricted by any 
custom of pre-emption among co-sharers or near relations. The 
present seller to Amba Datt received this property through: his 
ancestors from the original vendor in 1859 subject to the 
condition that that vendor’s family should always have the first 
right to re-purchase, Any other rights of pre-emption that may 
be claimed are subordinate to this original right created by the 
deed of sale. ‘The appeal therefore is dismissed with costs. 


Before Mr, R. E. Hamblin, C. S., Commissioner.

Pre-emption—Procedure—Claim by hissadar in respect of proprietary right in a 
khaikari village, 
Held that a co-sharer (hissadar) has a right of pre-emption in rospect of 
the hissadari right in a wholly khaikari village as against a purchaser who is a 
khaikar of the village. 


In this case Gopal Singh, owner of the proprietary right 
over certain land in mauza Baina, a village held entirely by 


pakka khaikars, had sold his right to Narain Singh and Tok 


Singh, two of the pakka khaikars of the village. Pre-emption 
was claimed by Trilok Singh and Chanar Singh, two of the other- 
proprietors. Apart from other findings immaterial to the ques- 
tion of custom, it was held thata hissadar has a right of pre- 
emption against a pakka khaikar, who has purchased the pro- 
prietary right over his (or other’s) land in the village, 

Babu Din Dayal for appellants. 

ORDER BY Mr. R. E. HAMBLIN, COMMISSIONER, 

Parties were heard on the 29th- November, 1901. This isa 
civil second appeal. In the first court Trilok Singh and Chanar 
Singh sued Narain Singh and Tek Singh, purchasers, and Gopal 
Singh, vendor, claiming the right of pre-emption with respect to- 


the property sold by Gopal Singh to Narain Singh and Tek 
Singh, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(ile) 

The lower court framed the issues (1) had the plaintiff the 
first refusal of the land offered him and did he refnse it ? (2) if 
so, has he thereby forfeited his right to sue? (3) if he has such 
right, on what terms is he entitled to pre-emption? It will be 
noticed that in these issues the word “ plaintiff” is used and not 
“ plaintiffs,” The first court evidently regarded Trilok Singh us 
the real plaintiff aud representing Chanar Singh, as it was he to 
whom the property had been offered. The lower court enters in 
its judgement that Chanar Singh is the minor brother of Trilok 
Singh. The lower court found that Trilok Singh had been 
given the chance of purchase and had refused and so had lost 
his right of pre-emption and dismissed the suit with costs. 
The second court has given Chanar Singh a decree for pre- 
emption, on the ground that he had not been given the chance 
of purchase, and it also remanded the case to the lower court 
under section 562 to determine the price at which pre-emption 
should be allowed, ‘The first court has accordingly fixed a price 
and given a decree for pre-emption at this price. The proce- 
dure is incorrect. The second court should have framed an 
issue under section 566 and on receipt of the return on that issue 
should have passed its final decree. It was not able to remand 
the case under section 562 and at the same time modify the 
decree of the first court as it did. Thisis an illegality justify- 
ing second appeal. I must cancel the order remanding the case 
under section 5$2 so as to dispose of the decree of the first court 
which has been prepared in accordance therewith. I am not 
able at present to pass final orders in the case as the decision of 
the following issues is necessary before I can do so :— 

(1) Was Chanar Singh an adult or minor ab the time the 

offer of the property was made to Trilok Singh ? 

(2) Was Trilok Singh acting for himself alone or on behalf 
of Chanar Singhand possibly others of the family 
when he declined the purchase ? 

(3) If Chanar Singh was a minor at sucha time, did Trilok 
Singh always act on his behalf ? 


= (4) Was Chanar Singh a recorded hissadar ab tho time of 
| the offer, and, if not; has he any right of pre-emption 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 72 ) 
(5) In a village in which the hissadars* have no khud culti- 
vation, has a hissedar any right of pre-emption regard- 
ing a sale of hissadari right in which the vendee is a 
kbaikar in the village ? z 
(6) If Chanar Singh.has any right of pre-emption, on what 
terms should pre-emption be allowed ? 

The appeal is allowed because the order of tho second court 
is contary to law and because the decision of further issues is 
necessary befare final orders can be given. The case is returned 
to the second court under section 566 of the’ Civil Proceduro- 
Code for trial of the above issues and re-submission with the 
findings within two months. 


2nd May, 1902, 


The Deputy Commissioner has returned his findings and 


` appellant has been heard. The appellant has filed a list of 


objections which I overrule. The findings on my issues 1, 2, 3 
and 4 are findings of fact which I cannot alter. Finding on issue 
5 isone of law arid I agree with the decision, Finding on 
issue 6 is also one of fact, and though appellant objects to the 
evidence on which the Deputy Commissioner acted, he placed no 
other evidence before the Deputy Commissioner regarding the 
value of the right of pre-emption. 

My first order of the 9th December, 1901, allowed the appeal. 
My order cancelling the Deputy Commissioner’s decree allowed 
the decree of the first court to revive. I therefore now seb aside 
all previous decrees, and give Chanar Singh a decree for exercise

months from this date, and if he fails to pay, hisfZclaim for pre- 
emption stands dismissed with costs in all courts. Subject to

GAJADHAR JAYAL, MAUZA JHANJAR (APPELTANT-PLAINTIFE) Y. JORA, 
AND OTHERS, MAUZA KANDI (Res PoNDENTSs-DEFENDANTS). 
{Special civil appeal no, 10 of 1902.) 


Second appeal against an order of Mr. B. (. Allen, C. S., Deputy Commis. 
sioner, Garhwal, datod the 10th March, 1902.

Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


CA 


Proe- omption—= Waiver of claim on previous occasions—Object of the custom— 
Non-resident co-sharer. 

Failure to claim pro-emption when land is sold on one occasion dogs. not 
dobar a claim in respect of a subsequent sale of the same property. 

This follows froma consideration of the object of the custom, viz. to enable 
co-sharers to exclude undesirable outsiders. In view of the wording of the 
wajib-ul-arz in Garhwal, held that a non-resident co-sharer has no right of 
pre-emption. 

JoRA, the vendor, had sold 76 nalis of land in mauza Kandi 
to Kedaru, who was not a co-sharer in that village. Pre-emption 
was claimed by Gajadhar, who was a co-sharer of the village but 
resided in anothor village. His claim was dismissed on the basis 
of a provision in the Garhwal memorandum of village customs 
restricting pre- emption to resident co-sharers of the village. 

This ruling has been reforred to and not followed in respect of 
Almora and Naini Tal districts by Mr. J. S. Campbell in Zechi 
Negi v. Joga and another (q.v.). The circumstances of that case 
however were distinguishable from this case, the claim there 
being based on near relationship to the vendor. Mr. Campbell 
would also from his obiter. dicta dissent from the principle laid 
down in this case in Garhwal cases also. 

Babu Din Dayal for the appellant. 


ORDER BY Mr. R. E. HAMBLIN, COMMISSIONER. 
Parties were heard on the 18th June; this is a second civil 
appeal and can be heard on any of tho grounds given in sec- 
tion 584, Code of Civil Procedure. Plaintiff-appollant has 
claimed pre-emption with respect to a sale by Jora to Kedaru. 
The courts below have taken the view that as plaintiff did not 
claim pre-emption at the timo Jora purchased at auction he can 


‘have no right to pre-emption now when Jora sells, They have 


overlooked the fact that by tho ruling entered in Weekly Notes, 
page 115 of 1898, there can be no pre-emption with respect to 
a sale at auction by order of a court in oxccution of a civil court 
decree ; they have also ovorlooked the principle on which pro- 
emption is based. It does not follow that becauso X has no 
objection to a purchase by B from A that he will not have any 
objection to a purchase by C from B. Pre-emption is based on 
the necessity of giving co-sharers the power to exclude undesir- 
able new members of a proprietary body. X may have a liking 
10 


July 28, 


1902, 


x 


July 17, 
1909. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 74 ) 


for Band consider him desirable as a eo-sharer; he may have 

the strongest objection to O, and think him very undesirable as 
a co-sharer. The lower courts have taken the view that because 
aco-sharer once declines to exercise the right of pre-emption 

with respect to a particular property he is therefore uaable in 

any future year to claim pre-emption with respect to any future 

sale of that same property ; such a view is wrong. 

But the appellant cannot succeed is his appeal before me 
for his basis of claim is the wajib-ul-arz and this only gives a 
right of pre-emption to resident co-sharers. Plaintiff’s residence 
is not in the village, and he cannot therefore claim to be a resi- 
dent co-sharer. If appellant had asserted in his grounds of 
appeal that he was a resident co-sharer, I would have felt ib 
necessary to return this issue to the second court for decision; 
he has contented himself with asserting he has the right to pre- 
emption ; he has not objected to the finding of the first courb 
that he is non-resident, and so I dismiss the appeal with costs.

RABI DATT, BIDYA DATT, ARYA DATT or MAUZA THAPLT, PATTI KAPHOL-

Appeal against an order of Mr. V. A, Stowell, O.S., Deputy Commissioner, 
Garhwal, dated the 2nd January, 1909. 

Pe-omption—Claim by near relative of vendor but not resident co-sharer against 
resident co-sharer purchaser. ; 

Held that a noar relative of the vendor, who was also a co-sharer in the 
yillage but was non-resident, had no right of pre-emption, againsta resident 
co-sharer who was unrelated to the vendor. 

Ix this case the claimant of pre-emption was related to the 


vendor within the third degree and was a co-sharer in the village, 


oes ane OS Sn ne en

Go) 


bub was non-resident. Tho purchaser was a resident co-sharer 
in the village, being a-khaikar who bad previously purchased a 
proprietary share. 

The bar of non-residence was held to override the usual 
prior right of the near relative. ‘The land was ancestral. 

This ruling was subsequently reversed by Mr. Campbell

ORDER By Mr. J. S. CAMPBELL, COMMISSIONER, 


T see no reason to interfere with the Deputy Commission- 
er’s finding in this case. 

Appellants are non-residen: hissadars of a purely khaikari 
village. Respondent is not only one of the resident body of 
khaikars, but has already on a previous occasion acquired some 
hissadari rights by purchase. He is therefore a resident hissa- 
dar as well as a resident khaikar. I do not consider that 
appellants are entitled on the grounds of relationship to pre- 
emption in preference to respondent. 

I therefore uphold the Deputy Commissioner’s order and 


dismiss this appeal with costs.

ORDER BY Mr. J. S. CAMPBELL, COMMISSIONER. 

I am unable to concur with the Deputy Commissioner’s 
findings in the case which raises an important question whether 
@ non-resident co-sharer can never claim pre-emption, 

In this case Lachi, appellant, Gusain, respondent no. 2, and 
a third brother, Moti, are recorded as the joint co-sharers in an 
ancestral property. Gusain has sold his. share to Joga, a resi- _ 
dent co-sharer, and the question now in dispute is whether Lachi, — 
being admittedly non-resident can claim pre-emption as against 
Joga. I think that, according to the Kumaun custom he most 
undoubtedly can do so, The object of pre-emption is first of all 
to keep the family intact, and next to keep undesirable aliens 
out of the village community. But in my opinion much more 
importanco has to be attached to the first object than to the 
second, ~- 

There are of course numerous rulings to the effect that a 
distant relation, who is not a co-sharor, cannot claim pre-emption 
as against a co-sharer; but they do not affect the present case, 
where the three brothers hold a joint undivided khata. 

To insist that no one but a resident co-sharer can claim pre- 
emption would be, in my opinion, contrary to custom and would 
entail great hardship in many cases. No government servant 
or professional man*could ask for pre-emption during the time 
he was absont from his ancestral home; and no man with pro- 
perty in more than one villago could Bele for pre-emption excep b 
in tho one village in which ho resides, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(ES) 

The question is somewhat more difficult in Garhwal, owing 
to a referonce to the “ resident ” co-sharer having crept into the 
memorandum of village customs at Mr. Pauw’s scttlement, but 
even there there is no reference to any necessity for the nearest 
kinsman being a resident co-sharer, Any kiusman within the 
third generation who is a co-sharer has a preferential claim 
for pre-emption as against a resident co-sharer, as I read the 
memorandum. I am not prepared to accept the obiter dictum 
of Mr. Stowell that a man must be a resident cu-sharer before 
he can claim pre-emption. s 

Mr. Hamblins’ ruling in Gajadhar Jayal v. Jora that a non- 
resident co-sharer has no right of pre-emption is entirely based 
‘upon the Garhwal memorandum of village custom. 

But there is nothing in Mr. Becketts ikrarnama or Mr. 
Goudge’s memorandum of village custom to indicate the extension 
of this principle to the Almora and Naini Tal districts, and I 
find that ib is opposed to both local custom and public policy. ` 

I therefore reverse the order of the lower court in this case 
and order that if plaintiff-appellant pay Rs. 100 into court 
within three months of this dato, he shall be allowed pre-emption 
of the land sold by respondent no, 2 to respondent no. 1. 

Respondent no. 1 will pay appellant’s costs in all courts.

ORDER By Mr. J. S. CAMPBELL, COMMISSIONER. 

T am unable to agree with the findings of tho lower courts 
in this.case. Appellant is own brother to the vendor, and also a 
hissadar in the village in his own right. As such (for the reasons

ORDER BY MR. J. S. CAMPBELL, COMMISSIONER. 


I am unable to agree with the findings of the lower courts 
in this case, 


The village of (old) Latauli was acquired by Government 
for public purposes, and as an act of grace Government gave the 
dispossessed village community a nayabad grant, including the 
land now in dispute, upon which to re-establish themselves : and 
they transferred themselves there bodily and called the grant 
“Naya Latauli.’ No new record of village customs was 
recorded (ib is not usual) for the nayabad granb which was 
assessed to revenue by summary settlement. In old Latauli the 
custom of pre-emption existed and was recorded (as elsewhere in 
the Almora district) in the settlement papers. I consider that 
tho status of the village community was exactly the same in new 
Latauli as ib had been in old Latauli, and that therefore the 
hissadars were entitled to resort to pre-emption to keep outsiders 
from entry into the nayabad grant. The fact that no pre-emp- 


tion was claimed in respect of certain recent sales does nob debar 


pre-emption in the present case. Moreover the period of limita- 
tion for pre-emption had not expired when the deputy collector 
was trying the present case, and it may have been claimed since 
his judgement was delivered. 

I quash the order of the lower court, and order, that if plain- 
tiffs pay Rs, 99 into court within one month, they shall be 
entitled to pre-empt the land in dispute, and receive their costs 
in all courts,

PARJAPATI NAITHANI or MAUZA KOLA, PATTI KHATSYUN, GARmwAL 

DISTRICT (APPELLANT) v. (1) KIRPAL SINGH, (2) DIWAN SINGH or 

pitro (8) TEJ RAM NAITAANI oF MAUZA Jaxa, PATTI KAPHOLSYUN, 
GARHWAL DISTRICT (RESPONDENTS), 


[Special civil appeal no, 79 of 1913.} 


Appoal against an order of ‘Mr, J. M. Clay, O.S., Deputy Oommissioner, 
Garhwal, dated the 28th July, 1913. 


Pre-emption—Non-ancestral land—Claim by near relative of vendor and 

co-sharer in the village but non-resident—Proof of custom. 

Held that in rospect of non-ancestral land a near rolative of the vendor 
(within the third degree) who is a co-sharer in the village but is non-rosident 
therein, has no right of pre-emption against a purchaser who is a resident co- 
sharer, but not a relativo of tho vendor. A plaintiff seeking pro-emption on 
the basis of an alleged custom must prove the existence of that custom. 

In this case Parjapati, plaintiff, was the first cousin of Tej 
Ram, who sold certain land in mauza Kola, apparently self- 
acquired (though this point does not appear to have been 
clearly decided), to Kedar Singh and Diwan Singh, who were 


_also co-sharers in the village, 


The plantiff claimed pre-emption as a relative within the third 
degree, but resided in another village, 

The defendants contended that the prior right of a near 
relative did not extend to non-ancestral lund and that a non-resi- 
dent co-sharer had no right of pre-emption. 

The first court decreed the claim on the ground of propin- 
quity of relationship. 

The first appellate court reversed this decision holding that 
a claimant for pre-emption must be a resident co-sharer. The 
Commissioner following a decision of Mr. Campbell’s (Narain 
Datt v. Bhola Datt) held that a near relative need not be a 


_ resident co-sharer to be entitled to pre-emption. 


On a reference by the Local Government to the High Court 
that Court returned the opinion reproduced below and the case 
was remanded to the Kumaun courts for further enquiry on the 
point of custom and decision and the ruling now cited is the 
final order in the case, 

The case is a Garhwal one and agrees with a ruling of Sir 
Henry Ramsay’s (Bali v, Ainlanand, September 18, 1873) quoted 
by Mr, Pauw, of which the record is now not forthcoming, In 


epee 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 81 ) 
that caso Sir Henry Ramsay laid it down that anear relative 
cannot, as such, claim pre-emption in non-ancestral land. 


In other cases (cf. Gajadhar v. Jora and another, Lachi Negi 
v. Joga and another) the question whether the land sold was 
ancestral or non-aucestral did not arise ; the land was in fact 
ancestral. In Narain Datt v.’Bhola Datt and others however 
the exact contrary to the present ruling was laid down by Mr. 
Campbell in 1913. 


Mr. D. R. Sawhny, counsel for .Kirpal Singh; and Mr, 
Mr. Lakshmi Narain, vakil for Parjapati Naithant. 

OPINION oF THE Hien Court (Sır Henry RICHARDS, KT., 

C.J., AND TUDBALL, J.), DATED THE 4TH FEBRUARY, 1915. 


This is a reference under rule 17 of the Kumaun rules. It 
appears that a suit was instituted for pre-emption. It may be 
taken that plaintiff alleged that thero was a custom of pre-emp- 
tion under which he was entitled to get the property on payment 
of the consideration. He was a co-sharer in the mahal 
and he was also a relative of the vendor, but he was non- 
resident in the village. It may be taken that the defendant 
pleaded that he also wasa co-sharer, that he was resident in 
the village, and that the property sold was non-ancestral, 
and that under these circumstances tho plaintiff was not 
entitled to a decree for pre-emption of the property. The 
courts have found that the plaintiff was a relative and a 
co-sharer, but that he wasa “ non-resident,” The courts have 
also found that the defendant was  co-sharor, that he was a 
“resident ” bubthab he was no relative. They have also found in 
favour of the plaintiff that the vendor did not before sale offer 
the property to the plaintiff. The courts framed no issue and did 
not find whether there was any Village custom of pre-emption 
which affected non-ancestral land, nor have they found whether 
under such a custom a non-resident co-sharer, who is a relative, 
has a right of pre-emption against a co-sharer who is & aes 
although not a relative. In our opinion it lies upon ® pe 
secking pre-emption on the basis of an alleged custom to press 
oxistence of the custom and to bring himself within 1% In the 
present caso ib would rather seem that both sides admitted that 
11 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 82 ) 


there was some right of pre-emption existing amongst the 
co-sharers in the village. 

The difference between them was that the vendee said that 
the custom did not extend to non-ancestral land, and that the 
plaintiff being non-resident had no right against him, a resident, 
We think that the courb ought to have framed issues upon these 
two questions and the decision should be in accordance with the 
findings thereon, The decree of the Commissioner therofore is 
open to objection under the provisions of section 100, clause (c) 
of the Code of Civil Procedure. 


This is our answer to the reference. We think that the costs 
of both parties should be costs in the case and abide the result, 


ORDER. By Mr. P. WYNDHAM, COMMISSIONER, 
DATED THE 27TH JULY, 1915, 


This case has been referred to this court for a finding on 
certain new issues and a decision in accordance therewith. 


The petitioner to the Local Government was respondent in 
this court where it was held that the appellant (the opposite 


party), though a non-resident, had a right of pre-emption as a 
relative. 


The following additional issues haye been framed:— 


(1) Is there any village custom of pre-emption which 
affects non-ancestral lands in this village ? 

(2) Under such custom has a non-resident co-sharer, who is 
a relative, aright of pre-emption against a resident 
co-sharer who is not a relative-? 


These issues were sent tothe court of the Deputy Commis- 
sioner for an enquiry and finding, 


The learned Deputy Commissioner finds— 


(1) there is a custom of pre-emption affecting non-ancestral 
lands in mauza Kola, patti Khatsyun ; 

(2) under this custom a non-resident co-sharer, who is a 
relative, has no right of pre-emption against .a resi- 
dent co-sharer who is not a relative, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 8 ) 

I have heard the opposite party against whom these findings 
operate. I see no reason to differ from the lower court in its 
findings on this issue. 

The result is that the opposite party—the appellant—in this 
court fails in his appeal which is accordingly dismissed with 
costs,

Temple lands Alienation by mahant—Incumbranoe deolared void.

Before Mr. H. Ross, Commissioner. 


BACHUA LOHAR or LADHOLI, PATTI Daron, AMORA DIBTRIOT. 
(APPELLANT) V. RAMUA, RATUA snp RATNUA oF DITTO 
RESPONDENTS. 

{Revonuo Appeal no. 111 of 1887.] 

Padhanchari land—Claim of rights adverse to the padhan. 
Teld that in padhanochari land no ono can acquire rights adyorse to the 
padhan ; each padhan must succeed to it unencumbered, 


November 19 
880. 


September 12, 


1887. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 88 ) 


Tho village headman (padhan or malguzar) in the hills usually holds a 
certain area of Government land ront (or revenue) free as remunoration or 
part remuneration for his duttes where this area is small he receives cash dues 
as well. f 

Claims to proprietary right in such land by adverso possession or by long 
possession in a padhan’s family or to occupancy right by tenants are sometimes 
raised but never successfully. 

In this case defendants were recorded as tenants in this 
land under plaintiff (who alleged they had dispossessed him) and 
claimed permanent tenancy rights in it. 

A similar ruling, where the heirs of a line of ex-padhans 
claimed to continue to hold the land was given by Colonel Grigg, 
Commissioner, in Nand Lal v. Musammat Dhram Sundari and 
others, Bhaltgaon, Talla Giwar (nob reproduced on the 14th 
May, 1895. 


—— 


ORDER BY Mr. H. Ross, COMMISSIONER. 


The order of the lower court as regards the second class of 
land, i.e. the undivided property of the community in it, it was 
correct to uphold the possession of the respondents. The posses- 
sion of the respondents should also have been upheld in the 
third class otherwise trouble is likely to arise. 5 

As regards the padhanchari land, no one can acquire rights 
in such land adverse to the padhan. The land is Government 
land given to a padhan for the performance of certain duties 
and each succeeding padhan must succeed to this land unencum- 
bered when he succeeds to his padhanchariship. He can then, 
if ho likes, give it oub to tenants for the full period of his 
padbanchariship, At his death and if he is dismissed the rights 
of these tenants lapse and the new padhan must obtain the land ` 
free of any rights by others. 

In this case there is no proof that appellant gave the defen- 
dants a cultivating lease and made them sirtans, and although 
long possession no doubt points to some understanding between 
the parties, still in the absence of any express agreement I think 
it safer to uphold the padhan. I therefore reverse this part 
of the order of the lower court and direct that the padhanchari 
land be made over to tho padhan, the appellant, Parties will 
bear their own eosts,
