( 89 ) 


Before the Board of Revenue. 
Messrs, J. J. LUMSDEN AND J. R. REID, MEMBERS. 
JASODHAR AND OTHERS or Guanewt, PATTI TALLA SYUNABA, 


ALMORA DISTRICT (PETITIONERS) v. KAMLI AND OTHERS OF 
DITTO 
(OPPOSITE PARTY). 
` Village common land—Cultivation by majority of shareholders against 
the will of minoriiy—Inhibitory injunction—Partition. 

Where the majority of tho shareholders in village oommon land had com- 
menced cultivating it against the wish of the minority and wished to haye 
partition effected, it was held on a suit by tho minority that common waste 
land, so long as itis undivided, cannot be brought under cultivation, except 
with the consent of all the co-sharers, but that partition can be effeoted on the 
application of the majority, who can then obtain separate possession of their 
shares. y 

In this case the village common land (gaon sanjait) had 
been kept waste for pasture. One party of the shareholders had 
commenced to cultivate it and on objection by the other party 


(the minority) who sued to restrain them, the former party 


desired partition. 


The minority were held to be entitled to restrain the others 
from cultivating the land, so long as it remained undivided, but 
the majority were held to be entitled to claim partition of their 
shares to enable them’to cultivate them when separated. 


— 


ORDER BY THE BOARD OF REVENUE. 
Messrs. J. J. Lumsden and J. R. Reid, Members. 


The wording of the settlement engagement regarding the 
cultivation of waste land held in common by the khaikars seems 
to be that when the khaikars desire to cultivate the common land 
all the shareholders shall divide itby arrangement among them- 
selves (hamare gaon men jo anbanta zamin hai jab kamana 
chanenge to apas ke salah se sub hissadar guon bant bewenge + 

The lower courts have held that the desire of the majority 
of the shareholders in number and interest is sufficient to give 
effect to the engagement above quoted. After hearing what the 
appellant has to say I see no sufficient reason to dissent from the 
decision of the lower courts as to tho principle upon which 
division of the common land may be applied for and carried out 
and in respect to it, this appeal is dismissed, , 

12 


July 18, 
1891. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 90 ) 


But the agreement doesnot, in my opinion, authorize even the 
majority of tho shareholders to take any part of the common 
land and bring it under cultivation without the consent of all the 
shareholders who have an interest in the land. Shareholders, 
being supported by a majority who desire to be put in separate 
possession of part of the common land must, if the whole body 
of shareholders do not consent to their separate occupation of 
part of the common land, apply for a partition of the common 
land to the district authorities. 

In so far therefore as the decrees of the lower courts uphold 
the possession of the respondents upon the land specified in the 
plaint, from the cultivation of which the plaintiffs sought to 
restrain the defendants, these decrees are modified, and until a 
formal partition is effected on the application of the defendants, 
they cannot be permitted to assume separate possession of any 
part of the common land. 

Each party to this appeal will bear its own costs. 


(Sd.) J. R. REID, 
Junior Member. 
30-6-1891. 


I concur. 


(Sd.) J. J, LUMSDEN, 
7-7-1891. Senior Member.

DULAP SINGH, HAR SINGH ann BAGSA SUNAR of MAUZA Yana © 
(APPELLANTS-DEFENDANTS) v. RAM SINGH, J1T SINGH anp LAL SINGH 
, (RESPONDENTS-PLAINTIFTS). 
[Revenue Regular Appeal.] 
TA Village common land—Partition—Division equally by families (Mawari Bant). 
payed The custom known as Mawari Bant (partition of the common land equally 
among all families of co-sharers) was enforced in this case on the basis of’ the 
village records and of & precedent order of Mr. Becketts, 
Tue usual principles of apportionment of rights on parti- 
tion in the village common lands (gaon sanjait) in the hills is in 


proportion to the share of the village land reyenue paid by each 


co-sharer (i.e, hissa shavah or rakam sharah). In certain 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


Eo 


villages a peculiar local custom is recorded or alleged known as 

Mawari Bant (from “mao” or “muwasa” a family); by this 

custom each family among the co-sharers receives an equal share - 
in the common land, whatever their separate proprietary holding 

may be. The custom is seldom proved, and this ruling is repro- 

duced as an instance of its actual enforcement and of the evidence 

on which it was allowed. (In villages where it prevails the settle- 

ment records show, or should show, the recognized “families” 

of the village, a point otherwise open to considerable dispute.) 


The lower court’s (Mr. H. Shaw, Deputy Collector) order is 
also reproduced as setting out the facts of the cage more clearly 
than that of the Commissioner. It ran as follows :— 


The question I am required to decide is whether should 
sanjait land be divided equally according to families, i.e. 
mawari, or whether should it be divided among the co-sharers 
in the proportion to the revenue they pay. The co-sharers are 
divided into two parties, the one claim mawari and the other 
hissa sharah. Both have produced proof ; that adduced by defen- 
dants amounts to nothing, it consists of two judgements by Mr. © 
Traill, which have no bearing on the present question, a third by 
the late Commissioner Sir Henry Ramsay, and that also has no 
bearing on the present case, and finally a memorandum prepared 
by the malguzar which is worthless as evidence or authority. 
The plaintiffs by the phant and the tehriz and also by a judge- 
ment of Mr. Beckett's, all relating to the village in which the 
land to be divided lies, prove that Mawart Bant is the custom of 


this village. 
Decree therefore is given in plaintiff’s favour with costs.

DURGA, DEB RAM, AMARI anp SHIBU, or mauza PORHARISAIN 
(APPELLANTS-DEFENDANTS) V. JAI SINGH anp LACHHAM SINGH 
OF DITTO (RESPONDENTS-DEFEND ANTS). 


(Revenue Regular Appeal no. 14 of 1891-92.) 


August 22, Village common land—Disability of o0-sharer to create occupancy ( khaikari) 
1892. right in his share— Partition — Inability of tenant to claim partition against pro- 
prietors, 


A co-sharer in common land cannot oreato khaikari right in his undivided 
share of such land; nor can the tenant seeking to enforce such a right claim 
partition against the joint proprietors. 

THE facts of the case appear sufficiently from the judgement. 
The principles laid down concern both the civil and the revenue 

courts, They do not declare any local custom in variation of 
the normal rules of law, but the ruling is included as illustrating 
local conditions in the hills. 


ORDER BY Mr, D. T. ROBERTS, COMMISSIONER. 


By registered deed on the 23rd August, 1887, Jit Singh created 
Jai Singh his khaikar for the area in his hissa kabza in Pokhari- 
sain and also included his share in the common land. The com- 
mon land is 6675 nalis and now Jai Singh sues the other co- 
sharers of the village for separation and possession of his share in 
this common land as khaikar under Jit Singh. 

The land is waste and Jai Singh admits he is notin possession. 

A suit of this nature is inadmissible. 

A khaikar is a tenant and a khaikari created by deed is not 
established until possession of the land is given by the hissadar 
who gives the land, 

No co-sharer can create a khaikari right in common land, 
because the khaikar’s right must be in some definite area and: 
nob in an unseparated share of wasteland, Again, partition can 
only be demanded by co-sharers and not by tenants in common 
waste land. Until Jit Singh or his representative has partitioned 


4 
| 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 93 ) 
his share in the common waste his deed conferring khaikari 
right on Jai Singh is inoperative and Jai Singh cannot maintain 
a suit for partition against the other co-sharers on the basis of 
that deed. 


The appeal is granted and the suit is dismissed with costs. 


Before Mr.D. T. Roberts, I.C.S., Commissioner. 


SWARU Anp JAI RAM OF MAUZA Mason GARHWAL 
(APPELLANTS-DEFENDANTS) V. BHAWANU oF 


MAUZA Marrana (RESPONDENT-PLAINTIFE). 
è [Revenue Regular Appeal no. 79 of 1891-92.) 


Village common land — Waiver of right—Loss of share by dispossession. Ones T, 
1892. 
Where a recorded co-sharer in common waste land (measured) allowed the 


other co-sharers to reclaim and cultivato the whole area and to continuo in 
possession of it for over 12 yoars without in any way asserting his rights, it was 
held that he had lost all title to his share. 


THE measured village common lands (gaon sanjatt) in the 
hills are often culturable waste, which has never been broken up 
or cultivated. In such cases they were not assessed to land 
revenue at settlement. These had been the condibions in the 
village in question in this suit. The appellants-defendants had 
after the previous settlement, about 30 years before, reclaimed 
and improved the common waste at considerable labour and 
expense and had held exclusive possession of it for many years. 
Sur Das, who was blind, had neither contributed towards the 
reclamation of the land nor ever held or claimed possession of 
his share. He was the appellants’ brother. He gifted most of 
his property, including his recorded share of the common land, to 
plaintiff-respondent, his nephew, before his death. = 

The lower court held that the common land was joint 
property of all the brothers, that Sur Das could have claimed 
partition of his recorded share ab any time during his lifetime, 
and that hisheir was still entitled to such share; the facts of 
actual possession were nob material to the case. The Commis- 
sioner however, in view of the circumstances of the pase” 
(which are not uncommon in the hills) reversed the lower courts 
order, 


May 18, 
3 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(94) 


ORDER BY Mr. D. T. ROBERTS, COMMISSIONER. 

This is a claim to one-fourth of the sanjait land, appertain- 
ing to the-share of Sur Das and is claimed by the respondent as 
heir of Sur Das under a deed of gift. 

The defendants object that Sur Das was never in possession of 
this sanjait land, that they alone broughb it under cultivation 
and have been in possession of it, The entry of land as common 
does not necessarily imply thateach sharer in the village has an 
equal right to a proportional share in it under all circumstances. 
Each sharer has a right to break up and bring under cultivation 
his share of the common land, but it does not follow that if he 
waives that right and allows the other sharers to cultivate the 
land he can at any time reclaim his share, and specially is this 
the case in unassessed land. 

In the present case Sur Das has expressly been found by the 
lower courb to have never been in possession of any portion of 
the sanjait land, and this means dispossession for more than 12 
years, T therefore hold that his successor cannot now claim to 
dispossess the other sharers who have broken up the land and 
have always been in cultivating possession. 

I therefore grant the appeal and dismiss the plaintiff's suit 
with costs. 


Before Mr. D. T. Roberts, I.C.S., Commissioner.

KUNA SAH MAKHALIA or Bacesar KATYOOR (APPELLANT-DEFENDANT) 
v PREMUA RAWAL or DITIO (RESPONDENT-PLAINTIFF). 
[Civil Appeal no. 3 of 1894 ] 
Appeal against an order of Mr. E. K. Pauw, C.B., Assistant Commiasioner, 
Almora, dated the 5th December, 1893. 
Temple lands—Sale by person holding propristary right on a service tenure. 

ed real In a village assigned tothe Hitpanteswar temple, Birua, a priest of the 
3 temple, was recorded as propriotor of certain land in his capacity as pricat, i,e. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 9 ) 
on a sorvice tenure, He sold this to Kuna Sah, a bania. Ona suit by Premua, 
the son of Birua, who was also a priest, it was held that a recorded co-sharer in 


a muafi village is entitled to do what he likes with his share: the custodians 
of the temple can sue for the income (siel). 


In villages assigned to temples (gunth lands) the temple 
stands in the place of Government, and the land is held either 
(a) by priests or other. functionaries who render service to the 
temple in lieu of payment or (b) by cultivators who pay the 
revenue or other dues to the temple; in either case the land- 
holder is commonly recorded as a proprietor (hissadar). 

There are various rulings regarding the power or disability 
of the recorded proprietor to alienate such lands, and it has 
generally been held that land held on a service tenure cannot be 
alienated. 

In this case Birua was a rawal, or hereditary priest of the 
Hitpanteswar temple, and as such he held gunth land ona 
service tenure. He mortgaged this to Kuna Sah who subse- 
quently sued for foreclosure and obtained a simple money 
decree. He got the land avtached and then arranged a private 
sale with Birua. ; 

Premua, the son, sued to set aside the sale on the ground of 
the inalienability of the tenure. It appears from the lower 
court’s order that he was already a priest himself and he sued in 
that capacity, but did not raise any plea of his rights as a son. 
The lower court following previous rulings (cf. Padamgir v. 
Dulanath and another) decreed the suit, but the Commissioner, 
ruling as above, dismissed it. 

The ruling is reproduced not on its merits as & ruling bub 
(a) because ib is a ruling and has been quoted as such to support 
sales of gunth lands held on service tenures and (b) to show 
from its wording that it is really irrelevant to the question of 
the alienation of service tenures, and to illustrate the mistakes 
into which courts inexperienced in local conditions are apt to 
fall, — be Se 

It is quite clear from the wording of the Commissioner 8 
order thab he supposed the tenure to be one simply paying 
revenue or dues to the temple, and ignored or failed to appre- 
hond the fact that the landholder paid nothing ab all but held 
the land as remuneration for serving as w price. 

13 


March 
1896. 


(S 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 98 ) 


The judgement itself shows that it cannot be quoted as laying 
down the principle that a priest holding temple land ona 
service tenure can alienate it to a person incapable of perform- 
ing such service. (See Commentary.) 


Pandit Hari Ram for appellant. 


ORDER BY COLONEL Ei. GRIGG, COMMISSIONER. 


The only question to decide is, whether the vendor Birua can 
sell his recorded share in this land. Birua is the father of 
Premua, respondent. The power to sell was virtually fought 
out in the execution of decree case. 


The Fard Tasdiki shows the seller is a recorded sharer in 
this muafi village, and is therefore entitled to do what he likes 
with his share, 


There is no doubt, I think, that the income from the lands 
goes or is intended to go to the support of the temple. If this 
is so, and of this there is no absolute documentary proof, then 


“the custodians of the temple are ab liberty to sue for the 


jncome. 


I accept this appeal and, reversing the order of the lower 
court, dismiss the claim with costs,

AMBA DATT JOSHI anD BACHI SINGH BHANDARI (DEFENDANTS- 
RESPONDENTS) V. DAULAT SINGH BHANDARI or MAUZA 


MELDUNGBI (PLAINTIFF-APPELDANT). 
[North-Western Provinces High Court’s Miscellaneous no. 128 of 1895.1] 


Temple lands (ganth)}—Service tenure— Gift by recorded co-sharer—Alleged 
perversion of cndowment— Capacity to alienate, 


In the village of Meldungri, which was assigned to the Brahmari temple, 
defendant Bachi Singh was recorded as a co-sharer. He gifted half his share 
to one Amba Datt. Plaintiff illeged that the land was held on condition of 
making offerings 4nd performing certain services for the temple, and that the 
dovor’s title was not transferable; the object of the endowment would be 
dofeated, if alienations were permitted. After an inquiry into tho tenuro and 
customs it was keld that such holders of templo lands could by custom alionate 


` 


ae 


+ s 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(9 ) 


their rights. It was not proved that the conditions of the endowment would be 
broken by the transfer. 

THE outline of the facts appears from the headnote above, 
In temple villages of the class in question the temple stands in 
the place of Government and the villagers are recorded as 
proprietary co-sharers ; in some of these villages they pay the 
assigned revenue assessment or other dues to the temple, and in 
others they hold on a service tenure, performing certain services 
for the templein lieu ofa money payment. In this case it 
appears that the donor held his land on condition of making 
certain offerings and perfurming certain services for the temple, 
It was contended that these services included certain menial acts 
which a Brahman, such as Amba Datt, donee, could not perform. 
The lower court however found that a Brahman would not be 
disqualified from performing these services, That court how- 
ever felt itself bound by previous rulings [cf. Dulanath v. 
Padamgir (q, v.)] to hold that gunth lands held on service tenure 
could not be alienated ; it found however that other co-sharers — 
in these gunth villages had customarily alienated their lands, 


The Commissioner, Colonel Grigg, held that there was 
nothing to show that the income of the land would not be devoted 
to the temple as heretofore or that the transferee would not 
perform the services required or that the temple would suffer in 
any way; asharer in a muafi village could by custom dispose of 
his rights (see his order reproduced below). He therefore upheld 
the transfer and dismissed the suit. On ao reference by the Local 
Government to the High Court a remand enquiry was ordered 
into the facts of the case on certain issues. The report of 
Colonel Grigg on these issues is reproduced below, and the High 
Court on this gave their opinion in favour of dismissing the suit ; 
the Local Government passed orders accordingly. : 

From a study of this ruling it will be seen that the principles 
laid down do not conflict with those of earlier rulings in any 
material degree. It is only ruled that co-sharers On & eat 
tenure in gwnth lands can alienate their rights, where suc 
alienation will not interfere with the service to be performed for 
the temple or where the object of the endowment will not be 
defeated by the transfer. It is clear thab the principles of tho 


ey 
if y 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 100 ) 


decision would not apply to uphold a transfer by a Brahman 
priest, holding on condition of priestly service, to an outcaste 
who could not approach the temple at all. This would involve a 
perversion of the endowment and of the conditions of the tenure. 
and would be clearly distinguishable from the present case. 

The following were the various orders passed in the case :— 

Order by Colonel Grigg, dated July 10, 1894. 

Remand report by Colonel Grigg, dated November 9, 1895. 

Reply by the High Court, dated February 6, 1896. 

Order of the Local Government, dated March 6, 1896. 


— 


ORDER BY CoLoneL E. E, FRIGG, COMMISSIONER, 
DATED THE 10TH JULY, 1894. 


` The land has not been alienated from the purpose for which 
it was granted. There are certain recorded sharers in the 
phant or malguzar’s record. One of them is Bachi, who has 
gifted half of his recorded share to Amba Datt. Plaintiff, who is 
a co-sharer, objects to this transfer. The muali grant of this 
village to the co-sharers is not on the file. : 

The record at the foot of the phant is: “the entire village 
is the gunth of the Brahmari temple; ” from this it would 
seem that the land was granted to the co-sharers for the service of 
this temple. There is nothing to show tkat the income arising 
from these lands will not be devoted to the temple as heretofore 
or that the person to whom the land is gifted will not perform the 
services required, or that the temple will suffer in any way by 
this gift. 

It has always been held that a sharer in a muafi village can. 
dispose of his rights. It remains for the custodians of' the 
temple to show that any of the conditions attaching to the sanad 
will be broken by this transfer. No attempt has been made to 
prove or show this. I accordingly accept this appeal and, 
reversing the order of the lower court, dismiss the claim with 
costs of both courts.

Tb is needless to point out that the term “kabzadar” has 
reference solely to persons being in possession. It is not to be 
construed as meaning the kabzadar of the North-Western Prov- 
inces. The village had been waste, but when the muafi lists 
were prepared in 1859, I think, the cultivated area, or ab any 
rate the area which was assigned for the purposes of the temple 
of Nanda Devi, or rather“ the goddess bearing that name, was

certain revenue. This for the above purposes was fixed ab 
Rs. 60. An Singh was recorded the malguzar. The Settlement

are no khaikars (i.e. occupancy holders corresponding somewhat 
to the term kabzadar in the North-Western Provinces). Phant,

The reply to the first issue is, the nature of the endow mens 
revenue-free land assigned to a temple ani recorded as being in 
the propriotary po session of cortain persons among whom at the 
present time are plaintiff Daulat Singh and Bashi Singh. 

The second issue is, what is the nature of the plaintiff’s title 
to the land in his occupation? Has he a proprietary right, or is 
he merely a tenant or licensee of the mahant of the temple ? 

The plaintiff’s counsel says there is no mahant of the temple, 
i.e., there is no recognized or established mahant, but he claims 
for his client that the latter does these duties, This is-denied by 
the defendants who urge that Ganga Datt performs the office 
of pujari when his services are required, and for this office a 
certain village has been assigned to him, and although Daulat 
asserts he collects the offerings, this is stoutly denied by Shib 
Singh, who is now the malguzar of the village. The evidence 
goes clean against Daulat Singh’s contentions, and moreover, it 
is very unlikely the malguzar or lambardar would for a moment 
allow Daulat Singh to exercise an office which had been assigned 
to him (Shib Singh) by the district authorities. Plaintiff’s title 
is set forth inthe khewat. He isa proprietor pure and simple 
and not a tenant or lessee of the mahant, 

ə. What is the nature of the title of the donor? Was his 
right proprietary? Was hea tenant or licensee of the mahant 
of the temple? 

Here again the khewat is the answer. The donor is a ro- 
corded sharer or proprietor. But plaintiff says. .Iam in

dant. The mere fact that defendant resides like many other 
proprietors of these hills in another village cannot annul his

doubt, be fought out sooner or later when this reference is 
decided, : 

But this claim refers to issue 4, Has the plaintiff any, and

of the gift ?

Beyond the bare statement to support which plaintiff has 
now produced two witnesses that he is in possession of the land, 
there is no proof. This claim is denied by the other side and 
supported by the evidence of the malguzar. The defendant is 
the recorded proprietor of the share he has gifted and the plaintiff 
has no recorded title to oppose the defendant’s claim. As 
regards his right to maintain this suit he has none, so far as I 
can see, and this, I think, is self-evident from what has been 
admitted by him in connection with issue 5. Is there any 
general usage in the district or particular usage in the village 
allowing or prohibiting an alienation of lands of this descrip- 
tion ? 

Not only does the plaintiff admit having sold certain lands to 
a man called Kunwar, who is said to, be his father-in-law, but the 
evidence shows there have been other alienations, and, moreover, 
in his finding Mr. Pauw points out that of the 11 villages which 
have been assigned to the Bhamari Devi five have merely the 
bands assigned, i.e. there is no revenue assessed on them, Mel- 
dhung is one of the latter. Of these five in no less than four 
have shares been either sold or gifted. He proved in fact that 
the custom of transfer did exist and was only deterred from 
dis missing the claim by the order of Sir Henry Ramsay passed 
in 1890 in respect to gunth land. 

The defendant’s counsel is ready to file other documents in 
support of transfer of such lands, but there appears no necessity 
for this.

JAIKISHEN, INDRA DEV LACHMI DUTT anD LILADHAR JOSHI or 


DANIA (MORTGAGORS) (APPELLANTS) v. Kirpan DATT PANT or DITTO,

i (Settlement Appeal no. 28 of 1902-03.] 


Second appeal against an order of Mr, J. E. Goudge, 0.8., Settlement 
Officer, Almora, dated the 14th Noyember, 1912, 


Unmeasured land— Extension of cultivation by possessory morlgagee— 
Resultant title. : 

Where a possessory mortgagee, being himsolf also a co-sharer in the village, 

extended his cultivation of the mortgaged land into adjoining Government 
waste, held that he was cntitled to proprietary right in such extensions against 
the mortgagors. 

THE facts of the case are that the respondent held a usu- 
fructuary mortgage of appellants’ separately held land in Dania 
village. By customary right in the hills a co-sharer is allowed 
to extend his cultivation into adjoining unmeasured (Goyern- 
ment) waste subject to executive regulation, and is given 
proprietary right over such extensions at the next settlement, 

In this case the respondent made extensions of cultivation 
in waste land adjoining that held by him on mortgage, At 
settlement it was claimed by the mortgagors that these extensions 
should be considered as appertaining to their holding and sbould 
be recorded as their proprietary land with the mortgages as a 
tenant, Tho Settlement Officer disagreeing with the Settlement 
Deputy Collector rejected their claim. ‘Ihe mortgagee was him- 
self a co-sbarer in the village, 


7 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 105 ) ee 


[Nors. — Questions relating to extensions of cultivation in unmeasured land 
are being reserved to the revenue courts, but under the rules Yegulating proce- 
dure where such questions arise in a civil suit, similar points may ariss in 
civil suits on mortgages: hence this ruling is included, for reference. ] 


ORDER By Mr. J. S. C. Davis, COMMISSIONER. 

Appellants were heard on the 13th January. The principal 
point is whether extensious of cultivation;made by possessory 
mortgagees should belong to them or to, the ‘mortgagors, I 
think there canbe no doubt that the decision’of the ,Settleoment 
Officer, whereby such extensions are recorded in ‘the’ name of 
the mortgagee, is correct. . 

The appellants (mortgagors) can only acquire rights in 
unmeasured land by cultivating if themselyes or through 
their sirtan tenants. The respondent (mortgagee) being a 
hissadar in his own right in addition to being mortgagee of 
the appellant’s share is fully entitled to extend his cultivation 
of the mortgaged share and to have such extonsion recorded in 
his own name. 

The appeal is dismissed with costs.

RAWAL GANESH LING or KEDARNATH'TEMPLE (APPELEANT) V. THE 
PANDAS OF KEDARNATH (RESPONDENTS ) 
[Special Civil Appeal no. 17 of 1905.] 

Appeal against an ordor,of Mr. V. A. Stowell, O.S., ‘Deputy Commissioner, 
Garhwal, dated the 17th July, 1905. 

Claim.—To set aside tho decree declaring raspondent-plaintifis, right to 
enter the temple of Kedarnath as pandas with thoir jajmans and to receive ] 
and retain gifts mide to them by way of shankalap, whether dolivery be maido 
inside-or outside the templo. : 

Kedarnath temple --Right of pandas to enter temple—Rightito sue—Distinetion 
between gifts and offerings—Right of pandas*to retain gifts. 

Where tho rawal of Kedarnath had obtained an order of the/District 
Magistrate prohibiting tho pandas (tho porsonal priests of the’ pilgrims) from 
entering the temple with their clients, and further „claimed tho right to all 
gifts made within tho templo to tho pandas, it was. held that the rawa! had 
Noright to deny excess to the temple to the pandas, and,that tho latter wero 

‘entitled to recaivs within tho temple and rotain all gifts mado*to them by 
their clients and intondod as gifts to thom and not “as offerings for ithe temple. 
The oxercise of theso rights must bo subject to, the power of „tho rawa? to 
maintain order and decency in the tomple. 

Tus was a suit by the body cf paadas attached, to the 

Kedarnath temple, ‘These men, a corporation of nominally 


14 


December 
11, 1905, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


aoe 


“ eight sayanas (or headmen) and eighteen scores of pandas ” 
are the hereditary personal priests or guides who accompany 
pilgrims to the temple and conduct them through the necessary 
ceremonies and to the various sacred places at and near Kedar- 
nath, 

In 1904 the rawal (chief priest), on the pretext of avoiding 
overcrowding and disturbances in the temple, had obtained an 
order from the District’ Magistrate forbidding the pandas from 
entering the temple with their clients ; he also denied their right 
to accept and retain gifts made to them within the temple and 
claimed for the temple everything given by a. pilgrim within its 
precincts, 

The pandas sued to establish their rights in these two matters. 

It was contended for the defendant that the plaintiffs had 
no right to sue as a body and no status in connection with the 
temple. 

The ruling involves several important points of general 
principle and the case concerns a large body of persons in 
Garhwal. 

The order of the lower appellate court, which discusses the 
case in greater detail, isreproduced, A similar ruling (not repro- 
duced) relating to the right to receive and retain gifts ab 
Guptkashi was given in Rawal Ganesh Ling v. The Pandas of 
Kedarnath (S. C. A.17 of 1905) by Mr. Shakespear, Commis- 
sioner. 

ORDER BY Me. V. A. STOWELL, Deputy COMMISSIONER 

OF GARHWAL, DATED THE 17TH JULY, 1905. 

I shall deal with this case and the cross-appeal no. 40 
jointly in this judgement. The lower courbhas gone into the 
facts and circumstances of the case at great length and I shall 
merely consider the four or five points which now require 
decision, without setting forth the past history of the relation 
between the parties and the religious ceremonies involved in tho 
performance of a pilgrimage. The first point raised is whe- 
ther the suit can lie under section 11, Code of Civil Procedure, 

The defendant quoted the finding of the Commissioner in the 
Badrinath case of 1895-96 in support of his contention that 


SSS eee 


Nt res lia aa 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


Ay 5) 


the suit is not maintainable. This case however is not on all- 
fours with that case. The plaintiffs in this case have, and always 
have had, an undoubted, though ill-defined, office in connection 
with the temple of Kedarnath and their suit relates to that 
office and their emoluments. The present suit is precisely simi- 
lar to Kali Das Jiv Ram and others v. Gor Parjaram Hirji and 
others (Bumbay, no. XV, page 309), bothin its nature and 
circumstances. I consider there can be no doubt as to its main- 
tainability. 

The defendant’s next objection relates to the amendment of 
the plaint, wherely an addition to the relief claimed was made. 
The lower court bas rigktly allowed this amendment since “ an 
alteration in the relief claimed does not alter thecharacter of the 
suit (vide Calcutta XX remarks on page 808). We now come to 
the two substantive issues, whether the plaintiff pandas are entitl- 
ed to accompany the pilgrims (their jajmans) into the temple 
and pilot them through the ceremonies performed therein, and 
whether they are entitled to receive and retain gifts from their 
clients whilst within the temple. 


It may be noted that in this suit we have nothing to do with 
the executive and administrative control. of the temple and the 
persons entering it. It is no answer to theclaim to say that 
allowing the plaintiffs to enter with their jajmans would ‘tend 
to cause confusion and crowding in the temple. The orderly 
arrangement and marshalling of persons entering the temple is 
a separate question of executive control, and if a crowd of pil- 
grims with a number of pandas tried to enter the temple at once 
the rawal could rightly check them and admit them in succes- 
sive parties. Ibis clear however that there is ample room for 
all pilgrims and pandas also -to enter the temple ap their due 
turns; the long and difficult journey from the plains precludes 
any possibility of crowding in the sense that more pilgrims visi 
the temple in a day than could be admitted conveniently. (As 
a matter of fact in the height of the soason only some 300 or 
400 a day pass up bhe pilgrim route.) : 3 

Whav ve z a with however ab presenb in A 
civil suit is the hereditary right of the pandas, 98 office ; ag laer 
of a sort of the Kedarnath temple, to accompany their jajmans 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 108 ) 


into the temple. Now it is clear that they, like everyone else 
who is not ceremonially unclean, have « perfect right to enter the 
temple; it also appears beyond doubt that they do accompany, 
and always have accompanied in their capacity as pandas, their 
clients in their ceremonial visit to the temple. Provided they 
behave themselves in a seemly aud orderly manner and submit 
to necessary regulations for the convenience of the temple ad- 
ministration, I consider that they cannot be interfered with. 
The rawal’s control extends to the appointment and dismissal 
of the temple staff, the receipt ‘and expenditure of the temple 
revenues, the ordering of the temple services and the enforce- 
ment of order and of proper a'd peaceable behaviour within the 
temple; he could exclude an outsider who entered the temple 
for improper purposes or in any way disturbed the services or 
the worshippers, but I consider he has no right to prohibit 
altogether the attendance in the temple on their jajmans of the 
pandas who are a recognizel body of spiritual advisers and 
guides, attached to the temple from time immemorial. 
Turning now to the question of dan dukshina (the gifts made 
by the pious to their attendant pandas). The lower court has, 
it seems to me, fallen into some confusion (which the defendant 
naturally seeks to increase) through a failure to clearly distin- 
guish between a gift and an offering. The pandas used, it 
appears, in former times to be entitled toa share of the actual 
offerings made to the god, This right they have lost and no 
longer claim. But there is all the difference in the world 
between an offering and agiit. All offerings must undoubtedly go 
to the temple and the rawa} as its manager has a right to every- 
thing that is intended to be given as an offering to the god or gods 
of the temple. But a gift is the property of the person to whom 
it is given and to whom the giver intends that it shall belong, 
and this is the case wherever delivery of the gift may be made. 
There are various orders on record precluding the pandas from 
taking or receiving anything thal is offered in the temple; there 
is no order laying down that a gift made by a pilgrim to a panda 
and intended as a gift to the panda may be appropriated by the 
temple, It is against law and commonsense that there should be 
any such ruling. The-intention of the giver is and must be 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 109 ) 


prepotent in such a matter, and if he invends the gift to go to the 
panda as a reward for his services or as a pious expression of -his 
gratitude to Brahmans, or for the purpose of inducing the panda 
to perform certain ceremonies for him in the future, then the 
panda is entitled to retain that gifs. Whether the shrine is a 
suitable place for the delivery of gifts is quite another question 
and one appertaining to the administrative control of the temple. 
If a pilgrim carried with him a load of articles and proceeded to 
open and hand them over in the shrine, or if the pandas created 
a disturbance by demanding the delivery of a gift in the shrine, 
they could very properly be turned out aud such practices put a 
stop to. But if a pilgrim having vowed a shankalap in the shrine 
proceeds to slip a gold mohur or a few rupees into his panda's 
hand in fulfilment of his vow, it is absurd to say that the gift 
must go to the temple or that the panda is prohibited from retain- 
ing it. I therefore accept the plaintiff-appellant’s appeal and 
modify the order of the lower court by amending paragraph (a) 
of the order and decree as follows :— 
“(q) To enter the temple of Kedarnath as pandas with their 
. jajmans and to receive and retain gifts from their 
jajmans when made to them by way of shankalap and 
intended for the personal benefit of the panda, whether 
delivery be made inside or oatside the temple. This 
right is subject to the administrative control of the 
rawal so far as the maintenance of order and decency 
and the enforcement of proper behaviour within the 
temple are concerned.” 
Costs in the lower conrt and of this appeal will be paid by 
the defendant-respondent. 


Orper By Mr. A. M, W. SHAKESPEAR, COMMISSIONER. 


This is a special civil appeal. Tho case refers to a dispute 
between the pandas and the raw of the Kedarnath temple. “Ib 
is argued in this court that the suit does not lie in the civil courb. 
On this point there is a precedent in the case Kalidus Jivram 
v. Qor Pargaram Hijri (Bombay, XV, page 809). In that case 
the Gors sued the shewak of the temple, being aggrieved by 
an order of the shewak that admission to the temple should he 


2 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(e110! ) 


by ticket. It was held that the order was ultra vires and gave 
a cause of action. In this case we find the rawal of Kedarnath 
got the Deputy Commissioner of Garhwal to pass an order to the 
effect that the pandas were not to enter the temple with pilgrims. 
A very clear cause of action then arises and I find the suit lies. 

As regards the facts it is clear that the pandas have from 
time immemorial entered the temple with their patrons (the 
pilgrims), The contention of the rawal that the pandas cannot 
enter the temple is undoubtedly wrong. The lower appellate 
court has granted a decree declaring that the pandas are entitled 
to enter the temple. 

A further question arose as to the right of the pandas to 
receive gifts from their patrons. 

It is necessary to consider the meaning of charhawa. This 
word means an offering to adeity. In old times it is clear that 
the pandas received half of such offerings. For years that right 
has been lost and the pandas do not now claim this. They claim 
however to receive and retain free gifts given to them as dan 
dakshina. They have clearly the right to such gifts, The 
decree of the lower appellate court is therefore correct. 

'Phe decree has carefully laid down that “ this right is subject 
to the control of the rawal sofar as the maintenance of order 
and decency and the enforcement of proper behaviour within the 
temple are concerned.” 

Tam of opinion that the wording of the decree is proper and 
I dismiss the appeal with costs.

(€ 111 ) 


Rights in water —Irrigation channels— Local custom of prior user, 

Held that the local custom regarding the utilization of water in the hills 
depends on the question of prior user. No man may divert or take the water of 
a stream, if by so doing he interferes with tho pre-existing utilization of such 
water by another man. Water in tho hills is the property of Government and 
its utilization is a matter of public policy. $ 

Tumis case relates to the utilization of the water of a hill 


stream for irrigation purposes by the construction of a channel 


(gul) to lead the water by gravitation on to the fields. All’ 


streams in the hills and their beds are ‘ unmeasured” and are 
recognized as the property of the State ; and the law of riparian 
proprietorship in force elsewhere has no application to the condi- 
tions in the hills. This ruling lays down the custom clearly and 
refers to various earlier rulings where the same principle was 
followed. 

As this is the latest and most comprehensive ruling on the 


- subject, and the principle has never been dissented from, I haye 


not thought it necessary to reproduce any other rulings on the 
subject. 

Babu Din Dayal, vakil for appellants; and Pandit Tara 
Datt Gairola for respondents. 

ORDER sy Mer. E, F. L. WINTER, COMMISSIONER. 

In this case the plaintiffs are the proprietors of a gul cons- 
tructed some eight years ago for irrigation purposes. The defend- 
ants-appellants recently consbructed a gul higher up the stream. 


` The plaintiffs urged that this interfered with the supply of water 


to their gul and sued to have it closed. The court of first appeal 
has found that except during the rains the defendants’ gul can- 
not be used except to the detriment of the plaintiffs and has 
given the plaintiffs a decree stopping the use of, the defendants’ 
gul from the end of Katik till the 15th Asarh and during the 
rains for all but two days in the week. 

An appeal is filed by the defendants on the ground thab every 
villager on the banks of a natural stream has a right to the use 
of the water in a stream, and that plaintiffs by the use of the 
water for eight years only had acquired no easement depriving 
defendants of their natural righb. 

The court of first appeal has found that streams in the hills 
are the property of Government and part of the waste and 


March 22, 
1909. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


ee) 


unmeasured land which is Government protected forest, and that, 
in accordance with the custom in Kumaun, the question as to the 
right of water is one of prior right, and that any man may take 
water for irrigation purposes, provided he does not interfere with 
any pre-existing right or utilization of the water. Once he has 
done so, no other man or village can claim to take the water so 
as to interfere with the first man’s supply. 

-I entirely concur in this view ; it is that consistently followed 
by this court. I have been referred to and can find no decisions 
opposed to it. On the other hand there are numerous decisions 
based on it. I refer to a few. 

In the case of Ganga and others v. Tula Ram, Mr. Ross, 
Commissioner, recorded in 1885: “It seems quite clear that the 
Mandal Sera people have been first in the field and had this gul 
made long before the Phultania people. There is not enough 
water for the Mandal and Phultania people and so only Mandal 
Sera can be supplied.” 

In the case of Ahmad Husain v. Mohi-ud-din, Mr. Ross, 
Commissioner, recorded in 1885: “ Appellant cannot urge any- 
thing regarding the distribution of the water, because it is the 
property of Government and Government can utilize it in the 
manner it thinks best for the State. ” 

In the case of Kedaru v. Hari Ram etc., Mr. Hamblin in 
1898 held that the right to extend irrigation from an existing 
gul depended on whether this would interfere with the irrigation 
by other persons at the time existing. 

I have myself decided several cases on this principle. It 
must be remembered in explanation of this custom also that 
irrigation from most of these streams is only possible by the 
construction of a gul at some expense. 

It would obviously he unfair and opposed to equity and public 
policy if when a man has incurred expense in constructing a gul 
to utilise water up to that time running to waste, another man 


higher up were to be allowed to make that gul useless by 
intercepting all the water. 


When a gul has been constructed and is in use the villagers 
higher up are only entitled to divert so much of the water as can 


be taken without detriment to the area up to that time irrigated 
by the already existing gul. 


ae 


Digitization by Arya Samaj} Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 118 ) 


a This is the custom as I find it existing in Kumaun, and I 
dismiss the appeal with costs.

ORDER BY Mr, P. WYNDHAM, COMMISSIONER,

This is an important case—the details have been fully set out

It appears that one Bala Ram, a Deoprayagi panda, died

This widow by two deeds of gift transferred whatever rights

husband’s sister. This sister had gone across the Alaknanda

under the deed of gift,

with in one deed registered at Tehri, while the Garhwal property 
was dealt with by a deed registered in British Garhwal. In the

dants (3) and (4) could officiate as pandas at Taptkund and 
the right to collect such offerings could be alienated.

right the better. To refuse an injunction may lead to much 
further litigation and perhaps to a breach of the peace. Pandas 
when they begin a dispute are inclined to bo lawless. 

I accordingly uphold the order of the learned Deputy Com- 
missioner, excopi in the matter of an injunction, and on this 
point the appeal will prevail. 

As regards costs, the appellant would be entitled to his costs 
inall courts as against tho respondent. 


OPINION OF THS Hra COURT, DATED THE 23RD Maron, 1916. 
This is a reference made by tho Local Government under rule 

17 of tho Kumaun rules. The facts of the case out of which the 
referenceariscs are these :—On the Tehri side of the Ganges is a 
temple called Deoprayag, on the British side, ata short distance 
from that temple and close to the temple of Badrinath, is a place 
called Taptkund. Pilgrims to Deoprayag go to Taptkund anl 
are conduciel there by certain pandas who receive offerings 
made at the kund. One of theso pandas was a man of the 
name of Tara Datt who is now dead. Musammat Sarda, the 
widow of Tara Datt, executed a deed of gift on the 7th January, 
1911, in favour of the defendants (2) and (3) of the rights which 
she alleged sho had inherited from her husband, ‘he plain- 
tiffs are seyen persons who call themselves the panch pandas of 
Badrinath, and they pray that the deed of gift be cancelled and 
a decree bo made restraining the defendants (2) and (3) from 
acting as pandas at Taptkund. ‘They base their claim on three 
grounds : first, that they are the reversioners to the estate of 
Tara Datt; secondly, that, according to the customs of the 
plaintifi’s family, if a person belonging to the Panda caste die 


| 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


without leaving heirs, his rights devolve upon the panch pandas 


. alone, and that therefore the plaintiffs are entitled to haye the 


deed set aside. Tne last ground of their claim is that they are 
persons who are solely entitled to take gifts and oferings made 
by the pilgrims at the Taptkund. In the first -paragraph of 
their plaint they say as follows: “ No one of the Panda caste 
excopt the p‘aintiffs is entitled to take dam and dakshina (gifts 
and offerings) from tha pilgrims at the places whore the plain- 
tiffs have a right to receive the same.” The court of first 


instance dismissed the suit. The court of first appeal also 


found in concurrenco with the court of the first instante that 
the plaintiffs wore nob reversioners of Tara Datt, either directly 
or remotely, as the plaintiffs alleged, on failure of heirs. These 
being findings of fact the plaintiffs have on those findings no 
right to maintain the suit for setting aside the gift. Whother 
the gift is a valid one or not, unless the plaintiffs have a right 
to challenge its validity, the court cannot makea decree declar- 
ing that it is invalid. We are of opinion that upon tho finding 
that the plaintiffs aro neither the dire:t reversionary heirs of 
Tara Datt nor persons who had an ultimate reversionary right, 
they are not competent to sue to have the gift set aside, and on 
this ground alone their claim to have the gift cancelled ought 
to have been dismissed. The court of first appeal decreed the 
plaintiffs suit for cancellation’ of tho gift, but dismissed it 
as regards the prayer for an injunction ; upon second appeal the 
learned Commissioner upheld the decision of tho first appellate 
court as to tho gift and reversed it on the question of injunction, 
and made a decreo granting an injunction to tho plaintiffs res- 
training the defendants from acting as pandas. Tho main 
ground upon which the injunction was granted by the learned 
Commissioner was that tho refusal might lead to further litiga- 
tion and perhaps to a breach of tho peace, This, in our opinion, 
is no valid ground for the issue of an injunction, ‘Tho main 
ground upon which the plaintiffs claimed an injunction was 
that they alone were entitled as panch pandas of Badrinath 
to the offerings mado at the Taptkund. ‘This right thoy failed 
to establish in any of tho courts below. We tako the finding of 
the first appellate court to be that they had nob any such right,

(118° 5 


That court in decrecing the cancellation of the gift proceeded 
on the ground that the defendants had acquired no right (under 
the gift made by Musammat Sarda, but thatin our opinion was 
besides the question. Whether the defendants hada right or 
‘not it was for the plaintiffs to establish the right which they 
asserted. ‘This the plaintiffs failed to do and on this ground 
alone their suit ought to have been dismissed, We are of opinion 
that the decision of the court of the first instance was correct 
and the plaintiffs’ suit ought to have been dismissed with costs 
in all courts including the costs of this reference in which should 
be included fees on the higher scale, This is our answer to the 
reference.

COMMENTARY ON KUMAUN RULINGS AND CUSTOMS, 


J,—INTRODUOCTORY. 


Scheme of commentary.—In the following notes all rulings 
referred to without other details than the names of the parties 
are local rulings and will be found reproduced in this collection. 
I have endeavoured to set oub concisely the system of local 
customs ina connected way, explaining the local conditions 
implied or involved in. the matters referred to, referring to 
Kumaun rulings, wherever} these were available, and filling in 
the gaps and noting on peculiarities or omissions in the rulings 
by the light of local experience and a study of local records, 
assisted by a body of local opinion obtained under the following 
circumstances. In 1915 I presided over a committee compris- 
ing at first four and subsequently five local gentlemen with a 
view to drafting a Kumaun Laws Bill, which should include a 
codification of the principle local customs. After three con- 
ferences, at one of which some other representatives of the 
division were present, a number of clauses embodying local 
customs were drafted and included in the Bill by agreement. 
(See appendix.) Subsequently when the presenb compilation 
was about to be undertaken, copies of these clauses were circu- 
lated to a number of leading Indian residents of the division 
and their opinions invited as to whether the customs had been 
correctly recorded. A number of replies have been received, 
and among them a report of a meeting of retired officials, vakils, 
village headmen and others held at Almora to consider the 
subject, This meeting approved the clauses as drafted with two 
very minor alterations, except that the great majority of those 
present upheld the old custom excluding daughters from inheri- 
tance, as against modern rulings of the courts. Tue draft clause 
had followed the rulings. 

Other opinions differed on two or three points, mostly of 
small importance, but the general result is a considerable body 
of Jocal opinion showing what are the actual customs followed, 

16 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 2) 


as certified to by Indian gentlemen in the best position to know, 
The results of this enquiry aud of dhe drafting .committee’s 
conferences form bhe local opinion, to which I refer on certain 
points, and together with existing literature on the subject 
attest the existence of various customs mentioned in these notes, 
regarding which no rulings are forthcoming. 

I have referred in various places to Tupper’s Punjab Ous- 
tomary Law (8 volumes, 188i) which deals with lengthy in- 
quiries on the same subject in the Punjab. These references, 
though not directly relevant as regards the existence of customs 
in Kumaun, are interesting as disclosing the existence in that 
province, and especially in the hill district of Kangra, of 
various customs, obsolete or unknown in most provinces, which it 
shares with the Kumaun hills. This may be of assistance in 
determining the first question regarding an alleged custom, 
namely whether it has any real existence, before a court proceeds 
to the further question whether the custom in question is applic- 
able to the parties concerned. . 

2, Scarcity of rulings and reasons for this—The scarcity 
of rulings on points of Kumaun customs, which a search of the 
- Commissioner’s record room reveals, is surprising at first sight 
but is easily explained. About 50 years ago, in order to 
' relieve a congested record room, nearly all the old files of the 
Commissioner’s court down to the latter part of Sir Henry 
Ramsay’s time were weeded out and destroyed, We are thus 
restricted to some of Sir Henry Ramsay’s latest rulings and to 
those of recent Commissioners from 1886 onwards. There are 
notes on record of a few earlier rulings of which the: files have 
been destroyed. 

Now Sir Henry Ramsay was Commissioner of Kumaun for 
a longer period than the entire service in India of most officers, 
andif he ever recorded any rulings discussing and laying 
down custom, he had doubtless passed this stage long before the 
latter years of his rule, and knowing all the local conditions and 
customs by heart he latterly contented himself with the briefest 
possible orders, often in the vernacular. At the same time it 
has been recorded that he disliked the principle of fixed rulings 
on points of custom and often refused to follow his own decisions; 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(85) 


he preferred to settle each case on its merits, as seemed equitable 
to him at the time by a summary order. ? 

Of more recent Commissioners one or two have been officers 
possessed of previoas experience of Kumaun, but the majority 
have come from the plains with no previous knowledge of local 
conditions or customs, and rulings by such officers on points of. 
local customs are of very varying value. In some cases they 
took the trouble to inquire into the peculiarities of local custom, 
butin others they followed without any inquiry the ordinary 
rules of Hindu Law or their own ingrained ideas imported from 
the plains. When the lower court happened to be similarly 
inexperienced, an erratic decision was not unnatural, especially 
as the Kumaun Bar is of modern growth; Sir Henry. Ramsay 
allowed no lawyers in Kumaun, 

3. Classification of rulings.—In the old days in Kumaun 
there was a broad aud simple classification of suits and proceed- 
ings. All cases, which concerned land were revenue cases, 
whether they related to questions of tenancy, proprietary rights, 
land revenue or anything else affecting land or landed property ; 
all other cases were civil. ‘Thus we find revenue appeals dealing 
with what would now be the subject of civil suits. 

In more: recent times the recognized modern ‘classification has 
been followed, bub since the same courts tried both classes of 
suits and strict accuracy of procedure has never been a distin- 
guishing feature of the local courts, we often find considerable 
vagueness and confusion in the classification of suits. 

The Commissioner’s court has always been the High Court 
of the division in civil matters (subject in recent times to a rarely 


roference by the Local Government to the Allah- 


used power oi 
; er has ‘been 


abad High Court for opinion), and the Commission 


practically the court of final appeal in revenue matters. The 


isl in t bub it 
Board of Revenue have a revisional power in these cases, 


is seldom used and in the old days very fow of the people evor 
ventured so far. 

The decisions of district officers of long experie 
hills were doubtless in many cases sounder and more i 
than rulings by Commissionors of no local experience, 


former can hardly be quoted as rulings.

II.—THE PEOPLE AND TENURES. 

å. The people of the hills.—An explanatory note regarding 
the people of the hills and theirland tenures, so far as these 
concern our subject, may be inserted here for the information of 
courts unfamiliar with the local conditions. 

The people are almost all Hindus, and the majority of 
the few Muhammadans found in the hills are very ignorant and 
semi-Hinduised in various ways; they have no special Muham- 
madan local customs. 

In the northernmost parts of the division we find the Bhotias, 
a race of traders, of mixed origin; they very rarely resort to the 
courts and there are norulings on record regarding their customs 
of inheritance, adoption, marriage, etc.; these customs are pecu- 
liar to themselves and quite different from those of the Hindus. 
The Bhotias are however gradually becoming Hinduised to some 
extent. But leaving asideafew banias in the bazars, more or less 
recent immigrants from the plains, the vast majority of the 
population consists of biths and doms, 

The biths or ordinary Hindu inhabitants are so called in 
contradistinction to the doms, who are probably of aboriginal 
descent, though regarded by some as low caste Hindus. The 
biths are, broadly speaking, divided into four main classes—Brah- 
mans, Rajputs, Khas-Brahmans and Khas-Rajputs or Khasias. 

These four classes comprised between them more than 95 
per cent. of the agriculturists (proprietors and tenants) of Garh- 
wal at the last settlement. s 

The Khasias are the most numerous of these classes; they 
do not generally wear the sacred thread, but are gradually assum- 
ing it ; they are really of mixed or Sudra origin, but ysually call 
hoedi ras Rajputs. ; 

The Khas-Brahmans or inferior cultivating a 
Brabmans wear the sacred thread but are generally of doubt- 
ful origin ; some of them are the offspring of or descended from a 
Brahman father and a Rajput or Khasia mother. It is these 
Khasias and Khas-Brahmans who follow the peculiar and often 
old-fashioned customs, which form the subject of the special 
rulingsand discussions relating to Hindu Law in Kumaun. 
They form the bulk of the village population. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


o) 

: The pure Rajputs, mostly immigrants of later times, and 
higher caste Brahmans, generally follow the normal rule of 
Hindu Law applicable to the twice-born classes, The real test 
in such disputes regarding custom should thus be whether the 
parties are of genuine Rajput or Brahman caste or are Khasias 
or Khas-Brahmans ; unfortunately it is often not easy to decide 
this point among the very numerous local sub-castes in the hills, 
and it is a principle rarely referred to in specific terms in the 
rulings. Even in one caste there may be higher and lower sub- 
divisions f Bachi v. Mahenar Singh). 

The doms follow the customs of the village Kbasias in a 
rather vague or lax way; they are a poor and ignorant class of 
out-castes and their customs rarely come before the courts. 
There appear to be no special rulings regarding their customs. 


5. Land tenwres.—In the hills only a small fraction of the 
land belongs to the people; this is náp or “measured” land, 
which has been measured or surveyed, and settled with the vil- 
lagers at settlement, and comprises the actual cultivated area 
and in places same blocks of adjoining waste. All other land is 
benáp or “ unmeasured ” land and is the property of the State. 

Proprietary right only exists, legally speaking, in náp land 
including nayabad grants settled under the nayabad rules. 
Extensions of cultivation into adjoining unmeasured land are 
however allowed by Government, subject to certain restrictions. 

They are measured and assessed at the next settlement. The 
proprietary right in the hills in normal villages belongs to a body 
of petty cultivating co-sharers (hissadars), holding under a sort 
of raiyatwari. settlement and with an organization resembling 
imperfect pattidari. Each co-sharer has his own separate hold- 
ing of a few acres, and a share in the village common (measured) 
land—gaonsanjait—where this latter has not been divided up by 
imperfect partition. All the co-sharers are jointly and severally 
liable for the village land reyenue since each village 18 one 
mahal (the term mahal is not used in the hills) and perfect 
Partition is unknown. There may be more than one malguzar 
or padhan (lambardar) in a village, but this does not et 
any division of the joint liability for the land revenue. ae 
Malguzar in lien of, or in addition to, cash dues usually holds 


ee AS ERS 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 
some padhanchari land as.a rent-free tenant of Government, as 
remuneration for his oftice. Joint co-sharers in a family 
holding are known as shikmi with the head of the family and 
are often not recorded in the revenue papers. 

The tenancy system of the hills does not concern the civil 
courts. Tenants comprise (a) khaikars, pakka khaikars who 
are practically under-proprietors and kachcha khaikars who are 
occupancy tenants, both paying the land revenue plus a fixed 
percentage as malikana and (b) sirtans or tenants-at-will. A 
proprietor cannot sue ina civil court to contest an order of a 
Settlement Officer recording a tenant of his land as a khaikar 
(Allahabad Weekly Notes, 1905, page 91). 

III.—RULINGS AND Customs. 
(i)—Hindu Law. 

6. Hindu Law.--The normal rule of Hindu Law followed in 
the hills is that of the Mitakshara school, and where no special 
local custom is established in rəspect of any principle of Hindu 
Law, it is to be assumed that tho Mitakshara rule is followed. 

In applying any test to local custom it is however necessary 
to bear in mind the origin of the people concerned and their 
present circumstances. The classes usually concerned in disputes 
relating to custom are the Khasias and Khas-Brahmans (see para- 
graph 4 above); now the Khasias (Khas-Rajputs) are the des- 
eendants of ancient immigrants into the hills and represent a 
“blending of pure Aryan with some aboriginal hill stock ” 
(Mr. Goudge; Almora Settlement Report). Similarly, the 
inferior Brahmans, Khas-Brahmans, are of mixed origin. It may 
threfore fairly be urged that in applying the rules of Hindu 
Law to these people the courts should regard them as represen- 
tatives of the Sudra class and should not insist too strongly on 
the presumption that they are bound by the strict rules of Hindu 
Law as applied to the purer and higher castes, These ancient 
immigrants brought up with them the now old-fashioned customs 
of their time, and their descendants, living in backward conditions 
in their remote hill valleys, retained these customs, in ignorance 
of the changes taking place in the outer world. We thus find 
obsolete customs, such as that of jethon (the eldest sons’s extra 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


Coes) 


portion) and Sautia Bant (division per stirpes among sons) still 
observed in some families in the hills; while the formalities which 
have crystallised into necessary conditions for various ceremonies 
(e.g-, adoption) are seldom or never observed among these vil- 
lagers, who are ignorant of the complicated requirements of the 
modern Hindu Law. They follow their old and familiar customs 
and it is difficult for them to understand that tle courts require 
them to prove a local custom, which is to them a matter of course, 
or to appreciate that the establishing of a custom which (though 
they do not know it) is in variation of Hindu Law, calls for 
special and weighty evidence. 

The remarks of the Commissioner in his final order in Fateh 
Singh v. Gabar Singh (q. v.) are deserving of serious consideration 
in this connection, They are strongly endorsed by local opinion 
also. 

In dealing with cases relating to custom there are, it must be 
remembered, three separate questions that may arise, (a) whether 
a certain custom or usage exists, (b) if so, what its exact inter- 
pretation and application are, (c) whether it is followed by the 
family or caste concerned in the case. 

It is nob enough to prove point (a) in a general way; the 
direct applicability of the custom to the parties concerned, point 


_ (ce) must also be proved and this is where litigants often fail. 


Ib ig an issue of fact and if any reasonable proof is adduced in 


- the first court, the question cannot be raised in second appeal 


(cf. Narpati v, Khimia and others). 

1, Hindu Law—Marriage.—The ordinary form of lawful 
marriage in the hills calls for no special remark. The essential 
ceremony of Saptapadi is always performed. Among the ordi- 
nary villagers a price is always paid for the bride, and the form 
of marriage is thus technically that known as Asura. Among 
the higher castes endeavours, are being made to abolish the 
custom of taking a bride-price, thus making the marriage one 
of the Brahma form. 

For a discussion on the subject of custom in this connection 
the High Court’s remand order in Fateh Singh v. Gabar Singh 
may be referred to. Thore are however two forms of marriage 
connection which call for special notice in the hills, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


as 


It is very common, especially among the Khasia castes, for 4 
man (whether he has or has not another wife living) to take to 
live with him the widow or the relinquished wife of another 
man. In some cases the man pays the woman’s price to the 
husband, or if the latter is dead, to the relatives, or failing them, 
to the woman’s family ; in other cases nothing is paid. 

A ceremony, the Ganeshpuja, is sometimes performed by the 
family priest and a feast is often given to the man’s caste-fellows, 
The man and woman thus living together are known as dhant 
and dhanti (or dogharia—the term dhanti is considered vulgar 
by the higher classes). Strictly speaking these terms only 
apply when the woman’s price has not been paid. ` When 
the woman is paid for, she is locally regarded as a wife, but 
the terms are commonly used in a more general sense. This 
connection is usually a permanent one and is not considered in 
any way disgraceful among the villagers. Where the woman’s 
price has been paid, the offspring of the connection are socially 
equal to all members of their father’s caste, otherwise they are 
inferior to some extent, until the price has been paid ; this can 
be done at any time. It is of course not a lawful marriage ; 
and the children of such a connection are no doubt illegitimate 
in law. The point on which the results of this form of counec- 
tion are constantly coming before the courts, is in the numerous 
cases of disputed succession turning on the right of inheritance 
of these illegitimate children, This will be discussed under the 
head of inheritance. 

It may be remarked however that, among the villagers of 
the Khasia and Khas-Brahman classes, the keeping of a dhanti 
wife is much more common than a second legal marriage, and 
where one comes across instances (not in connection with a 
question of inheritance or marriage) of a man having a second 
wife or children by a second wife, the probabilities are that the 
second wife isadhantt. Ithas often been objected by appellate 
courts that the alleged laxity in respect of marriage in the hills 
is not supported by sufficient concrete instances ; a perusal 
however of the rulings in this collection or of any bundle of 
records taken at random, will illustrate the commonness of this 
form of unauthorized “ marriage ” among the ordinary villagers, 


ee 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 9 ) 


8. Marriage~ The levirate.—The custom whereby the younger 
brother takes to wife his elder brother’s widow (bhawaj) is 
commonly followed among all but the higher castes of Brahmans 
and Rajputs in the hills. This is not strictly the levirate custom, 
since the younger brother does not merely beget offspring for 
his brother, but inherits his brothor’s widow to be his own wife 
and sons (bhaujetha) begotten on her belong to their natural 
father. Where the widow goes to live with the younger brother, 
she is equal toa lawfully married wife and her offspring are 
legitimate, but where she continues to live in her former hus- 
band’s house and merely cohabits with the younger brother, 
she is only a concubine and her children are illegitimate. (See 
Padmu v. Bhawan Singh, and Kirpal Singh v. Partab Singh 
and others.) 


On the death of the younger brother the bhawaj is not 
entitled to a widow’s estate in his property ; she must look to the 
sons of her original husband for maintenance (Jamnu v. Musam- 
mat Manuli and others). She would thus take a widow’s estate 
in her original husband’s property if he left no sons. If her- 
original husband left no estate sho might claim maintenance 
from the younger brother’s estate; this is the loval Opinion, 
The term bhawaj is loosely used and is often applied to the 
widow of a cousin or of a more distant relative, though not 
usually if resident in a different village. In the case of a man’s 
taking such a more distant relative’s widow to wife, the position 
would presumably be that of the usual dhan¢ and dhanti con- 
nection referred to above. 


The levirate and this connected custom are discussed by 
Mayne in his chapter on “ Marriage and Sonship” and the pre- 
valence of the custom among certain castes and in certain locali- 
ties in the Punjab is referred to in Tuppor’s Punjab Customary 
Law, volume 1I, pages 94 to 96. and 131 and volume III, page 27. 
In the Punjab in the case of a brothcr’s widow “consent and 
cohabitation are all that is required, the sons all inherit as 
legitimate.” (Tupper, volume II, page 131.) 


The tekwa.—Occasionally in some Khasia villages the whole 
of the deceased’s property is made over to another man, on 
the condition that he lives with the widow as his wife. 

17 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(ie 10) 


This second husband is known as tekwa. The reversioners 
by this arrangement give up their claim to any part of the 
deceased’s property. Tho practice is regarded asa somewhat 
immoral one (Pauw : Garhwal Settlement Report, page 44). I 
have never seen any decisions or cases referring to this custom. 

9, Hindu Law— Adoption.—Adcption in the ordinary sense 
does not appear to be very common in the hills. Wherein- 
stances occur among the ordinary village classes, they are com- 
monly characterized by the absence of any of the usual forma- 
lities of the Hindu Law. If it be accepted that the Khasias are 
really of the Sudra class, this is in accordance with the normal 
rule (cf.6 Allahabad, page 281; Trevelyan’s Hindu Family 
Law, page 153, etc.\. The only local ruling on the subject 
(Padmu v. Bhawan Singh and others) refers to this absence of 
‘any formalities ; Bhawan Singh took a widow with her son to 
live with him and treated the boy as his son; it was held that 
he had practically adopted the boy and that such informal adop- 
tion is all that takes place among the ordinary villagers in the 
hills. To such cases the principle of factum valet may in any 
case often be applied (cf. 9 Allahabad, page 253; 21 Calcutta, 
pages 460 to 487 ; and Tupper’s Punjab Customary Law, volume 
III, page 82). ‘The necessary qualifications for an adoption 
may be presumed to apply in the hills, as elsewhere, i.e. as 
regards capacity to adopt or to he adopted, etc. 

The gharjawain.—A special and fairly common form of 
adoption in the hills is that in which a man, who has no son, 
marries his daughter to a son-in-law who agrees to live perma- 
nently in his house, and is known thereafter as the gharjawain. 
In this case if the married couple continue to live permanently 
with the father, they inherit the estate, but if they leave him and 
go to the son-in-law’s house, they are disinherited, Ib must be 
remembered that the hill people do not recognise the right of the 
daughter to inherit (see below). 

It is common for the father to make over his property, Or 
part of it, to the couple during his lifetime, usually by a deed 
declaratory of the arrangement. Such adoptions of a gharjawain 
are sometimes made by a widow, who often goes through the 
form of nominally selling the estate to the son-in-law on the 
pretence that he has paid or is to pay the debts of the deceased, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


s 


Cam) 


Such an adoption by the widow is presumably invalid unless she 
has Leen expressly authorized to make it by her late husband. 

I bave found no rulings of the Kumaun High Court on this 
custom. 

The same custom is found in Madras in one caste (cf. 4 
Madras, pago 272), and is apparently common in various parts of 
the Punjab among certain castes. (See Tupper, volume I, pages 
74, 79 and &0, volume II, pages 75, 158, 186, 209, 213 and 
volume ITI, page 84). : 

From the customs recorded at pages 74, 79 and 80 of yolume 
I of Tupper’s work, it appears that in the districts there referred 
to the inheritance of the gharjawain and his wife or their issue 
depends on their being placed in possession of the property by 
the father before his death. Inthe Punjab, as in these hills, 
the daughter is very commonly not allowed to inherit ; hence 
this device of the adopted son-in-law. It was proposed by Sir 
W. Rattigan in his draft Punjab Customary Laws Bill, 1897, 
clause 18(b) (1) (2) to give legal sanction to the daughter’s 
inheritance in the circumstances of this custom in preference to 
all collaterals. 

10. Hindu Law—The joint family.—The unity of the joint 
Hindu family is less strictly observed in respect of alienations in 
the hills than elsewhere. The question is however. not free from 
doubt. Tho only direct ruling on the subject appears to be 
Sukhdeo Prasad v. Gauri Datt in which the ordinary rule of 
Hindu Law was applied; an alienation by a younger brother of 
his share of the joint estate without the consent of his elder 
brother was set aside on the latter’s suit. 

On the other hand Mr. Pauw in his Garhwal Settlement 
Report remarks ‘Bub in the hills the shikmi [joint] 
hissadar has always been permitted to exercise full proprietary 
rights over his nominal share of the inheritance, and to claim 
that his portion shall not be held responsible for debts due from 
the manager, unless ho is especially montioned as liable in the 
decree, A fraudulent use is frequently made of this power, 
particularly in the case of private sales.” 

And the committee which drafted the Kumaun La 
1915 agreed to the insertion of the following clause :—

( 12 ) 


“A male adult coparcener in joint family property, other 
than a son or a grandson during the lifetime of his father or 
grandfather, shall be deemed to be entitled at any time to 
alienate his ascertainable share of the joint family estate without 
the consent of his coparceners, subject to the liability of his 
share for any common debt owed by the coparceners.”’ 

This clause was again approved at the public meeting convened 
to consider the whole subject at Almora in April, 1916, On the 
other hand a member of the 1915 drafting committee (the 
Hon’ble Pandit Tara Datt) now suggests that there has been 
confusion between the joint Hindu family and a jointly held or 
jointly recorded estate. -Formal partitions through the courts 
have never been common in the hills, considering the vast number 
of petty proprietors, bub it is extremely common for —joint 
co-sharers (usually collaterals), who are no longer a joint family, 
to divide up their lands by a private partition (gharbatwara), 
while allowing the whole ancestral estate to remain jointly recorded 
in the record-of-rights. The tendency of the joint family in the 
hills is to separate (as indicated by these private partitions). 

Pandit Tara Datt would therefore reduco the difference from 
the normal rule of Hindu Law to one of legal presumption, 
and would say that in Kumaun the jointness of the family should 
not be presumed in the case of collateral relatives whose shares 
are recorded as joint in the revenue papers; in the case of 
alicnations by such collaterals of thoir respective shares, the 
burden of proving that they are members of a joint family 
should be on the person impugning the transaction. ‘This however 
is contrary to the general consensus of local opinion, which 
would go considerably further as indicated above. An illustration 
of what Pandit Tara Datt refers to, the joint holding of a 
separated family and the dealings of co-sharers in such holdings, 
will be found in Chandri v. Kalmu and another. 

ll. Joint family —Maintenance and partition.—W here the 
elder brother’s widow is taken to wife by the younger brother 
she is entitled, on the latter’s death, to maintenance (or a widow’s 
estate) from her original husband’s estate (see paragraph 8 above, 
Jamnu v. Musammat Manuli). Jn default of such estate she 
may claim maintenance from the younger brother’s estate,
