( 13 ) 


according to the drafting committee of the Kumaun Laws Bill 
and local opinion. Similarly, by such opinion a dhanti wife (see 
paragraph 7) is entitled to a widow’s estate only when no law- 
fully married wife also survives the husband ; where such a 
lawful wife survives, the dhanti is only entitled to maintenance 
from her second husband’s estate. , 

A daughter, when excluded from inheritance by collaterals, 
has been held entitled t maintenance from her father’s estate 
(Jamnu v. Musammat Manuli and others), The question of the 
daughters exclusion from inhoritance is dealt with below (see 
“ Inheritance ’’). 

Askot being an impartible estate, the relatives of the Rajwar 
are entitled to maintenance from him (Lala Ranjit Singh v. 
Rajwar Pushkar Pal.and others). 

Partition.—The only peculiar custom relating to partition 
is that of Sautia Bant or division of the father’s estate per stirpes 
among the sons by different wives (i.e. if A has one son by one 


wife and three sons by a second wife, thon by Sautia Bant the - 


one son takes half the estate and the three sons the other half) ; 
where this custom is put forward, it is commonly maintained 
on the ground of a division on this principle having been carried 
out by the father during his lifetime. It comes more properly 
however under the heading of“ Inheritance” and is discussed 
under that head. See also under that heading for the custom of 
jethon, which is also connected with partition. 

12. Inheritance—Sons.—Vhere are various peculiar local 
customs relating to inheritance. Tho most prominent of these 
is that, whereby among the Khasia and Khas-Brahman castes 
the illegitimate sons by a dhanti wife (see paragraph 7) inherit 
equally to their father with sons by a lawiully married wife. 

The Khasia castes, as remarked above, are of mixed origin 
and are not pure Brahmans or Rajputs. They would thus 
naturally follow the rules of the Sudras, and among Sudras an 
illegitimate son is entitled to a share (though not @ full share) 
in his father’s estate. 

In the hills however it is beyond doubt that illegitimate 
sons among Khasia and Khas-Brahman castes do inherit equal 
shares with tho legitimate sons as a matter of right, though 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(Ce ee) 


there are frequent contests over such inheritances. Such cases 
turn on the question whether the custom of illegitimate sons 
inheriting is followed by the particular caste in question, and 
unless the parties belong to a pure Rajput or Brahman caste, 
there is an undoubted presumption in favour of the custom. 
There is often however, as indicated in paragraph 4, some diffi- 
culty in ascertaining the status of a sub-caste. A Khas-Rajput 
will always or nearly always call himself a Rajput. One con- 
stantly comes across cases, where the question of this custom is 
nob in issue, in which the facts show that such inheritance had 
taken place. 

Among the hill people of the Kangra district a similar custom 
and a similar distinction of castes prevail. There the distinction 
is between those castes (or clans) whose women “ affect seclusion 
and do not work in the fields” and who do not contract widow 
marriages, and those whose women do work in the fields and 
who do marry widows; among the former the son of a “ kept 
woman ” as oppose to a lawfully married woman does not 
inherit; among the latter the son of any kept women (not of 
degraded caste) by custom shares equally with the son of a law- 
ful wife (Tupper, volume LI, page 184). 

A similar test or classification of castes would work very 
fairly well in the Kumaun division. 

These illegitimate sons however do not, according to most 
rulings, inherit to collaterals, and cannot control their father’s 
disposal of property. 

Rulings on the subject may be summarized as follows :— 

In Nur Singh v. Ram Singh and others Mr, Ross awarded 
four-fifths of the father’s estate to the four sons by a dhanti and 
one-fifth to the only son by a lawiully married wife; the latter 
claimed one-half of the estate by Sauti Bant. The parties were 
` Khas-Rajputs of the Bhakuni caste..—( September, 1887.) 

Colonel Erskine inHari Kishan v. Radhapati (July, 1890) 
held that the custom of illegitimate sons among village Brah- 
mans succeeding to collaterals was not established ; the lower 
court's remand report mentions that the principle of such sons 
succeeding to their father was not contested by the respondent 
and was proved by the evidence, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(15) 


Tn Bach Ram and another v. Tarapati and others the right 
of illegitimate sons to succeed to their father’s and half-bro- 
ther’s estate was established ; the parties were Khas-Brahmans, 
Mr. Giles’ order of August 14, 1891, upheli by the Board of 
Revenue. 

In Jharu v. Nathu and others however Mr. D. T. Roberts 
upheld a decision applying the ordinary rules of Hindu Law to 
the respective inheritance of an illegitimate son and legitimate 
daughters of a village Brahman; no rofer2nce to, or enquiry 
about, local custom was made however, and it appeared that the 
father had divided the estate during his lifetime giving shares 
to the three children—(February 2, 1893.) 

In Umrao Singh and another v. Jhagru the title of an illegi- 
timate brother to half the estate had been admitted by the 
legitimate brother (Khas-Rajputs) ; the Commissioner, Colonel 
Erskine, was not prepared, without further careful enquiry, to 
accept the general principle laid down by the lower court that in 
Garhwal sons by dhantis inherit equally with the issue by alaw- 
ful wife.—( March 13, 1894.) 

In Bachi v. Mahenar Singh it was held that among the bisht 
caste of Rajputs, though illegitimate sons may inherit a share 
in their father’s estate, they do not inherit to collaterals. The 
father had divided up his estate equally among all the sons, and 
on the death of one of the legitimate sons the illegitimate son 
claimed a share in his estate. A special remaud enquiry into the

In Karam Singh v. Sher Singh and another Mr. J. S. Campbell 
held (June 25, 1913) that though illegitimate sons among 
Khasias inherit a share of thoir father’s estate, their rights only 


accrue on their father’s death ; they cannot control his disposal 


of bis property during his life. 

In Fateh Singh v. Gabar Singh the parties were Bhandari 
Rajputs ; the father had taken a dhanti wite with the perfor- 
mance of the Ganeshpuja ceremony. The Local Government on 
the High Court’s opinion remanded the case for enquiry as to

( 16 ) 


whether this form of marriage was a lawful marriage or nob. 
It was not a lawful marriage, but by a confusion of issues it was 
held that this finding involved the non-possession of rights by 
the son of the dhanti wife, which was really quite a different 
issue and did not follow from the above finding; see notes on 
the case. (Final order by Mr. Wyndham of April 15, 1916.) 
The custom was declared by tho committee drafting the 
Kumaun Laws Billin 1915 in the following clause which has 
been approved by local opinion (excepting one or two gentlemen 
who consider that strictor morality should be enforced by legis- 
lation, while not contesting the existence of the custom) :— 

Clause 13.—“ In the circumstances (i.e, where a man has 
taken a dhanti wife) and among the castes (Khas-Brahmans, 
Khas-Rajputs and doms) referred to in section 12, the courts 
may presume that the offspring of such a connection inherit 
equally with the offspring by a regularly married wife, and that 
in the absence of offspring of such married wife the offspring 
of such a connection inherit their father’s estate. 

Children by the elder brother's widow.—A subsequent clause 
laid down a similar rule for the inheritance of children born to 
the younger brother by the elder brother’s widow. (See para- 
graph 8, levira‘e) this principle has been ləid down in a 
somewhat indefinite way in Padmw v. Bhawan Singh and others 
(q: v.) and indirectly confirmed, though restricted in its applica- 
tion in Kirpal Singh v. Partab Singh and others, where it was 
held that the son of a man by his elder brother's widow does 
not inherit his father’s estate, unless the father takes the widow 
to live in his own house and treats her as of his own family. 
(See also Dulab Singh and another v. Musammat Ganga and 


another for an instance of a daughter by a brothers widow — 


inheriting.) In parts of the Punjib, where such marriages are 
customary, children by tho older brother’s widow are legitimate 
and inherit (Tupper, volume IT, pages 131, 162 and 174). 

13, Inheritance—Sons—Siutia Bant.—A custom not infre- 
quently alleged as being followed by a particular family is that 
of Sautin Bunt (ineritauce per stirpes) between the sons by 
different wives. (See paragraph 11, Partition.) This is not a 
general custom nor is it one observed hy certain caste: or classes. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( W7 ) 


Tt appears to be followed as a custom by certain families or in 
certain villages, but when set up it is most usually supported by 
a will or by a partition effected by the father during his lifetime. 
It is an abnormal custom and the presumption even in the hills 
is strongly in favour of the usual rule of Bhai Bant (inheritance 
per capita), each son taking an equal share. Mr. Pauw, Deputy 
Commissioner of Garhwal, in a judgement on the subject said 
that the custom appeared to be of Nepalese origin and of com- 
paratively recent introduction, I do not know on what grounds 
he asserted this. It would seem more probable that the custom 
is an old-fashioned survival from early times, connected with 
matriarchal ideas (cf. Mayne on Partition), In the Punjab, 
where it is known as Chundavand, it prevails in the Gurdaspur 
district, among the Rajputs and some others of Gurgaon, as 
a village or family custom in: Rohtak, and is universal in the 
Kangra hills (Tupper, volume I, pages 72 and 78; volume II, 
pages 188, 175 and 183), 

In the oldest ruling on the subject (November 23, 1861) Sir 
Henry Ramsay laid it down that in the absence of a will a 
partition by Sautia Bant cannot be made by the courts (Nand 
Ram v. Bhajan Datt). In Ratan Singh and others v. Sher 
Singh he ruled that in the absence of a written agreement 
or will, or the strongest evidence, a Sutia Bant ought not to be 
given. In Padmw v. Shib Datt and others however he upheld 
a Sautia Bant which from a partition deed and the facts of the 
existing possession appeared to have been carried oub by tho 
father some years before. 

In Rabi Datt v. Daya Ram and others the Board of Revenue 
held that Bhat Bant is the normal custom in Garhwal and special 
authority (such as a special agreement or disposal of property) 
must be proved to alter the law in a particular case. 

An instance of the custom being proved to /exisb in. a parti- 
cular family will be found in Narpati v. Khimia and others, 
in which the High Court pointed out that the question whether 
a given custom prevails in a particular family is a question of 
fact and no second appeal lies on the point. 

In another case, not reproduced (Ganga Datt v. Bhajan Datt 
and others) it was found on the evidence that Saulia Bant had 

18 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( is.) 

been carried out and the'parties were in actual possession accord- 
ingly and this arrangement was upheld by the Commissioner 
(Mr. D.T. Roberts), And in Asaru and others v. Bali Ram and 
others (the record of which has not been found) Sir Henry 
Ramsay quashed a Sautia Bant that had been carrid out by a 
father on the ground that the mere fact of his having two wives 
living in separate houses and at feud with one another did not 
justify him in making a Sawtia Bant. 


14. Inheritance—Sons—Jethon.—The custom of jethon, the 
extra portion of the first-born son (jetha larka)is mentioned by 
Mayne as an obsolete custom, The eldest son was “originally 
entitled to a special share on partition, either a tenth or a 
twentieth in excess of the others, or some special chattel, or an 
extra portion of the flocks.” This custom is not infrequently 
alleged in Kumaun but is seldom established. 


In an examination of the memorandum of village customs in 
Garhwal however it was found that 10 villages out of 44 taken at 
random recorded the custom as prevailing, while an eleventh 
noted it as sometimes followed, Ina sample memorandum of a 
Naini Tal village it was stated that “sometimes fields, some- 
times moveables are given by way of jethon.” 


Cases are occasionally met with where inequalities between 
the shares of brothers are explained by this custom having been 
put into force, but there does not appear to be any fixed or 
recognised principle determining the extent of the extra share, 
and from the rarity of cases on the point ib would seem to be a 


subject usually dealt with by family agreement or the father’s 
decision, when he makes a partition. 


In the only ruling forthcoming on the subject (Jhagar Singh 
y. Ishri Singh), the Commissioner ruled that “tho claim of 
jethon must be incontestably proved to be a custom of the 
individual family concerned,” and disallowed a jethon share, 
_which the lower court had decreed on an arbitration award. 


In the Kangra hills the custom is said to be general; a field, 
a cow, or ox, or other valuable thing is given to the eldest son 
as jethunda ; among the Khatris of Multan the eldesb son gets - 
an extra one-fourth, and in Ambala the oldest son inherits 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 19 ) 


something extra as jathkwn (Tupper, volume II, pages 182 
and 269 ; volume III, page 41). j 

15. Inheritance—Daughters.—By the ordinary rules of 
Hindu Law the daughter comes next to the widow in order of 
inheritance, but, as noted by Mayne, this rule is subject to local 
customs excluding the daughter from inheritance. Such a local 
custom has been universally followed in the Kumaun hills by all 
classes, the daughter and her son not being considered as heirs 
at all, All local opinion confirms the existence of this custom, 
though two or three gentlemen consider that in view of modern 
decisions recognizing the daughter’s rights it would now bo 
difficult to establish the custom and the ordinary rule should be 
introduced. No rulings have however been discovered laying 
down definitely the right of daughters in general to inherit, 
though in two recent cases (Dulab Singh and another v. Musam- 
mat Ganga and another and Budh Singh v. Musammat Rangi) 
it was held that in the case of pure Rajput castes the normal rule 
of Mitakshara Law must be followed, unless the parties opposing 
the daughter’s claim could establish a local eustom excluding 
them. Mr, Pauw in his Garhwal Settlement Report (1896) 
remarks: “The daughter’s position in the hills is much weaker 
than under ordinary Hindu Law. Though decisions such as 
Bahadur Singh and others of Mathana, Ringwarsywn v. Prasadi, 
28th August, 1885 (Mr. Ross, Commissioner) and Kura of Talla 
Kolu, Khatlt v. Lalu, 3rd May, 1892 (Mr. Roberts, Commissioner) 
have declared the daughters entitled to succeed in preference to 
unrelated co-sharers of the village and distant relatives, still her 
right is not generally recognized by the people themselves.” 

The record of the first of the two rulings mentioned is not 
discoverable. The second [Lalu v. Kura (q.v.)] does not lay down 
any definite rule regarding the daughtex’s inheritance ; ib is true 
that the Commissioner remarked that it was for Lalu to prove that 
he was heir by Hindu Law, but he went to say that as Lalu proved 
nothing, except that he was of the same caste as the deceased 
Lalmani, he had no claim and it was unnecessary to decide the 
general question as to under what circumstances and with what 
limitations the daughter was entitled to inherit. ‘There are no 
rulings forthcoming which discuss the goneral local custom at all 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 20 ) 


In Jharw v. Nathu and others two legitimate’ daughters 
obtained a decree for a house of their father’s against their illegi- 
timate brother, but no plea of custom was raised and the father 
had divided up his property in his lifetime. 


On the other hand in Jamnuv. Musammat Manuli and others 
Colonel Erskine ruled without any hesitation that the daughter 
had no claim to inherit her father’s estate as against male 
collaterals (a paternal uncle and first cousins) whatever her 
rights to maintenance might be, The parties were Khasias and 
the ruling is thus not in direct conflict with those of Dulab 
Singh and another v, Musammat Ganga and another and Budh’ 
Singh v. Musammat Rangi, referred to above, in which the 
reason given for presuming the normal rule of Hindu Law to 
apply was that the parties were pure Rajputs, At the same time 
‘no reference to the caste of the parties was made in Jamnw v. 
Musammat Manuli and this ruling represents more correctly the 
old-established custom of the hills. 


Among the Hindus of the Punjab there is a widespread cus- 
tom excluding daughters from inheritance [of. Tupper, volume 
II, pages 145, 177, 184, 209 (100) and 259 ; volume III, page 48]. 


The daughter’s son would naturally be excluded on the same 
principle, but there are no rulings discoverable on the point. - 


As remarked above (paragraph 9) the device of the adopted 
son-in-law (gharjawain) is often resorted to in these hills, as in 
the Punjab, to enable the daughter to succeed. 


16. Inheritance—Lhe village community.—In default of all 
heirs under Hindu Law the remaining co-sharers of the village 
are the ultimate reversioners to the share of a deceased co-sharer, 
which becomes village common land. Tiis is mentioned in the 
memorandum of village customs of Mr. Beckett’s Kumaun 
Settlement (1874) and the right has been recognized in Bach 
Ram v. Chanar Singh and others and in Tila v. Bhagwat Singh 
and others. 

i7. Inheritance—The Naik caste-—The Naiks are a peculiar 
caste, who hold a limited number of villages in various parts of © 
the hills. Thoy do not belong to any of the main classes of 


eee 
2 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


{Ei} 


Hindus in the hills, though they generally observe the customs 
of the Khasia castes. Their female children are invariably (or 
practically invariably) devoted to prostitution as soon as they 
attain the age of puborty and go down to the plains to practise 
their trade. They often return later and marry or form con- 
nections with men of their caste (who also take Khasia wives), 
Being the chief money-makers of their’ caste the females take 
a more prominent position than those of other castes, and as is 
natural in the circumstances rules of descent and legitimacy are 
not too rigidly applied. 


Their customs of inheritance were inquired into at considerable 
length in Lal Singh and another v. Musammat Sundar, where it 
was decided that in this caste sisters succeed to ancestral pro- 
porty and inherit to brothers in default of male descendants. In 
Diwanu v. Lungi it was laid down that in a community of Naiks 
it would be superfluous to enter too closely into questions of 
legitimacy ; sons of a man by the same mother should be regard- 
ed as his heirs and as heirs to each other. 


18. Inheritance—Jogis—Priestly rights.—A point on which 
there is perhaps some doubt is the rule of succession in the case 
of the cultivating classes of Jogis and Gusains. Mr. Pauw in his 
Settlement Report says: “ Among the various castes of jogis 
known as giri, puri, nath, bairagi, etc., the succession lies to the 
chela or disciple, not to the son. This is not improbably a rem- 
nant of the time, when this class was celibate, At the present 
time celibacy is seldom observed, while a large number, parti- 
cularly near Srinagar, are mere cultivators and are only to be 
distinguished from others by their orange-coloured dress and 
the custom prevailing amonga some of thom of oaia large 
wooden rings in their ears.” 


The rule of suecession given by Mr. Pauw was approved as a 
presumption, until the contrary should be proved, by the drafting 
committee of 1915 and has been accepted by local opinion gen- 
orally, but one or two gentlemen have expressed doubts of its 
correctness, and think the prosumption should be in favour of the 
sons inheriting. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(22. ) 


As regards succession to cortain priestly rights at Badrinath 
and Kedarnath, the drafting committee of the 1915 Bill framed 
the following clause: ‘ Among the pandas of Badrinath and 
Kedarnath the courts shall presume that the priestly rights 
known as Pandachari Vrithé rights [rights of acting as priests 
to the pilgrims at certain sacred spots] are personal in the 
panda castes, and that daughters married to men of non-panda 
castes do not inherit such rights, except where the daughter’s 
husband in such a case is lawfully adopted as a gharjawain.” 


This is approved as correct by local opinion. (See also paragraph 
27 below.) 


19, Hindu Law—Woman’s estate.—The only peculiar custom 
relating to a woman’s estate appears to be that whereby a widow 
having inherited her widow’s estate forfeits her rights by 
subsequent unchastity, There are no rulings on this point, bub 
itis accepted by local opinion as the universally prevailing custom 
in the hills. Tho declaration of the custom was embodied in the 
1915 draft Bill in the following clause :— 


“ If a widow subsequent to inheriting her widow’s estate 
voluntarily leaves her late husband’s home and goes to live with 
another man, she shall be deemed to have forfeited all interest in 
her late husband’s estate,” 


An essential part of the custom appears to be the restriction 
of its operation to cases (such as that of the dhanti wife, see 
paragraph 7) where the widow abandons her late husband’s house 
and resides with another man. This custom is also common in 
the Punjab (cf. Tupper, volume II, pages 144, 177 and 184; 
volume IIT, page 46) and was embodied in Sir William Rattigan’s 
draft Bill on Customary Law, clause 18. Presumably the elder 
brother’s widow, when taken to wife by the younger brother, 
would be an exception (cf. Sir William Ratiigan’s draft Bill). 


An old-fashioned local custom whereby widows used to apply 
te the District Officer for permission to alienate the estate, or any 
part of it, did not interfere with the rights of the reversioners to 
contest such alienations by suit, even when permission had been 
given, (See Mathura Datt v, Musammat Dharain Sundari and 
another.) Such applications wero not uncommon a fow years ago.

ILI.—PRE-EMPTION. 


20. General.—The custom of pre-emption prevails in Kumaun 
as elsewhere and follows much the same lines, Its origin in 
the hills is uncertain; Mr. Giles, an officer of considerable 
experience, remarked in a remand enquiry in 1890 that the 
custom was unknown in pre-British times and “ was borrowed 
from the Muhammadan Law and introduced into Kumaun by 


_ British officials for political purposes.’ However this may be, 


the custom has been in force for many years and is the source of 
much litigation and annoyance as elsewhere. 


A full statement of the local custom as it has been finally 
developed is recorded in Mr. Pauw’s Garhwal Settlement 
Memorandum of Village Customs (1896), which contains the 
same rules on the subject for all villages. The rules as there 
given are as follows :—. 

(1) The option of purchase mus6 first be given to the 
vendor’s kinsmen and then to other co-sharers of the 
village. If they decline to buy, the vendor may sell 
to an outsider. If ho gives no prior- notice to his kins- 
men and the co-sharers they may claim to pre-empt. 

(2) The nearest relative (within the third degree) of the 
vendor has, if he is a co-sharer, a right of pre-emption 
against any purchaser who is an unrelated or distantly 
related co-sharer. 

(3) If the land is sold to an outsider, then the first right of 
pre-emption lies with relatives within the third degree 
(being co-sharers) and, failing them, with other resident 
co-sharers of the village. 

The custom is not stated in full in the records of the other 


‘districts, bub follows the same rules except that the qualification 


of residence for a claimant (rule 3) is a disputed point. (See 
rulings below.) Local opinion is in favour of abolishing this 
restriction. The general principles of the custom as recorded 
above aro accopted without dispute and there are rulings on most 
points regarding which any doubt might arise. The method of 
reckoning the third degree of relationship ( ‘pusht) has not been 
discussed in any rulings, but this is always calculated by counting

themselves, thus—=

21. Pre-emption—Rulings.—The principles laid down b 
rulings may be summarised as follows :— 

Relative, but not co-sharer.—A near relative of the vendor, 
who is not a co-sharer in the village, has no right of pre-emption 
ab all. (See Parsi Sah v. Bijat Ram and others, which refer 
to other similar rulings and discusses the whole custom.) 


Claims by non-resident co-sharers, near relatives and others.— 
There are two points in connection with the pre-emption custom 
on which there is a conflict of rulings ; one is with regard to the 
qualification of residence in the village for a claimant to pre-emp- 
tion, The circumstances of the various rulings need to be distin- 
guished carefully. The first ruling. (Gajadhar v. Jora and 
others, 1902) laid down that a non-resident co-sharer (who was 
not a near relative of the vendor) had no right of pre-emption 
against an outsider purchaser (not a co-sharer) in Garhwal 
district, following the memorandum of customs. 


In the next ruling (Rabi Datt and another vy. Parjapati, 
1909), it was held that a non-resident co-sharer, who was related 
to the vendor within the third degree, had no right of pre-emption 
against a purchaser who was a resident: co-sharer bub not 
related to the vendor; this referred to ancestral land. This 
ruling was directly reversed by the same Commissioner in Lachi 
Negi v. Joga and another (March, 1913), the circumstances being 
exactly the same ; from the court’s remarks it would also dissent 
from the distinguishable principle laid down in Gajadhar v. Jora 
and others (see above). The judgement is worth perusing. 


The ruling of Lachi Negi v. Jega was again followed in 
Narain Dait v, Bhola Datt and others (Avgust, 1918) and the 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 25 ) 
principle further extended in this case to non-ancestral land. 


This last extension of the principle has how 


ever since been over- 
ruled (see below). ; 


Thus far however we have it laid down as the present custom 
that— 


(a) a non-resident co-sharer in Garhwal has no right of , 
pre-emption (as œ co-sharer) against a purchaser who 
is not a co-sharer in the village and 


(b) arelative of the vendor within the third degree who is 
a co-sharer, but nota resident in the village, has a 
right of pre-emption, as a relative, against a resident 
co-sharer in the village (who is not related or more 
distantly related to the vendor) in respect of ancestral 
land. (A‘near relative who is not a co-sharer in the 
village at all has no right, see Parsi Sah v. Bijai 
Ram and others quoted above.) 


Local opinion is, as mentioned above, againat making resi- 
dence in the villago a necessary qualification for a claimant. 

Non-ancestral land—Claim by near relative.—As mentioned 
above the ruling of Narain Datt v. Bhola Datt and others 
extended the right of a near relative to non-ancestral land. 


This was in conflict with a ruling of Sir Henry Ramsay in 
Bali v. Amlanand (September 1873), where it was held that a 
near relative, as such, has no right of pre-emption in non- 
ancestral land. ‘The record of this case has however been weeded 
or mislaid and cannot now be found. 3 


This latter ruling has however again been re-established in 
Parjapati v. Kirpal Singh and another (1916), which went to 
the High Court on a reference and in which it was held that the 
claimant failed to prove the custom supporting his right. 


A near relative has therefore no special or prior right in 
respect of non-ancestral land. ‘This seems reasonable. 


Waiver of claim and subsequent sale..—-Failure to claim pre- 
emption on the occasion of one sale does nob debar a claim on 
the subsequent salo of the same land (@ajadhar v. Jora and 
others), 

19 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 26 ) 


Claim by co-sharer by purchase.—A co-sharer, who has him- 
self become a co-sharer by purchase, has the same right of pre- 
emption as any other co-sharer (Moti v. Lal Singh and another). 


Principal (asl) village and hamlet (laga).—For the purposes 
of pre-emption the principal (asi) village and its subsidiary 
villages (lagas) areone, A co-sharer in alaga has no right of 
pre-emption against a purchaser who is already a co-sharer in 
the principal village, but not in the laga (Datt Ram v. Raghu- 
mathu and others). 

Buildings.— There is no custom of pre emption in respect of 
buildings apart from land (Jai Kishan and others v. Mahesh- 
anand and another). Š 


Tenants and pre-emption.—Khaikars [who are either (a) 
under-proprietors or (b) occupancy tenants, see paragraph 5] have 
no locus standi in respect of pre-emption. If a khaikar pur- 
chases the proprietary right over his own land, a co-sharer of the 
village has a right of pre-emption against him Narain Singh and 
another v. Ohanar Singh and Pan Singh v. Dan Singh). 


Contractual stipulation overrides pre-emptive right.—W here 
land is sold, subject to a condition for re-purchase in the event- 
of a re-sale, such condition overrides any customary right of pre- 
emption (Joga and another v. Amba Datt and another). 


Town lots in Almora and Muhammadans.—Lhe custom of 
pre-emption extends to town lots in Almora ; pre-emption can be 
claimed by a joint co-sharer with the vendor. Almora Muham- 
madans do not observe the legal forms of the Shara Muham- 
madi, but follow the local rules applicable to Hindus. Among 
local Muhammadans a claim by a near relative (not a co-sharer) 
might also be entertuined (Roshan Din and Ali Bakhsh v. 
Mukim and Lachmi Ballabh). (See however the Deputy Com- 
missioner’s remand report in this case regarding local custom.) 


Villager removing to new site,— Whore a village was acquired 
by Government and the villagers emigrated en masse to a block 
of land | granted by Government and founded a new village there, 
it was held that thoy carried with them the customs of pre-emp- 
tion applicable to the old village (Narotam and another v. 
Rochfort and others).

(Pai) 


4 


IV.—MISOELLANEOVS. 


22. Village community—Common lands.—In the village 
communities of the hills with their land-holding system resemb- 
ling the imperfect pattidari of the plains, there is usually an 
area of common measured land (gaon sanjait) owned jointly by 
all the co-sharers. It is often waste pasture land, sometimes let 
out to tenants and sometimes in part cultivated by some of the 
co-sharers. It is managod by the headman (malguzar or padhan 
of the village, subject to the approval vf his actions by the 
other co-sharers, who have their remedy by suit if he mismanages 
ib or does any act prejudicial to the common interest, The 
common land sometimes receives accessions of area from _heirless 
holdings reverting to the coparcenary body as the ultimate 
reversioners (see paragraph 16). i 

As regards dealings with the common land, if ib is unculbi= 
vated waste, the consent of all the co-sharersis necessary before 
it can be brought under cultivation (so long asit remains com- 
mon property) ; see the Board’s order in Jasodhar and others 
v. Kamli and others, and the Garhwal Settlement agreement of 
1872, “ (5) If we wish to cultivate the undivided land of 
the village, we shall by common consent of all the hissadars 
divide it.’ Where this is not agreed to the party wishing 
to cultivate must get their proportionate share separated by 
partition (see ruling quoted above). A co-sharer may in certain 
circumstances lose his right to a share in the common land 
(Swaru and another v. Bhawanw ) ; but this is unusual. 

Partition—Mawart Bant.—Rights on partition of the 
common land are normally proportionate to each co-sharer’s share 
of the village land revenue. In some villages however the pecu- 
liar custom known as Mawari Bant or partition by families 
prevails, In this case oach “ family ” of the village community 
takes an equal share irrespective of the area and revenue of their 
respective holdingr. Lhe custom, if it prevails, should be found 
noted in the village settlement papers, and the common land in 
such cases is sometimes recorded as gaon sanjait mawari bant, 
The “ families ” will also often be found recorded in the soettle- 

ment papers, otherwise itmay be difficult to detormine tho original 
“ familios ” among the changing shares a good many years later. 


` 
á 


x 


- 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 28 ) 


An instance of this custom being enforced against the wishes 
of the majority of the co-sharers will be found in Dalip Singh 
and others v. Ram Singh and others. 


It goes without saying that no headman or co-sharer can 
alienate or create any interest in any specific area of the com- 
mon land without the consent of all the co-sharercs (cf. Durga 
and others v. Jai Singh and another). 


Padhanchari land.—Padhanchari land (sec paragraph 5)is the 
property of the State and the padhan (or his representative, 
where he does not live in the village) is only a rent-free tenant 
in ib. 

No one can acquire rights in it adverse to the padhan, and 
though a padhan can put in tenants or lessess for his lifetime, 
each successive padhan must succeed to it unencumbered (Bachua 
v. Ramua and others). Similarly, in Nand Lal v. Musammat 


Dharm Sundari and others (S. C. A. no. 4 of 1895) (not ə 


reproduced) Colonel Grigg, Commissioner, on the 13¢'1/14th May, 
1895, rejected the claim of the family which had held the padhan- 
ship for a long time and lost it, to proprietary right in the 
padhanchari land, holding that whatever the nature of their 
tenure might once have been, the land was now only ‘‘ padban- 
chari—the allowance in land granted to the padhan.” 


23. Unmeasured land—Extensions of cultivation.—As ox- 
plained in paragraph 5 all land in the hills, other than that measured 
and recorded at settlement at private property, is the property 
of the. State. ‘The villagers are however executively permitted 
under certain restrictions to extend their cultivation into adjoin- 
_ ing unmeasured (benap) waste, Apart from this, blooks of land 
separate from the old cultivation may be formally granted in 
proprietary right as nayabad grants, a summary settlement being 
made with the grantee, and a map and record-of-rights prepared. 
Tn such nayabad grants the grantee becomes an ordinary pro- 
prietor, subject to any special conditions of the grant and 
the ordinary rules of law and custom will apply. It is 
open to any one claiming a share and possession in such a grant 
to bring a suib for that purpose, the grant not being conclusive 
inter partes [Bachi and others v. Chanar Singh by Colonel Grigg, 


ne) 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 29 ) 


Commissioner, 9th March, 1899 (not reproduced) following 
Indian Law Reports, volume, VIII, Allahabad, page 649.] 

Cases relating to extensions of old cultivation into adjoining 
benap land are being reserved to the revenue courts. There are 
frequent disputes over these between rival claimants or relating 
to customary rights of wav, pasturage, etc. 

The question of jurisdiction will however be complicated in 
practice by the hillman’s habit of dealing with his extensions 
of cultivation (to which he has by custom a good title as against 
other cultivators, though not against the State) jointly with his 
measured proprietary land. Thus a man will sell or mortgage 
his 2 acres of measured land in a village, together with his 
benap cultivation fora lump sum by one instrument, Disputes 
about such transactions or cases regarding succession to, or posses- 
sion of, estates comprising benap cultivation as well as measured 
land must cause some confusion between the two classes of courts. 

Such cases however should not present much difficulty. The 
unmeasured land belongs to the man who reclaims it, and is 
subject to the same customary rights and rules as any other 
property. Any disputes specifically relating to the extension 
into benap will go before the revenue courts. In Jaz Kishan 
and others v. Kirpal Datt, a ruling relating to one aspect of ; 
such extensions have been reproduced. 

24. Water rights.—As the measured area in the hills is 
confined to cultivated, or occasionally, culturable land, the 
beds of rivers, streams and lakes will be found to be unmeasured 
and all such waters are recognized as the property of the State. 
There is thus no system of riparian proprietorship and private 
rights in water, such as prevails elsowhore. The waters of hill 
streams are commonly utilized for water mills, which are assessed 
to milf rents by Government as a charge for the use of the 
water, and for irrigation. Sanction to the establishment of a 
water mill and the assessment of such new mills to mill rent are 
matters dealb with exccutively. Once sanctioned a water mill 
becomes ordinary property of tho owner and his heirs or transferees. . 

The only special local custom relating to disputes about 
water rights is that of prior user, which is a consequence of the 
conditions of State control and the absonco of private ownership, 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 80 ) 


By this custom any man who ut'lizes the water of a stream 
till then running to waste, acquires a right to tho amount of water 
actually needed for his reasonable requirements as against any 
later comer, who may wish to divert the supply for his own purpo- 
ses. Theright of the prior user, in case a dispute arises, is 
limited to the actual requirements of his operations up to that 
time, Thus aman who has constructed an irrigation channel 
and has been irrigating 5 acres of land, cannot claim more water 
than is necessary for that area on the pretext that he intended 
to extend his irrigation to 10 acres later on. : 

The custom is well set forth and earlier rulings summarised 
in Baga and others v. Ram Singh and others. 

Thokdari dues.—Thokdars in the hills were originally farm- 
ers of the revenue and usually landowners of ‘leading families. 
They are now hereditary police officials of practically nominal 
functions and remunerated by dues consisting of a percentage 


on the revenue of the villages comprised in thoir thokdari, 


They are appointed and can be dismissed by the Commissioner, 
The only cases relating to them which come before the civil 
courts are concerned with their dues. : 

These dues are a salary or remuneration for their office and 
not private property ; hence they cannot be alienated nor can 
shares be claimed in them. In Guman Singh v. Kushal Singh 
ip was held that a thokdar can bind himself to pay his dues to 
another person, but he cannot alienate them or bind his suc- 
cessor, Similarly, where a thokdar Daulat Singh, had mortgaged 
his dues, Rs, 62 per annum, in 1880 to one Sheo Ram as 
interest on a loan of Rs. 580 and this came to notice in 1898, 
the Commissioner dismissed Daulat Singh and abolished the 
thokdarship. The Board of Revenue (Messrs, Moule and 
Evans) wrote: “ The duties performed by a thokdar may not 
be of much account, still it is clearly a service tenure and the 
rights and duties are personal to the thokdar and cannot be 
transferred by sale or mortgage to another.” (Order dated 


October 14, 1898.) They placed Daulat Singh under suspen- — 


sion, giving him a year to redeem the mortgage, 
26. Temple lands.—There are numerous villages in the hills 
assignod free of revenue to various temples ; these vill ages and 


TONET aa 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


(Sig) 


their lands are known as gunth. .'The assignment only includes 
the measured land and not the unmeasured waste and jungle, 
which remains the property of the State as elsewhere. This has 
been laid down on various occasions, and since all unmeasured 
waste lands in the hills were declared Government protected 
forests in 1898, the point can no longer be contested, 

The various rights in these gunth lands require to be distin- 
guished with some care. There is, firstly, the temple, acting 
through its chief priest or manager (rawal, mahant, sirgiroh, 
pir or daroga) and standing in the place of Government as 
overlord of the villagers and assignee of the.revenue or nominal 
revenue. ‘The interest of the temple cannot be alienated by any 
one (cf. Durga Singh v. Salig Ram). 

Secondly, under the temple there are proprietors (hissadars) 
as in an ordinary village under Government. These hissadars 
are of two distinct classes. i 

The one class consists of cultivators who pay the assigned 
revenue or other dues, in cash`or kind, to the temple. 

The second class consists of priests, servants or other funo- 
tionaries of the temple, who hold the land revenue-free in return 
for services rendered by them to the temple, 

The various cases which occur in respect of these lands relate 
to the alienability or otherwise of these two classes of tenures. 
They obviously stand on very different footings. 

In the first case ij makes nodifference to the temple who 
pays in the cash or other dues leviable from the cultivator. The 
latter can in such villages undoubtedly alienate his land ; these 
tenures are much the more common, 

But in thesecond case, while a priest or functionary can eke 
out a subsistence on the produce of his two or three acres of 
cultivation, he could not live on the two or three rupees of reve- 
nue which at the usual hill rates the temple would receive from 

an Outsider, if the land were sold to such a purchaser, nor could 
the temple maintain a priest or servant on the income from tho 


. land. If therefore a priest or other holder of a service tenure 


could alienate his holding the worship of the temple would cease 
and the object of the endowment would be defeated ; there would 
be a perversion of the endowment. 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


( 32 ) 


This principle is clearly applicable to sales of such holdings 
_ to outsiders, who cannot render the services on the performance 
of which the tenure depends, e.g. a salo by a Brahman priest to 
a Mubhammadan or dom. Whether it would apply to a transfer 
to a purchaser who could equally well render the required ser- 
vice is a more debatable point, 


There are various rulings on the subject of these service 
tenures. Unfortunately the records of the two oldest cannot 
be traced. They were evidently before Mr. Pauw who quotes 
them in his Settlement Report, 1896, and his extracts from them 
may be reproduced, 


In Kundan Lal Sah v. Panua, gunth land held on a service 
tenure was attached in execution of a decree. The Senior Assist- 
ant Commissioner, Colonel Garstin, referred the case to Sir 
Henry Ramsay with the following report: “In my opinion 
where gwnth land is in the possession of the pujaris, in return 
for which they are required to perform service in the temple, 
the land in their oe cannot be attached in satisfaction 
of their private debts. For,if it is auctioned, this service in the 
temple will fall on the purchaser and this cannot be performed 
by every caste... In villages in which the possession of 
the gunth land is not with the temple servants the temple 
authorities do not appear entitled to raise any objection to at- 
tachment.” Sir Henry Ramsay passed the following order on 
this reference (13th June, 1878): “The opinion of the Senior 
Assistant Commissioner is correct. Gunth lands should not be 
attached in satisfaction of a private debt.” 


Again, in Gulab Singh v. Ram Datt, Mr. Giles, Senior 
Assistant Commissioner, wrote: “ There are two kinds of gunth 
lands. , Of one kind the pujari of the temple receives the profits 
as payment for his sorvices to the temple, With such land a 
decree-holder against the pujari has, I conceive, no right of 
interference. But with respect to the other kind of gunth land, 
the temple stands in the place of Government with regard to 
ordinary revenue-paying land, Its assessment was fixed at 
_ Settlement and the temple authorities have no power to alber it ; 
nor 80 long as the revenue-payer meets the demand, have they 


Aa 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


Gach) 


any power of interference with him.” This view was upheld on 
appeal by Colonel Erskine, Commissioner (December 18, 1890), 

The third ruling in which a sale of such service-tenure land 
by apriest to an outsider was cancelled ona suit by the chief 
priest of the templo is Padamgir v. Dulanath and another (q.v.). 

There are two later rulings which are often quoted as revers- 
ing the above decisions. These aro Amba Datt and another v. 
Daulat Singh and Kuna Sah v. Premua Rawal, and they are 
both reproduced in this volume. A perusal of these cases will 
however show that they do not reverse the principal laid down 
above. In the latter case the Commissioner clearly did not 
understand the nature of the tenure, while the former decision 
could at most beheld to approve such transfers in cases where 
the purchaser was equally competent to render the services 
required from the landholder. 

The three earlier decisions appear undoubtedly to lay down 
the correct principle for such cases. 

27. Priestly vrights.In view of tho numerous temples and 
places of pilgrimage in the hills, isis natural that a good many 
disputes should occur with regard to succession to temple offices 
and priestly rights at places of pilgrimage. These however 
do not as a rule depend on principles of local custom, 
but have to be decided on the facts of the particular case, 
The succession to the rawalship of the two great temples of 
Badrinath and Kedarnath has been settled by schemes of 
Management. An instance of a claim to exclude outsiders 
from admission to their body and to set up a monopoly of 
the right to collect offerings at a sacred spot, with an illustration 
of the usual issues in such cases will be found in the case of the 
panch pandas of Badrinath (Musammat Sarda and another 
v. Lajja Ram and others). See also paragraph 8 with regard 
to the succession to priestly rights. There have been many dis- 
putes between the rawal (or chief priest) of Kedarnath and the 
pandas of Kedarnath, a numerous and well-to-do body of (nomi- 
nally) “eight sayanas” [or headmen] “ and eighteen scores of 
pandas.” Jn the most important case Rawal Ganesh Ling v. 
The pandas of Kedarnath (q. v.) tho lattor vindicated their right 
as spiritual guides of the pilgrims, to accompany them into the 

20 


Digitization by Arya Samaj Charitable Foundation, Chennai 


CB 
temple and receive and retain gifts there, A similar case 
between the same parties (S. C. A. 15 of 1905, not reproduced) 
established their right in the same way to receive gifts at Gupta- 
kashi, Whileina third case (S, O. A. 8 of 1907) The Pandas 


v. Rawal Ganesh Ling and others (nob reproduced) the pandas. 


failed to establish the exclusive right of their body to the posts 
of Samalia and Bhandari in the temple. 

Most of these cases do not involve any special principles of 
law or local custom. Hach temple has its own special usages. 
which have to be decided by, the evidence in each case.
